## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc. Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the proposal. The information contained in the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.

The total length of the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service time standard.

The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and Programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneously with the submission to the Board subject to the confidentiality requirements of the proposal pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The water cycle is one of the most complex and often overlooked parts of the global climate crisis. The feedback between land use and water, as water can become a substantial carbon emitter when managed improperly, with methane emissions from untreated waste and wastewater accounting for 7% of global greenhouse gas emissions, while riverine, marine, and coastal ecosystems have the potential to act as a powerful sink for CO2. The current state of the world's freshwater resources is unsustainable, and the water cycle and the climate is threatening many livelihoods, as growing economies and populations are reducing access to clean water and sanitation. According to the World Health Organization estimates that there already are 750 million people with no access to clean water, and 2.5 billion people lacking access to proper sanitation. Even the oceans, which cover 71% of the Earth's surface, are under threat from rising sea levels, increased salinity, increasing temperatures, decreasing biodiversity, and rising sea levels. At current rates, by 2050 there will be a great increase volume of plastic in the oceans than all marine life combined.

There is a clear need for climate-resilient infrastructure to mitigate the risks described above. This requires significant investment in water infrastructure, as inadequate water and sanitation infrastructure are estimated to be related to 80% of all illnesses in the developing world. In total, the World Bank estimates that $1 trillion will be needed by 2030 for climate-resilient infrastructure development in countries in Asia at USD 4.4 billion and developing countries in Africa at USD 16 billion. Climate finance is responding; however, investment requirements far exceed the current level of financing.

Clima investor Tier ("C2") has the mission to develop and construct infrastructure projects in developing countries that are climate-resilient and sustainable while also addressing the long-term consequences of climate change by ameliorating GHG emissions and by increasing resilience. To do so, C2 will deploy an innovative whole-of-life financing approach utilizing two independent but operationally interlinked funds: (i) the Development Fund ("DF") provides development loans to early-stage projects and their ecosystems, whereas the Climate Finance Facility ("CFF") is a separate fund that provides grants to projects. The CFF provides loans to projects in developing countries at USD 145 million; the prospective fund sizes are estimated at a USD 100 million DF2 and a USD 1 billion CFF2 (of which 80% would be eligible for CFF proceeds – as USD 80 million). The CFF will provide grants to projects in developing countries that have been identified to provide scarce, highly additional donor capital to the structure which is required to crowd in further private sector participation.

C2 will invest climate adaptation benefits in the water sector by building infrastructure that sources, transports, and treats the water necessary for both municipal and industrial users to be shielded from the consequences of climate change. In the sanitation sector, C2 will realize mitigation and adaptation benefits through projects that reduce methane emissions from wastewater treatment plants and reuse water re-use. Finally, in the ocean sector, C2 will invest in physical infrastructures such as environmental services for ports or shipping initiatives that ameliorate GHG emissions. Additionally, C2 will pursue investments in coastal infrastructure to protect against climate change impacts on coastlines (including coastal, and marine ecosystems hereafter; "ecosystems"). Ecosystems are an important part of nature's defences against climate change and an important part of people's livelihoods. They are currently

under stark threat facing ecological degradation at a rate ten to hundreds of times higher than the average over the past 10 million years, and with more than 50% of natural ecosystems at risk of being lost".

Through investments in nature-based solutions, CIZ targets cross-cutting climate change mitigation and adaptation benefits by enhancing the natural ability of restored ecosystems to sequester carbon and foster ecosystem-based adaptation.

By developing and constructing additional water supply, treatment, distribution, and waste-water treatment systems, as well as physical infrastructure such as roads and bridges, CIZ will have a quantifiable and measurable social and environmental impact in developing countries. CIZ aims to construct up to USD 2.96 billion in infrastructure projects in developing countries (core indicator 3). The primary objective of the project is to provide access to clean water for at least 10 million people, round-the-clock access to reliable and safe water supply despite climate shocks and stresses and protected and strengthened ecosystems (core indicator 2). In addition, the coverage of ecosystems protected and strengthened by CIZ will increase from an estimated 3.5% in 2018 to approximately 47% by 2030 (primary indicator 4.1). With regards to mitigation benefits, CIZ will reduce or avoid ~9.6 million tCO2e per year for a period of at least 20 years (core indicator 5) and increase the resilience of communities (core indicator 1). CIZ will create additional opportunities for scalability and replicability beyond its project targets through programs shift described in more detail in section D2. The specific climate vulnerabilities within the countries eligible for OCF funding through CIZ are described in Annex 7.

FMO, the Dutch Development Bank, will act as Accredited Entity for this proposal and as an Executing Entity for the first two projects under Climate Investor Two (CIT2). FMO is a wholly owned joint venture between FMO and Sanlam, will act as the Fund Manager of Climate Investor Two, and is the first Executing Entity. The DFJ and the CEFZ, the two Funds within Climate Investor Two, will act as the second and third Executing Entities.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### The climate crisis

Globally, the seven hottest years in recorded history have been the six years from 2014 to 2020*, driven by increasing concentrations of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere. Simultaneously atmospheric carbon reached a corresponding record high in 2020, driven primarily by anthropogenic use of fossil fuels*. As of 2020, global average temperatures are 1.1°C higher than pre-industrial temperatures, the effect of which is already being felt across the globe. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) projects that a 1°C increase in temperature, a developing nation can expect a decrease in the gross domestic product (GDP) by up to 3%. A 2°C increase could lead to an average GDP loss of around 5% globally. Higher temperatures, combined with the vulnerability of developing nations to these temperatures highlights the critical need for climate finance to be invested in projects and programs which both decrease greenhouse gas emissions and build resilience against the impacts of climate change.

Climate finance has primarily invested in climate mitigation projects, receiving an average of USD $37 billion per year from 2015-2018. In contrast, adaptation projects have received only USD $9 billion annually during this period. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has primarily invested in climate mitigation projects, receiving an average of USD $37 billion per year from 2015-2018. In contrast, adaptation projects have received only USD $9 billion annually during this period.

#### The importance of the water cycle in the climate crisis

Water is a critical component for human life and economic development and can mitigate and respond to the climate crisis. Water is paramount to all life on earth, and any imbalance in its cycle or supply chain can quickly become detrimental to the global climate and the many livelihoods that depend upon it. Imbalances are created by climate change, which leads to more frequent and severe droughts and floods; a lack of investment in climate change, and a growing lack of resilient infrastructure. An example of how these imbalances have serious consequences for many is shown by the grim observation that over than 80% of illnesses in the developing world are water-related.

C2 aims to develop and construct the infrastructure that's required to future-proof parts of the water cycle. The investment thesis differentiates between three different sectors: (i) "the water sector", (ii) the sanitation sector, and (iii) the urban water sector. The water sector includes all activities related to water that source, transport, and treat water to and from municipalities and sectors. The sanitation sector looks at treating waste and wastewater to avoid and sequester methane emissions while providing clean sanitation services for communities. The urban water sector is focused on providing reliable and affordable environmental services to ports and harbors and (2) protect, strengthen, and restore important ecosystems.

#### The water sectors

Water is fundamental to human life and economic development but is also the medium through which the impacts of climate change will be felt on earth, with a warming climate causing increasingly variable and unpredictable rainfall. Changing climate patterns, together with growing economies and populations could lead to an additional 500 million people being exposed to water stress by 2040, living over 3 billion of national GDP at risk*. UNESCOS estimates that over 7 in 20 people live in areas subject to water stress. A study done by the WHO and UNESCO in 2017 shows that globally more than 80% of illnesses are water-related. Women and girls, who make up a majority of the population do not have immediate access to safe water to drink. Women are often more negatively impacted as they are responsible for collecting the water within the household. These responsibilities reduce the likelihood of women receiving education. One of the objectives of C2 in the water sector is to realize

The oceans provide half of the Earth’s oxygen, feed three billion people, and have absorbed an estimated 93% of the added heat arising from human-driven emissions to the atmosphere. In economic terms, the annual value of goods and services provided by the ocean is estimated at USD 2.5 trillion, making it the world's seventh-largest economy, after the United Kingdom®.showing its importance for food security, livelihoods, and climate regulation.

The oceans are also a major sink for carbon dioxide, absorbing about one-third of all human-caused CO₂ emissions annually. However, this absorption is causing the melting of inland glaciers and ice, causing rising sea levels with significant impacts on shorelines (coastal erosion, saltwater intrusion into freshwater aquifers). By 2100, sea level could rise by up to two meters by the end of the century, putting shorelines, but also local water and sanitation infrastructure projects, further at risk®. Oceans are under further severe pressure due to uncontrolled pollution, often causing eutrophication and dead zones (wastewater being dumped in the world’s rivers and shores, and resource overexploitation.

In addition to reducing ocean pollution through the implementation of national policies, climate change will also impact coastal areas, ports, ships, and harbors that contributes to improved ocean health (by reducing greenhouse gas emissions). In addition, it will pursue investments and ecosystem-based approaches to protect, preserve and/or restore rivers, coastline, marine, and wetland ecosystems. Financing ecosystems has traditionally been seen as net-cost projects by

### B2 (a): Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

Climate Investor Two aims to create a paradigm shift by making investments which result in the scaling up of climate-resilient water supply and sanitation infrastructure development that meets the mitigation needs of developing countries. If it succeeds in this, low-emission, climate-resilient sustainable development will be catalyzed in the programme's host countries because successful climate investments in the target sectors with a risk-adjusted return, will be demonstrated to commercial


---
**Page 13**
---

invests in, this will foster significant contributions to blue and green climate finance in emerging markets, bringing countries closer to meeting their INDCs, and the global targets of the SDOs.

To derive above, CIZ’s theory of change relies on a number of reasonable assumptions. First, it is assumed that there will be sufficient expertise provided by project co-developers and co-investors for project implementation. Second, it is assumed that there will be adequate access to financial services technical, legal and ESG expertise, which is applied to projects accordingly in order to complement internal capacity. Third, it is assumed that the availability of a combination of public and private sector service contracts enable the financial sustainability of projects. This condition is considered and assessed prior to and during the development phase of a CIZ project. Third, it is assumed that targeted expertise will be available for project implementation in terms of technical, legal and ESG factors. CFMs and ESG and technical teams scrutinize different aspects of a potential project to evaluate and consider its long-term feasibility. Fourth, it is assumed that the projects will generate a material development impact on the communities they serve. Fifth, it is assumed that there are no significant political factors. As the case of assumption number one, this is supported by CFMs own in house expertise, which will do due diligence to ensure that the project is not politically sensitive. Sixth, it is assumed that PPG regulation clearly defines a process for private sector participation. Seventh, it is assumed that wastewater quality standards are effectively enforced in the beneficiary countries. As the regulatory environment is complex and subject to change, this assumption is bolstered by the fact that the threats posed by climate change, this assumption is likely to develop in CIZ’s favor over the 20-year implementation period. Similarly, the increased need to address infrastructure deficits support assumptions seven and eight. Eighth, it is assumed there will be a stable political environment in operating geographies. While this too is subject to change, the fact that CIZ has been operational for more than five years, and takes measures to minimize associated risks. This can include project structuring approaches, political risk insurance, and also the support of the CIZ investor community which contains a number of influential regional investors who are committed to supporting CIZ’s development and expansion in CIZ’s target sections. This assumption, like assumptions six and seven, is bolstered by the necessity for commercial investment to address the scale of the existing deficits within CIZ’s target sections.

Furthermore, in order to generate a paradigm shift, CIZ’s theory of change identifies and aims to address or mitigate the following risks and barriers:

With respect to climate risk, the CIZ theory of change identifies that climate change, increasing population pressure, and economic development are all factors which will make infrastructure not built. This will be exacerbated as untreated waste and wastewater will further increase GHG emissions while causing environmental pollution and health hazards. Simultaneously, the exploitation of the coastal and marine ecosystems is likely to continue unabated, with negative consequences for those most dependent on the oceans and their ecosystems by these. While this continues, the issue is compounded by rising sea levels and increasing temperatures which will jeopardize the health of the communities and their ecosystems further. Finally, deterioration in the base habitat of coral reef coastal wetland, and marine ecosystems will reduce nature’s ability to act as a carbon sink, reduce the resilience of coastal communities to climate change, and negatively influence local communities’ livelihoods. When considered together these five individual factors represent a network of related and interdependent drivers of climate risk.

Given CIZ’s planned interventions and the climate risks it intends to address, it is possible to identify a number of barriers and risks to the accomplishment of the paradigm shift it will trigger.


---
**Page 14**
---

Barrier 1 is that the potential long-term benefits of investing in target sector projects may not outweigh the high political risks. This can be linked to Risk 1, that low volumes of private investments are mobilized due to a lack of demonstrating the commercial viability of investing in target sector projects.

The water, sanitation, and ocean sectors have primarily been within the public domain. Traditional project financing models for these sectors have not been successful because they are hindered by both (i) providing private sector investors with a blended finance solution that reduces their risks and (ii) by demonstrating the bankability of projects in the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors.

A lack of low-cost, high-impact solutions further jeopardizes global temperature and the resilience that developing countries have against it.

Barrier 2 is the limited know-how and capacity for the development and construction of complicated and technology-intensive infrastructure projects. There’s often limited capacity for the development and construction of complicated and technology-intensive infrastructure projects. Adequately addressing this will require cooperation between all the developers and third parties.

Barrier 3 is the increasingly limited public sector budgets and commitment for target sector projects in host countries.

The budgets that developing countries have available to invest in the water, sanitation, and ocean sectors are often insufficient to meet the scale of investment required. This is especially true when considering the investments that would be needed to stay within the 1.5-degree pathway while ensuring resilient water supply will require the mobilization of private sector finance.

Climate Investment Funds (CIFs) can help address and mitigate these risks by:

(i) establishing and managing Climate Investment Two (including the Development – and the Construction Equity Fund) and

(ii) development and implementation of mitigation and adaptation projects in developing countries.

The establishment and management of the two different funds, that together provide a whole-of-life solution for water, sanitation, and ocean infrastructure projects in developing countries. These funds will finance such as Climate Investor Two in mobilizing (private sector) investors with different risk to replicate their investments at scale. The CIFs will also support the development of innovative scaling and replication of water, sanitation, and ocean infrastructure projects in developing countries.

Furthermore, showcasing the commercial viability of water, sanitation, and ocean infrastructure projects is a key challenge for these sectors. Climate Investment Two aims to address this by the mobilization and implementation of such projects in the host countries by providing a knowledge of transfer to local parties. CIFs aim to do this by including local partners and developers in both the development as well as the construction phase of each project, and will also include financial intermediaries, including DFIs, to the refinancing of its projects.

Climate Investor Two will work with governments to discuss how to finance development loans from the fund, including feasibility studies, ESFs, agency assessments, stakeholder engagements, potential pilot projects and everything else that’s required before a project can go into its construction phase. Once successfully developed, these projects graduate to the CIFs, which will implement (construct and operate) the projects by providing up to 100% of the equity (label it is

### Project/Programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)
#### Introduction to Climate Investor Two
Climate Investor Two is a blended finance facility mandated to develop and construct projects in the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors in developing countries to contribute to climate mitigation and adaptation benefits. CII2 duplicates the structure of the CII fund that launched in 2017 and received a GCF commitment in 2019 (FP99). CII2, like its predecessor CII1, was designed to address the lack of private financing options in developing countries by providing a whole-of-life project financing solution.

![](image.png)

Development Fund. The Development Fund ("DF" or "DF2") has been set up to provide development loans, accompanied by development support/assistance provided by CFM and 3rd parties, to project owners in the early stages of their projects. This will help to reduce transaction costs, reducing the development timeline and improving the ultimate bankability of infrastructure projects. The DF2 will increase the implementation rate of projects – while demonstrating the commercial viability of investments in water, sanitation, and sectors. CFM ensures that the DF2 is only used for projects where a Donor wants to get involved in the high-impact development phase. Once projects successfully complete their development phase by passing the assessment (including but not limited to gender assessments), the project moves on to the construction phase. The CEFF reimburses the grant to the DF2 and the project moves on to the construction phase. CFM ensures the DF2 is only used for projects where a Donor wants to get involved in the high-impact development phase (CFM expects 2/3 of the projects to graduate). The CEFF reimburses the grant with a premium that compensates for losses made on unsuccessful projects. The premium paid remains subject to the decision taken by the DF2 (as it would equal 50% if the decision has been made up).

## Construction Equity Fund.

The CEF has been set up to provide kick-start projects from their development phase after the "proof of concept", by providing up to 100 percent of the required equity (or a portion thereof) for the project. The CEF will be structured as an investment fund and will include different sorts of equity (ordinary, preferred, shareholder loans) and will not always constitute a majority stake. It aims to further decrease the construction time and costs by: (i) reducing the complexity and number of transactions involved in financing projects; (ii) providing access to larger amounts of capital and thereby reducing the (costly) exposure period on investments; (iii) reducing costs by eliminating certain debt costs specific, (iv) recycling capital from completed projects to new project to reduce the time any funding is required. The CEF will be structured as a multi-tiered investment fund with a 20:40:40 approach to manage three different tiers of investors: (i) Donors in the most junior tranche (representing 20% of the CEF), and (ii) commercial and institutional investors in the senior tier (representing 60% of the CEF); and (iii) commercial and institutional investors in the senior tranche (representing 40% of the CEF). The CEF, like the DFZ, recycles its capital over the 20-year implementation period (two full project cycles), with a target payback by year 15. The 20:40:40 ratios between the three different tiers set and will always be maintained.

CFM, which will be responsible for the sourcing for and execution of projects based within the following sectors:

- Water, including the following sub-sectors:
    * Bulk water supply and storage.

    * Waste water treatment.
    * Water utilities and concessions.
    * Pumping.
    * Household water connections; and
    * Decentralized water treatment and distribution.

- Sanitation (waste and wastewater), including the following sub-sectors:
    * Wastewater collection and sewerage networks.
    * Wastewater treatment facilities, including wastewater to energy production and nutrient extraction.
    * Wastewater recycling facilities.
    * Waste to value facilities, including waste sorting, recycling, composting, and waste to energy production.
    * Methane capture and destruction for waste facilities.

- Oceans, including the following sub-sectors:
    * Sustainable management, protection, and/or restoration of wetland, riverine, coastal, and marine ecosystems.
    * Seaweed aquaculture.
    * Climate-resilient port infrastructure.

##### 2.1.1: Identifying potential investment opportunities and initial screening by CFM IC:
The CFM deal team will do the initial project sourcing. The IC then decides whether (i) the project meets the investment mandate and (ii) whether additional information, restructuring, or due diligence is required before presenting it to the external IC. The GCM IC should be seen as a first “quality control”.

##### 2.1.2: Re-structuring after CFM IC and initial due diligence
The deal team will then work with the project to be considered before the actual Climate Investor Two Development Fund Investment Committee (DFZ IC). After that, the deal team will work to get all the information required to present the impact and business case of the project to the DFZ IC.

During this phase, the deal teams will gather all the information required to present the impact and business case towards the DFZ IC. The deal team will do initial due diligence on the project and the third party (co-)developers and sponsors.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V15.0 | PAGE 19 OF 63

## Activity 2.1.3: Project approval by DFZ IC:

The deal team presents the case to the DFZ IC after doing the initial due diligence and completing the data collection requested by the GCF IC. The DFZ IC will judge proposals, among other indicators on the adaptation and mitigation impact they provide for their host countries. For adaptation, the deal teams will provide them with historical climate trends (trends observed changes or variability in changes in temperature, precipitation, wind speed, etc.) and projected future climate trends (how these changes will hurt communities in the project area). Adaptation benefits will be further addressed by explaining how the project company's products, services, or technology will help the community adapt to the changing climate. For mitigation, the deal team will explain why the project does not lead to maladaptation, an explanation of the Rho Market score for climate adaptation (to be achieved by the company) and an overview of the impact of the project on the carbon balance in terms of avoided emissions and/or sequestration of CO2. To assess mitigation benefits, DFZ deal teams will explain how the project company's products, services, or technology will lead to a reduction in greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and/or increase in a carbon Score that is to be achieved by the project company and a description of the GHG accounting methodology to be used to validate the climate mitigation impact realized by the company's services, products, or technologies (including the baseline, mid-term, and final projections).

During the DFZ IC approval process, the deal team will also provide a more detailed independent assessment and/or mitigation assessment based on the criteria set out above. This detailed independent assessment will be carried out as part of the more detailed due diligence that's conducted during the development phase (Activity 2.1.4) with the support of an Environmental Social Assessment (ESIA) by Lord Z Investment Committee (CEFZ IC) (Activity 2.2). This independent assessment will form part of the materials that the CEFZ IC receives and bases its approval on.

It is only after approved by the DFZ IC (including, for the avoidance of doubt, the statutory initial climate impact assessment) that a project would receive funding from the DFZ IC to move into the next stage of detailed due diligence and project development. GCF proceeds will only be released when the deal team has demonstrated that the project is not likely to have a significant contribution to the envisioned climate impact. Likewise, the proceeds from divestments will run through the CEFZ's waterfall on per project basis, ensuring that the GCF proceeds are not commingled with other funds.

## Activity 2.1.4: Project development and extensive due diligence (including but not limited to stakeholder engagement, gender assessment, and Environmental and Social Impact Assessment):

The actual development phase starts after the approval of the DFZ IC. During this phase, CFIM will conduct, supported by independent parties, extensive due diligence. This due diligence included, but was not limited to: a detailed climate impact assessment (CIA), an environmental and social impact assessments (ESIA). The ESIA will then judge whether the projects have any resettlement risk (which is not expected to happen often) after which a Resettlement Action Plan (RAP) would be drafted. This phase also includes extensive stakeholder engagement, gender assessment, and other impacts are selected. This phase needs to be completed on all important aspects before the construction phase can start.

## Activity 2.2: Implementation (construction and operation of mitigation and adaptation projects for water, sanitation, and ocean sectors in developing countries):


---
**Page 23**
---

Activity 2.3.1: Project approval by CEFIC IC

Upon completion of the development case, the deal team re-proposes the project to the CEFIC IC. This proposal will include all the findings off the due diligence and evaluations done in Activity 2.1.4. It is only after approval of the CEFIC IC (including, for the avoidance of doubt, the submission of a NOC) that the project can be considered as having received funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). GCF proceeds will only be used for projects in countries for which an NOC has been obtained and to projects which contribute to the emission reduction targets. The CEFIC IC will review all projects on a case-by-case basis, and will run through the GCF’s waterfall on a project basis, ensuring that the GCF proceeds are not commingled with those from projects in countries for which no NOC has been obtained.

Activity 2.3.2: Project construction

The CEFIC will then repay the development loan from the DFU including a premium to ensure full repayment of the loan and equity used to start a project’s construction phase after the successful approval of the CEFIC IC. During the construction phase, CFM will actively manage and oversee the project, in example by nominating directors or representatives on the boards or sub-committees of each investment entity, ensuring environmental sustainability (ESG) compliance, and maximum value creation.

Activity 2.3.3: Project refinancing at end-of-life

After successful construction, the project moves to the operational phase. Once a project is stable in terms of operations, impact, and cash flows, a process will commence procuring debt at the project level and, a few years later, the project exit will be arranged following approval from the CEFIC IC through its waterfall. The proceeds will then go towards funding to other projects.

CFM is organized along regional and functional lines which combine: (i) a strong investment and operational focus with industry and local expertise and (ii) solid middle and back-office skills in operations, risk management, strategy (iii) sound risk management, coordinated legal / tax structuring, and robust compliance functionality.

CFM will operate from its offices in The Hague, the Netherlands (head office), Cape Town, Singapore, and Bogota (regional offices) as follows:

### Head office

CFM’s head office is located in The Hague, Netherlands, and was established in 2015. At present the office has a total of 34 full-time employees. The key responsibilities at the head office for CFM in The Hague include: (i) strategy & central oversight, (ii) tax & valuations, (iii) fund management, (iv) investor relations & capital raising, and (v) central environmental, social & governance management.

CFM’s regional offices are led by regional heads with strong networks and specific skills and experience in diverse geographies and sectors, thereby providing the regional teams with regional intelligence. Three regional offices have been established, in South Africa, Colombia, and Singapore.

### Experience of the two parties:

FMO invests in sectors with the highest long-term impact: financial institutions, energy, agribusiness, and infrastructure. FMO is a financial institution owned-51% by the Dutch State; 42% by Dutch banks, and 7% by employees’ associations, trade unions, and individual investors.

With an investment portfolio of c.a. EUR 10 billion spanning over 85 countries, FMO is one of the larger bilateral private sector development banks in the world. FMO’s strategic goal is to be the preferred partner for climate action.

## B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Worldwide 780 million people still do not have access to an improved water source. Demand shortages are more pronounced in Africa and Asia. Furthermore, in developing countries around 3.2 million children die each year because of unsafe drinking water and poor sanitation. Waste is the fourth largest sector of global greenhouse gas emissions. The GCF has committed USD 1 billion for waste management as a key target sector for climate adaptation interventions. However, adaptation finance faces a severe funding gap. The Economic estimates that USD 460bn to USD 300bn in climate adaptation finance is needed annually by 2050. This funding gap can be addressed through two complementary and mutually reinforcing ways that it is: (i) reducing the face of climate-driven shocks – thereby delivering climate adaptation benefits; and (ii) reducing GHG emissions by creating (bio)cycles from the waste and wastewater that they form.

These projects, whether designed by the public or private sector, need to be implemented in a way that delivers on the additional climate requirements while keeping the costs of the services affordable to all. This is particularly important as there is a need for the GCF to engage with the private sector to bridge the funding gap and (ii) making the services accessible and affordable for all.

CO2 has been designed to do the above by blending investment funding with private funding in two structured funds. The CEF2 structure mobilizes private finance by reducing the risk with a junior-equity transaction structure. The CEF3 structure is a hybrid of debt and equity, combining a blended-funding approach in conjunction with the DFF. The CEF2 was modeled in a way that accommodates multiple investors’ risk-return preferences while minimizing concessionality as much as possible. The three tiers are:

* Tier 1 (T1) makes up [50%] of CO2 and is comprised of junior equity.
* Tier 2 (T2) makes up [49%] of CO2 and is comprised of ordinary equity.
* Tier 3 (T3) makes up [94%] of CO2 and is comprised of senior equity.

The GCF funding will play a key role in enabling CEF2 by providing a significant investment into Tier T1 for which commercial investors are not willing to invest. The GCF funding will also help mobilize the private sector in T2 and T3 by taking away some of the risks (without compromising commercial investors). T1 ameliorates the risk profile of the project, making it more attractive to commercial investors who are currently reluctant to invest into projects that have a high degree of concessionality. The CEF2 is designed for commercial investors. An important part of CO2’s investment mandate is LCR denominated revenues. LCR risk is expected to be more profound in a larger part of CO2’s portfolio than it is for C1. Commercial investors are not willing to invest into projects that have a high degree of concessionality and are required to absorb and buffer any (perceptions) of LCR risks to mobilize commercial investors into T2.

In addition to the investments made by the GCF funding in water and sanitation infrastructure, there is also an urgent need for innovative financing solutions for the emerging sector of ecosystem-based approaches. The GCF has committed USD 10 million for this purpose. This will be used to support pilot projects and finance ecosystem projects for which there is currently a limited track record, and practically no interest of the private sector, creating a demonstration effect that could open this sector for future private sector investments.

## B

A C2 intervention to GCF would unlock $14–$14x total investment at the project level (see Investment Leverage Ratio below) for climate change adaptation and mitigation. GCF is applying for a total GCF contribution to C2 of USD 145m. An investment of this size provides GCF the opportunity to become the lead financier in a portfolio of projects, potentially generating the largest additionally cost impact on C2’s members.

Through the C2 intervention, GCF has identified the following channels of GCF additionally through a C2 intervention:

- **Investment by GCF will support C2’s investment focus to a subset of countries, for which NOGs have been obtained, that have a greater need for intervention that fits within their NDCs.**

- A GCF intervention in the DFZ will catalyze a facility that offers scarce early-stage funding for projects with high potential but low readiness for financing. Projects are expected to be located to commercial projects. Access to development capital is one of the main unique selling points of C2. By providing access to the DFZ facility, GCF will enable C2 to facilitate the development of a greater number of projects, all of which offer GCF a preferential financing opportunity.

## B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Sustainability of the paradigm shift

Through organic development of developing countries’ financial markets and with a C2 demonstration effect, it is expected that private sector investors will be attracted to invest in projects that meet their preconditions; they are expected to fund a greater share of construction costs than an all-equity strategy where this is preferred over a traditional project finance approach. This may reduce costs across the board for GCF and its partners. The ability to identify the most cost-effective projects with funding would be instrumental to this market development through its catalytic impact on the project companies in which C2 invests.

The projects and companies financed through C2 will focus on commercially viable assets with intermediate-term investment needs and characteristics, in the range of 10-25 years. The commercial focus of C2’s investments ensures a long-term interest by owners and investors in the projects. In contrast to grant-financed projects, satisfactory financial returns will only be realized if the projects continue to perform well over their lifetime. This is particularly important for projects that are subject to cost overruns; in contrast, failure to ensure this – for example through poor maintenance – will have contractual or legal ramifications.

The sustainability of the paradigm shift will be further imprinted by involving local banks, potentially including Direct Access Entities (DAEs), in the re-financing of the Programme’s sub-projects after they have become operational.

The below refers to the entire Climate Investor Tier program. The GCF Funding Phased however only pertains in 85% and GCF’s C2 will therefore add up to USD 10 min for the CIF2 and USD 80 min, for the DFZ.

CIF3 aims to raise to 1,100 mln in funding for C2. This consists of USD 100 min. in reimbursable grants for the DFZ and USD 1,000 min. in funding for the CIF2.

C2, being the DFZ and the CIF2, had its first close, for a total of USD 675 mln., in November 2021[1]. This included investments from the European Commission, the Dutch Fund for Climate and Development in climate adaptation fund from the Dutch Ministry of Foreign Affairs (via the DFCD), KfW, AFD, BMZ, BID, CAF, IDB, IFAD, IFC, IOM, IMAS Foundation, BNG bank, Sanlam, FMO, Nordic Development Fund, and KLP, FAO, GIZ, and others. The CIF2 had its first close for USD 400 mln., in May 2021[2]. This DFCD and the European Commission investments, which have both invested in the DFZ and the T1 are also managed by FMO on behalf of either Donor. For the CIF2, all onboarded investors have successfully passed the AES & EKC KYC policies.

As explained in sections B.1., B.3, and B.5, the CIF is a structured fund with three different tiers with different investors that have different risk profiles. The first tier, or Tier 0, consists of institutional investors who are the largest donors and arguably the most additional. The second tier, or Tier 2, constitutes of impact investors who are also large donors but have a higher proportion of institutional and private investors. The ratio between the three tiers is set at 20:40:40. Whenever one or two out of the three tiers exceed that ratio that capital remains in the fund until the next close where the ratio can be adjusted to the value of the maximum value for which the 20:40:40 can be preserved.

The initial close primarily consisted of T2 (USD 151 mln.) and T3 (USD 400 mln.) investments. The T1 is currently closed, with T1 investors being considered as "currently unlocked" (as the 'unlocked' value is the maximum value for which 20:40:40 can be preserved). GCF participation in the T1 will therefore mobilize a large amount of private finance without requiring additional T3 investors coming in at later closes.

C2i's potential to catalyze private sector financing is particularly important in the context of the growing urgency for adaptation finance, which despite the growing urgency for adaptation finance, can falter for a lack of established precedent. ## D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

C2 has identified the following SDGs as primary goals:

- Climate Action (SDG 13)
- Affordable and Clean Energy (SDG 7)
- Clean Water and Sanitation (SDG 6)
- Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17)

By contributing to the above, C2 sees potential co-benefits in the following SDGs:

- Good Health and Well-being (SDG 3)
- Gender Equality (SDG 5)
- Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8)
- Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9)
- Life below water (SDG 14)

Environmental co-benefits:
- Decontaminating the earth's water will increase biodiversity and improve the livelihoods and resilience of local communities.

Social co-benefits include health co-benefits:

- Safe access to water and sanitation will reduce the spread and severity of diseases related to contaminated water, especially in areas lacking of sanitation.
- Development, construction, and operation of the infrastructure projects will leverage existing local capabilities and knowledge.
- Community-based programs planned and implemented alongside C2 projects will tackle core issues identified by stakeholders in communities where C2 is active.

### D.4. Needs of the recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Programme targets 18 different countries, spread over Latin America, Asia, and Africa, that all have significantly different scales and intensities of climate vulnerability (including water-stressed areas). The Programme will be implemented in a phased manner, with the first phase focusing on one country per year basis. The 18 countries have been identified on their National Designated Contributions (NDC) and the fit these with have the product offered by C2i. A detailed climate vulnerability assessment per country can be found in Annex 3.

### D.5. Needs of the recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

- **Host nation climate plans**, including National Designated Contribution (NDC) commitments (and any subsequent updates) and the National Adaptation Plans, as well sector plans from relevant ministries/authorities. The C2i team will review these documents to determine which countries selected by C2i recognize the importance of water, waste, and renewable energy, as highlighted in Annex 1.
- The initial short-listed countries cite specific targets to combat climate change (with a focus on adaptation) that are consistent with their NDCs and long-term plans. At least one such short-listed country aligns with C2i's regional office’s investment priorities.
- C2i has identified potential projects that could qualify for funding.

### Positive and ongoing stakeholder engagement: C2i to date has built a robust relationship with every single country on the initial shortlist, as evidenced by the letters of support on the back of receiving NOLs from such countries for the Climate Investor One (C1) Programme. ## The D.E. Efficiency and effectiveness (max: 300 words, approximately 1 page)

The GCF's investment has an expected mobilization ratio of 14 at the fund level (as every USD of T1 will mobilize 4 USD in the more senior tranches). A mobilization ratio of 1/4 is on the higher end of the benchmark and equal to the CIII proposal.

The below assumes an average 60% ownership of CI2 in the construction phase and two project life cycles during the implementation period.

### GCF Leverage Ratio

Investment leverage ratio = value of investment (total project cost) divided by the total amount of GCF support relating to this investment.

The investment leverage ratio (referring to the total investments at project level catalyzed by the GCF contribution) = A/B which equals 16x whereby:
- A = the contributions from CE2 (USD 1 billion) + Partner Contributions (USD 360 million)
- B = the GCF contribution (USD 145 million)

Including the “recycling” funded from the CE2, this equates to 20x whereby:
- A = the DFS (USD 180 million) + the CE2 (USD 2 billion) + Partner Contributions (USD 727 million)
- B = the GCF contribution (USD 145 million)

Private sector leverage ratio = amount of private sector financing in the investment project divided by the amount of GCF support. The private loans/leverage quality ratio (referring to the total investments made by the private sector catalyzed by the GCF contribution) = A/B which equals 16x whereby:
- A = Private sector investors in CE2 recycled (USD 800 million + USD 800 million) + private sector partners at project level (recycled USD 727m)
- B = The GCF contribution (USD 145 million)

CII's primary efficiency and effectiveness benefits are derived from its whole life financing approach:

- Funding made available by C12 at all stages of the project including early stage development and for pilot schemes, generates a pipeline of projects whilst building the capacity of local developers.
- The recycling character of C12 enables more projects to get developed and constructed during the implementation period of the fund (the capital is expected to be recycled twice).
- The whole of life approach reduces the time project developers usually lose at fundraising at the different phases of project development.
- Flexibility to use all-equity financing during the construction phase of the project. This eliminates cash flow and financial distress from the project during construction, whilst also ensuring the long-term interest in maintaining the project.
- Leaner capital expenditure of C12 projects through exclusive technology partnership agreements with Original Equipment Manufacturers (OEMs), unlocking economies of scale for equipment sourcing, installation, and maintenance.

E

Within sixty (60) calendar days after the end of each calendar quarter ended 31 March, 30 June, and 30 September, CFM shall prepare and provide all Investors with a quarterly report consisting of, inter alia:

- An unaudited profit and loss account, balance sheet, and cash flow statement of the Programme for the preceding quarter;
- Explanation thereof;
- An overview of the investment activity undertaken by the Programme within the reporting period;
- An overview of each project company, including:
  - A description of the Project Company's business;
  - The economic interest of the Programme in the Project Company;
  - The percentage of the Project Company owned by the Fund;
  - A discussion on any key events;
  - Selected financial information; and
  - If applicable, the amount of capital repaid or realized, the realized liquidation or disposal proceeds; and,
- Quarterly capital account statements.

Within one hundred and twenty (120) calendar days after the end of each financial year the Manager shall prepare and provide all Investors with an annual report (the "Annual Report") consisting of, inter alia:

- The annual accounts of the Programme, comprising at least a balance sheet, a profit, and loss account and explanatory notes thereto, as audited by an independent auditor;
- A report of the Auditor on its findings resulting from its audit of the annual accounts;
- An overview of each Project Company acquired sold; or otherwise disposed of during the reporting period;
- A value of each investment;
- A report on KPIs;
- A report on impact indicators;
- A breakdown of the Programme's operating costs during the reporting period;
- A schedule of Drawdowns and distributions effected during the reporting period;
- The total amount of the Undrawn Commitments as per the end of the reporting period; and,
- A report detailing the activities of the Programme during the reporting period.

## Environmental & Social Reporting

An important aspect of GCF's risk management process is the ongoing monitoring and review of each investment. Depending on which Fund that investment is drawing from (Development or Construction Equity), different monitoring requirements will be applied. Monitoring is commensurate with the risk categorization, with physical visits to the location(s) of higher-risk projects during construction.

All project companies drawing from the CEF2 are also required to monitor the performance of any contractors employed at the project site(s) and to participate in regular meetings with GCF to ensure that they are meeting their agreed E&S performance targets.

In addition, GCF will also ensure that any legal requirements for monitoring, for example, that may be included in permits and licenses for development, construction, and operation, are incorporated into the project monitoring and evaluation plan. GCF requires all projects in receipt of CEF2 to be reported in accordance with defined key performance indicators (presented below). In addition, the performance measurement framework indicated in H1.1 and H1.2 will be incorporated into fund level and project-level monitoring and evaluation activities that will be managed by GCF.

## Evaluations

The Accreditation Entity, together with GCF, will arrange for an interim and final evaluation to be outsourced to an independent evaluator after 10 and 20 years, respectively. The interim evaluation will focus on the extent to which C2 is achieving its goals regarding the number of - mention GCF impact indicators -. The final evaluation will constitute an ex-op effectiveness study. The evaluation will focus on the DAC criteria, which focus on relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, impact and sustainability and the GCF's 5 evaluation criteria.

- Relevance: the extent to which C2 is aligned with the objectives and policies of GCF
- Effectiveness: the extent to which the objectives of C2 were achieved
- Efficiency: the extent to which activities were cost-efficient and timely
- Impact: determining the positive and negative changes produced by C2, directly or indirectly, intended or unintended.
- Sustainability: the extent to which the investments of C2 are likely to continue operating after C2 has exited

# Compliance Risk

The risk is that CFM and CFM-managed vehicles' compliance obligations (regulatory or internal) are not met, including reporting requirements.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
- Dedicated legal and compliance staff
- Quarterly compliance reporting and testing
- Continuous monitoring
- Formal staff induction training
- Documented policies and procedures (periodically updated as required)

## Selected Risk Factor 3
- Category: Compliance
- Probability: Medium
- Impact: Low

## Description
Conflicts of Interest Risk

The risk that a conflict of interest occurs in an area of CFM's service provision to its Managed Vehicles, their investors, and its relationship with all of its stakeholders whereby CFM (or an investor or other stakeholder of CFM) may benefit whilst possible tangible loss may be caused to (another) investor or the managed vehicle service.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
- CFM has a comprehensive policy established to manage conflicts of interest
- Follow-up on any identified conflict of interest by implementing measures to manage and mitigate the identified conflict will be implemented promptly through three basic mechanisms to manage a conflict, namely controlling, avoiding, and mitigating.
- The appropriate mechanism to manage a conflict will depend on the circumstances and nature of any given conflict.
- Documented policies and procedures (periodically updated as required)

## Selected Risk Factor 4
- Category: Credit
- Probability: Medium
- Impact: Low

## Description
Interest Rate Risk

The risk that the cost of borrowing increases (and CFPIY Term 2 returns decrease) due to changes in interest rates post refinancing event.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
- Changes to debt facilities on hedging policy and terms and conditions.
- Borrowing restrictions within the trust deed and prospectus or information memorandum, investment policy, regulatory requirements, and other relevant documents.
- Fund board to be advised of the selection process and in the negotiation of terms and conditions of any debt package.
- The fund will be advised (and seek) advice from its legal advisors/derivatives team to manage interest rate exposure.
- Calculations confirming adherence to borrowing limits, financial covenants under lending documentation, and hedging policies tabled at board meetings.

## Selected Risk Factor 5
- Category: Foreign
- Probability: Medium
- Impact: Medium

## Currency Risk

The risk that future cash receipts/payments will decrease/increase due to adverse movement in exchange rates.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

- **Miss-off take agreements converted by C2 at the asset level are directly or indirectly denominated in USD leading to an influx of USD (or equivalent) amounts.**
  - It is therefore estimated that most off-take agreements will be indexed to USD currencies, if deemed desirable/necessary.
  - C2 may also consider hedging these cash flows through a combination of financial instruments and/or derivatives using a hedging mechanism that enjoys wide support from the DFI community including FMO, EBRD and KfW.

- **In general, the Fund will not be able to hedge its currency positions on non-US-denominated investments.**
  - The Fund may consider hedging its currency positions only to the extent required for hedging currency positions on non-US-denominated investments.
  - C2 is not obliged to do this; however
- **A hedging policy may be adapted in respect of non-US-currency commitments for imminent acquisitions or divestments, contingent liabilities and/or other financial instruments such as dividend distributions.**
  - Distributions and other cash flows from assets are not hedged.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)

CFM, in cooperation with, and as approved by the AE, has developed an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) to ensure that all projects comply with the requirements for ES management. The ESMS manual has been disclosed in accordance with the GCF's ex-ante disclosure requirements for category A1+ projects/programmes. The document, provided in Annex 6, describes the requirements for ES management and outlines how these are embedded into CFM’s Responsible Investment Policy (‘RIP’). It describes how the arrangements are embedded into CFM’s responsible investment framework that is designed to ensure compliance with the Equator Principles (EP) and Environmental and Social (E&S) management measures that need to be adopted by the Project Company. The ESMS has been designed in accordance with the requirements of the GCF, as well as international standards including ISO 14001 and ISO 45001 as well as the AE’s Sustainability Policy Universe.

The structure the ESMS is presented in the figure below and summarized as follows:

* **Sections 1 and 2:** Following an introductory section, section 2 of the ESMS outlines the context and scope, with a brief presentation of the CFM Managed Vehicles and the potential E&S impacts associated with them. Section 3 describes the key roles and responsibilities and expectations for the scope of application of the ESMS.
* **Section 4:** Describes the senior management’s place for oversight of the ESMS, the resources assigned for its implementation, the key roles and responsibilities and how the right level of direction and capacity is ensured.
* **Section 5:** Describes CFM’s policies, principles, commitments, and E&S requirements that form the overall Responsible Investment Framework.
* **Chapter 6 describes how CFM operationalizes the RIP across the investment cycle through risk management processes.**
* **Chapter 7 presents the system in place for monitoring and periodically reviewing the effectiveness of the ESMS, through the definition of E&S performance indicators and impact indicators, audits, and management reviews.**
* **Chapter 8 outlines the arrangements in place for communication with external stakeholders, including government agencies, local communities, and other relevant parties.**
* The ESMS also includes a set of Annexures, including Annex 2 (List of Excluded Activities); Annex 4 (Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework), and Annex 5 (Resettlement Planning Framework).

## EAS Governance and Management

CFM, as the AG, plays a critical role in CFMI governance. Specifically in relation to EAS governance and management CFM has committed to FMO as AE to: (i) implement and maintain all EAS as to the satisfaction of FMO; (ii) appoint or acquire services of ESG specialists; (iii) submit annual reports including EAS performance and (iv) conduct an independent audit of EAS and project programs.

CFM, as the EE, has appointed Head of ESG that oversees and implements all EAS commitments and responsibilities. The Head of ESG directly reports to the MB and is part of the Executive Committee. Reporting to the Head of ESG is a broader team of ESG professionals. The team will be expanded if necessary to ensure adequate resourcing.

In addition, CFM will: (i) hire external EAS advisors to support development of project level studies and ongoing monitoring; and (ii) partner with organizations (such as universities, think tanks, research organization and training institutions) to deliver specialist knowledge and expertise focused on delivering community engagement projects. Furthermore, all CFM Investment Staff have a responsibility for ensuring that all projects are developed and implemented according to CFM's requirements as set out in the EAS.

At the project level, CFM will require the project to manage EAS risks as set out in the EAS. Each project we ensure they have a project level ESGM as well as adequate staffing and resources. CFM’s ESG team will work closely together with the project company to ensure compliance.


---
**Page 61**
---

EA5 Risk Management

CFM’s approach to responsible investment is enshrined in the RIC which guides all investment decisions and related activities relating to the CFM Managed Vehicles. The principles and undertakings of the RIC are reinforced by a set of commitments which are contained in the RIP. These commitments are implemented through the arrangements described in the ESMS Manual and supporting documentation.

The ESG considerations are fully integrated into the investment process, starting with providing inputs such as the need for an environmental and social assessment (ESAS) to project development, construction, operation, and final exit. Each investment is assessed to confirm the potential for adverse impacts and risks, and this leads to a preliminary ES & risk categorization. As a general rule, Category A projects will require an ESAS in accordance with the requirements set out in Section 6 of the ESMS manual.

The risk category will inform the scope of the development activities that will be required, including the commissioning of an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) for all Category A as well as Category B projects. The ESIA will be commissioned by CFM during the project preparation phase for adoption by the Project Company from the start of the construction phase. Section 6 of the ESMS manual provides a detailed description of how to assess E&S impacts and risks are assessed and integrated into the investment and decision-making process.

In addition to managing adverse impacts and risk, CFM is committed to maximizing opportunities for delivering environmental and social benefits in collaboration with local communities in the area of influence of its projects. These benefits are designed to be additional to the positive development outcomes that will be focused on local actions that can be taken to support progress towards sustainable development (SDG 17) and poverty reduction (SDG 3). Budget is committed to community development during the development, construction, and operational phases.

The CFM’s environmental and social management programme will be established during the development phase of each project, and the programmes will be implemented under the guidance of CFM. The programmes are typically developed in conjunction with the local network, local knowledge, and expertise required to deliver successful outcomes.


Human Rights

CFM recognizes the centrality of human rights to sustainable development and ensuring fair distribution of development and opportunities and benefits. CFM is committed to operating in accordance with all legal requirements and agreements, including the ILO Core Conventions and the UN’s Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. These principles have been implemented within CFM, eliminating all forms of unlawful discrimination and promoting equal opportunity for all employees.


---
**Page 62**
---

and other areas where CFM has control or influence over. Project companies will be required to provide information relating to their suppliers.

### Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment

CFM's gender policy, as described in G.1 and annexed to the FP, details the commitments and arrangements for achieving gender equality and women’s empowerment within the business and in relation to the projects. CFM recognizes that women have much as gain from its projects (and particular those related to water, sanitation and energy) as men do. Women who are more active in their relative social position, will likely not profit as men from the access to energy, water, and sanitation resources provided by these projects. Therefore, it is important for CFM to ensure that gender-related risks and opportunities be assessed and managed in accordance with a local gender action plan (see G.2) as well as in accordance with other E&S safeguards as described in the ESMS manual.

Of particular note is the issue of gender-based violence and harassment to which members of the Project workforce may be exposed, including sexual harassment. CFM has adopted a number of projects to implement measures to safeguard female members of its workforces as well as in the broader communities it serves. This includes conducting risk assessments for gender-based violence at all stages of project implementation, adoption of complaint mechanisms and measures to ensure that cases are reported or suspected cases are reported. The grievance mechanism adopted by every project will make specific provisions for a confidential approach as well as providing the necessary support to whistleblowers and others who are involved in managing the case receiving appropriate training in line with international guidance.

### Indigenous Peoples

CFM recognizes the special importance of respecting the rights of Indigenous Peoples (IPs) and of avoiding adverse social impacts on the IPs which may arise because of a Project. CFM will avoid proceeding with a Project beyond the Development Phase (activity 2.1) where adverse impacts cannot be avoided. When avoidance is not feasible, CFM requires measures to be put in place to minimize and mitigate these impacts as far as possible. The mitigation measures must be consistent with the requirements of the IP Policy and are identified in a project-specific IP Plan.

CFM’s Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) which is designed to guide all projects in terms of their approach towards IPs, and which is aligned with the requirements of the IP Policy, the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples, and the United Nations Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights. All grievance mechanisms must be developed in accordance with the IPPF and the UN Guiding Principles.

### Corporate Grievance Mechanism

CFM provides a fund-broad grievance mechanism for external stakeholders on its website. This grievance mechanism is implemented by stakeholders such as NGOs and CSOs, government representatives, investors, and the wider public. Grievance mechanisms are also implemented by the Project Companies and are intended for use by the Project affected communities. All grievance mechanisms must be developed in accordance with the UN Guiding Principles on Business and Human Rights.

All grievance mechanisms will also consider gender-sensitivity and provide a survivor-centered approach for responding to reported cases of gender-based violence and sexual harassment.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.5.0 | PAGE 80 OF 63

## CFP recognizes that projects can only be successful if they are gender sensitive.

The use of the term 'gender' refers to the social relations between women and men, thereby the need to work with women and men to ensure both equitable benefit. It is further recognized that women have as much to gain from its projects as men do. The GCF believes that a project which does not address gender equality in its social position, will likely not profit as men to the access to energy, water, and sanitation resources.

CFM, in cooperation with the AE, has developed a Gender Action Plan (GAP) "to present an overview of relevant gender-related issues in the water, sanitation, and oceans sectors in the 25 targeted countries. The GAP will also provide guidance on how to mainstream gender into project design and implementation approach described in the GCF guidance document for mainstreaming gender in projects. The GAP will be supplemented by local analysis of gender issues and opportunities, and a local gender action plan that will be developed as part of the project preparation phase. The AE will ensure that the gender component is included in this action plan throughout the lifecycle of every asset (and will form part of the handover at the exit of an investment).

The GAP is intended to support the Manager in fulfilling its commitments to responsible investment in accordance with the Responsible Investment Framework and C2's Responsible Investment Policy. In particular, the GAP is based on the Framework’s commitment to “Do No Harm” and “Do Good” and the policy’s commitment to “maximizing opportunities for positive social benefits from its investments on local communities” and “maximizing opportunities for positive social benefits to be realized.” In this sense, the GCF's gender mainstreaming arrangements that already exist will be addressed in a more direct manner by C2’s GAP. The AE and the EE also confirm that the budget would be reallocated if it was deemed insufficient during the implementation of C2.

The gender assessments and the full Gender Action Plan are to be provided as Annex 8.

## G.3 Financial Management and procurement (max. 500 words, excluding tables) | page

The financial management is organized in two parts: (i) at the FMOO and (ii) at the AE level. On the AE's level, the AE will enter into the Funding Activity Agreement (FAA) with the GCF that will oversee the fiduciary management of the GCF’s funding in its GCF account and on a ledger specific to C2. The AE will request the GCF funds, based on the funding needs as predicted by CFM or on a bi-annual basis. The AE will be responsible for ensuring that all payments are made within 30 days from the date of receipt. The AE will manage any unused funds of the GCF. The drawdown requests and payments at FMOO will follow FMOO's standard policies and procedures for FMOO investment funding.

On the FMOO level, the financial management is organized by CFM including drawdowns from and distributions to investors, as well as all fund-related accounting will be instigated, overseen, and performed by CFM (as asset manager). CFM has an Authorization and Payments Policy in place, which details the process of authorizing and making payments. The policy also sets out the responsibilities for ensuring that all controls required in order to ensure a sound control environment. CFM has controlled access to the CIO Fund bank accounts and is responsible for initiating and releasing payments according to the Authorization and Payments Policy. CFM will be responsible for maintaining the records of all transactions, and investor reporting is controlled and managed by CFM. A monthly review of the trial balance is performed by the AE. The annual audit of the GCF’s financial statements is conducted by PwC. The annual audit of CFM's accounts will be audited annually by PwC. Accounts will be prepared according to the International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) and will consist of, among others, a balance sheet, profit and loss account, explanatory notes, auditor's report, an overview of project turnover activity, breakdown of


---
**Page 64**
---

management proposal, breakdown of fund operating costs, etc., CFM will require the project companies to prepare accounts in accordance with appropriately recognized financial reporting standards and each project company is required to appoint an Auditor. CFM's financial reports will be subject to FMO review and approval as well as GCF's participation on CFM's supervisory board & CFM's Audit, Risk, and Compliance Committee.

The AE’s approved procurement policy will be applied, in addition to CFMs, throughout the programme including in regard to activities implemented by the EE. Please refer to Annex 10: Procurement Plan, for further details on procurement thresholds and requirements

### G.A. Disclosure of funding proposal

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF as no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity of the project company has prepared two copies of this document:
  - full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the applicable laws and regulations
  - redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above containing confidential information.

# H. ANNEXES

## H.1 Mandatory annexes

- **Annex 1**: NDA no-objection letter(s) (template provided)
- **Annex 2**: Feasibility study - and a market study if applicable
- **Annex 3**: Economic and/or financial analyses in spreadsheet format
- **Annex 4**: Detailed budget plan (template provided)
- **Annex 5**: Implementation timeline including key project/Programme milestones (template provided)
- **Annex 6**: E&S document corresponding to the E&S category (A, B or C; or II, IZ or III):
    - Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) or
    - Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) or
    - Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)
- **Annex 7**: Summary of consultations and stakeholder engagement plan
- **Annex 8**: Gender assessment and project/Programme-level action plan (template provided)
- **Annex 9**: Legal due diligence (regulation, taxation, and insurance)
- **Annex 10**: Procurement plan (template provided)
- **Annex 11**: Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 12**: Co-financing request (template provided)
- **Annex 14**: Term sheet including a detailed disbursement schedule and, if applicable, repayment schedule

## H.2 Other annexes as applicable

- **Annex 15**: Evidence of internal approval (template provided)
- **Annex 16**: Maps indicating the location of proposed interventions
- **Annex 17**: Multi-country project/Programme information (template provided)
- **Annex 18**: Appraisal, due diligence, or evaluation report for proposals based on up-scaling or replicating a project
- **Annex 19**: Procedures for contracting procurement by third parties or executing entities undertaking projects financed by the Fund
- **Annex 20**: First (AM, ICFT, KYC) assessment
- **Annex 21**: Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
- **Annex 22**: Assessment of GHG emission reductions and their monitoring and reporting (for mitigation and cross-cutting projects)*
- **Annex X**
