# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP190

Proposal name: Climate Investor Two

Accredited entity: FMO - Dutch Entrepreneurial Development Bank

Project/programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### Impact potential Scale: Medium

#### 1.1 Deterioration
Climate Investor Two (C2T) intends to develop and construct infrastructure projects in developing countries in the water, sanitation, and ocean sectors that reduce the effects and consequences of climate change by mitigating greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and enhancing resilience.

The programme is based on a multi-faceted blended finance approach applied by Climate Investor One (FPI901) (approved by the Board of Directors of FMO on 28 June 2017). The blended finance approach will mobilize public investments and unlock private capital across three sectors:

- **Water:** The sector is defined by projects that source, transport, and treat water from municipalities and other sectors. Climate adaptation benefits include building infrastructure to withstand extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and storms.
- **Sanitation:** The sanitation sector looks at treating raw wastewater to avoid sequester methane emissions and increase water availability in areas experiencing increased rainfall or drought.
- **Ocean:** The ocean and ecosystems sector aims to reduce GHG emissions from and enhance the resilience of ports and shipping and (b) protect, strengthen, and restore important coastal habitats such as mangroves, coral reefs, and seagrass beds against storms and surges intensified by climate change.

C2T intends to deploy a whole-of-the-finance approach utilising two independent but operationalized funds. The funds will focus on different stages of the project lifecycle: A Development Fund (DF) provides reimbursable grants-to early-stage climate projects, while a Construction Finance Facility (CEF) provides loans for projects during the construction phase. The DF has been set up to provide development loans (in the form of reimbursable grants) to eligible projects that are selected by Climate Change Fund Managers (CCFM) and third parties, to project companies. The purpose of this is its fast-track qualifying development projects to financial close by reducing the development timeline and improving the likelihood of successful completion. Once a project has successfully completed their development phase, CEF reimburses the grant to DF, and the project moves on to the construction phase. In this way, CEF ensures that DF remains capable of recycling its capital.

CEF has been set up to finance the construction phase by providing up to 75 per cent of the required project equity. The CEF equity approach is designed to address a


---
**Page 102**
---

# Green Climate Fund

## Structural Shifts in Many Local Banks and Lending Institutions Being Unwilling to Assume Construction Risk

This further aims to decrease the construction time and costs by:
(i) reducing the complexity and costs at the project level through an equity-only funding strategy;
(ii) reducing costs of debt financing for projects through a blended finance vehicle;
(iii) reducing costs by eliminating debt-specific costs; and (iv) recycling capital from completed projects into new investments.

## CEF Structure

CEF itself is structured as a blended finance vehicle to better mobilize commercial capital at scale into CEF. It is made up of a multi-tiered structure to accommodate multiple investors with different risk appetites, including:
- Tier 1 makes up 20 per cent of CEF and it comprised of junior equity;
- Tier 2 makes up 40 per cent of CEF and it comprised of ordinary equity; and
- Tier 3 makes up 40 per cent of CEF and it comprised of senior equity.

### The USD 15 billion funding proposal requests a total commitment from the GCF of USD 9.8 billion, of which USD 4.5 billion will be mobilized as grants and USD 5.3 billion to Tier 1 of CEF. The expected capital size of the prospective fund is estimated at USD 90 million for DFID and USD 2 billion for other GCF partners.

### The junior tier 1 capital at concessional rates (0 per cent inflation Consumer Price Index (CPI)). GDP per capita in developing countries is below USD 5,000. Tier 3 tranches and thus mobilize private sector finance into the facility. For instance, the junior equity tranche will be used to invest in projects where there are significant risks of political instability or social unrest.

### This programme would be GCF’s first at scale private sector programme in the water and sanitation sector. The programme aims to mobilize capital at scale. The USD 145 million investment by GCF will potentially catalyse USD 942 million at the local level, and with higher leverage ratios for other investors, the total amount of funds mobilized will be higher.

### The Dutch Entrepreneurial Development Bank (FMO) will act as the accredited entity (AE) for this proposal. CPM, a joint venture between FMO and Sanitation, will act as the fund manager of G2 and is the first executing entity (EE). DFID and CEF, the two funds within C2, will be the second EE.

### IMPACTS: G2 aims to make investments which result in the scaling and replication of water and sanitation services that meet the drinking water supply and sanitation needs and mitigation needs of developing countries. The investments themselves are expected to generate measurable climate impacts and also are intended to have a market demonstration effect, thereby catalyzing private sector investment for sustainable development in host countries with a risk-adjusted return, will catalyse commercial financing.

### The water cycle is a key component of the global climate crisis. The World Bank estimates that by 2050, water shortages caused by climate change could reduce the gross domestic product (GDP) growth rate in developing countries by up to 6 per cent. Developing countries in Africa by USD 1.6 trillion, compounding existing health and development challenges. Water is also a key component of the global climate crisis, with methane emissions from untreated waste and wastewater accounting for 7 per cent of global GHG emissions. The water cycle can be seen as a carbon sink that sequesters and avoids carbon emissions, and restoring them can enhance their services in buffering against climate change-intensified disasters.


---
**Page 103**
---

### The Financing of the Water, Sanitation and Ocean Sectors

The financing of the water, sanitation and ocean sectors has primarily been within the public domain. However, the budgets that developing countries have available to invest in these sectors are insufficient for the task at hand. Adequately addressing the lack of low-environmental impact infrastructure is a critical pathway toward achieving a sustainable development pathway while ensuring resilient water supply in areas experiencing reduced water availability and increased demand.

Traditional private investors, however, perceive the sectors as high-risk, particularly in developing countries.

### GCF’s Two-Fund Blended Approach

GCF’s two-fund blended approach is intended to address these risks (real and perceived) by both providing private sector investors with a blended finance solution that reduces risk and increasing the availability of public financing for water, sanitation, and ocean infrastructure projects by demonstrating the possibility of risk-adjusted returns to investors. This can help to unlock the potential levels of investing in such sectors and contribute toward solving some of the perceived high risks.

Furthermore, highlighting the commercial viability of water, sanitation and ocean infrastructure projects is a key element of GCF’s strategy for financing and implementation of such projects in host countries by providing a transfer of knowledge to local partners. GCF aims to do this by including local partners and developers in both the development and implementation phases of projects. This will help to build capacity within local financial intermediaries, including direct access entities, in the refinancing of its local projects.

### Successfully Developing and Implementing Adaptation Projects

Successfully developing and implementing adaptation and mitigation projects for the water, sanitation and ocean sectors in developing countries will involve: (i) improving water supply; (ii) increasing access to improved sanitation; (iii) improving access to sanitation; (iv) improving renewable energy capacity and generation; (v) improving access to fast-track green ports and shipping; (vi) restoring and/or protecting riverine, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems. In terms of mitigation potential, improving water supply infrastructure will help to reduce the demand for fossil fuels in transport, while increasing access to cost-efficient green ports and shipping, and restoring and/or protecting riverine, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems can contribute toward reducing emissions from the water, sanitation and oceans sectors of host countries. With regard to mitigation benefits, GCF is expected to reduce or avoid 6 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) per year by 2030.

### In terms of adaptation potential

In terms of adaptation potential, developing and constructing additional water supply infrastructure in developing countries that will help to protect and restore ecological infrastructure, will enable GCF to reduce the vulnerability of developing countries to climate hazards. GCF aims to commit up to USD 3.7 billion in infrastructure projects in developing countries over the next five years. These projects are expected collectively to provide approx. 14 million people with year-round access to reliable and safe water supplies, while also protecting and restoring riverine, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems covering approx.900,000 hectares to help them adapt to climate variability and change.

### GCF’s Contribution

GCF is also expected to contribute to technology transfer by proactively cooperating with local partners and developers during project development as well during the implementation phase of projects.

It is important that all investment projects in which GCF funds are used to take an equity stake in GCF to ultimately deliver on the specific climate change adaptation and mitigation impacts. All investments must be subject to a robust climate adaptation assessment at the level of investor, activity and/or site prior to any decision on investment through IFP


---
**Page 104**
---

and CIF, targeted to the broad way in which the funding proposal discusses interwoven development and climate challenges, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) identified a risk that a shortage of viable deals could lead to low standards being applied by CIF in its investments. The TAP also highlighted the need for CIF to ensure that it funds business-as-usual water and sanitation investment projects that lack any clearly motivated, specific adaptation benefits. In response to this feedback from the independent TAP on this, the AE highlighted the screening process the Climate Investor Two Development Fund Investment Committee uses to identify deals with clear climate change adaptation benefits: "The deal teams will provide [the] Climate Investor Two Development Fund Investment Committee[with] historic climate trends (observed changes or variability in climate conditions over time) and projected future climate trends (projected changes in climate conditions over the next 20 years). The committee will use this information (an explanation on how these changes will hurt communities in the project area), adaptation benefits, and CIF’s investment criteria to determine whether a deal is suitable. These services, or technology will help the community adapt to these climate impacts, an explanation of how they will do so, and an explanation of the Rio Markers for climate adaptation (to be achieved by the company) and an overview of the project’s baseline and mid-term (year 10 and final year 20 projections)."

Overall impact potential is assessed as Medium. The median rating is conservative and reflects the fact that CIF has not yet demonstrated its ability to identify, finance, and execution risk. CIF demonstrated its capacity to manage a similar vehicle in Climate Investor One. However, CIF’s focus on highly transformative sectors will require new capacity and expertise, leading us to help our impact rating improve.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: Medium**

The focus of the funding proposal is an innovative investment scheme enabling new investments in previously or minimally track record in developing markets in new sectors previously considered as high risk (water, sanitation, ocean), accelerating the development and implementation of climate change adaptation measures. CIF will provide different types of technical support to the private sector at the development stage as well as ready access to capital for projects that are deemed suitable. The innovative elements of the investment scheme and could be considered innovative for the targeted host countries.

C2G aims to create a paradigm shift by making investments which result in the scaling and replication of water, sanitation and ocean infrastructure that is aligned with the adaptation needs of developing countries. CIF’s focus on these sectors has been perceived as risky or better suited for public finance, especially in developing countries. C2G will be able to provide CIF with an opportunity to demonstrate its ability to attract private sector funding, although the success of these efforts will depend on participating countries’ policy and regulatory environments. Additionally, C2G has the potential to demonstrate the actual versus theoretical benefits of investing in water, sanitation and ocean infrastructure generally. Data and learning from C2G has the potential to change the way financial institutions view investments in these sectors, increasing their stability and transferring sectoral technology and knowledge to local communities.

Moreover, CIF’s whole-life financing approach itself is paradigm shifting by eliminating the need for a separate project finance facility. This will be achieved by ensuring that projects can progress smoothly and expeditiously from a concept or pilot project to a scaled-up investment. The success of this approach has already been demonstrated by Climate Investor One's success and now by the willingness and appetite of CIF and its investors to shift into more commercially difficult sectors with C2G.


---
**Page 105**
---

application to a new thematic area sets a precedent for similar approaches to be implemented by other institutions and in other sectors. By demonstrating a replicable and adaptable approach to catalyzing private sector financing of climate-focused infrastructure, local project development, and investment in adaptation, C2I can serve as an example for upscaling, building on its precedent.

C2I's potential to catalyze impact in this context is amplified by the scarcity of infrastructure-focused climate adaptation funds, which, despite the growing urgency for adaptation finance, can falter at lack of established precedents. Bringing C2I to market will provide a new and innovative approach to financing adaptation projects, thereby contributing to a reallocation of the primarily mitigation-themed funding available at present.

In the longer term, GEF will also create opportunities for scalability and replicability beyond its investments, through the transfer of knowledge and technical skills to local partners.


Overall paradigm shift potential is assessed as Medium. Replication of the investment model is considered feasible on a national scale.


### 1 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

Alignment with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): C2I contributes to the achievement of several SDGs, primarily Climate Action (SDG 13), Affordable and Clean Energy (SDG 7), Clean Water and Sanitation (SDG 6), and Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17). By contributing to these goals, C2I supports a range of other SDGs, including:

- SDGs: Good Health and Well-being (SDG 3); Gender Equality (SDG 5); Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8); Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure (SDG 9) and Life Below Water (SDG 14)

Environmental co-benefits: C2I can improve water quality and in turn increase biodiversity. By improving access to clean drinking water, the health of rivers and the ecosystem will also protect communities from floods and increase biodiversity and food stock.

Social and health benefits: Changing climate patterns, together with growing economies and populations, could reduce urban water availability by 2050 in two-thirds of countries. In many developing countries, women are disproportionately affected at risk. A study done by the World Health Organization in 2017 shows that globally, more than 785 million people do not have access to safe drinking water services and over 2 billion people do not have basic sanitation facilities. One of the objectives of C2I is to realize climate change adaptation impacts in countries undergoing, or expected to undergo, rapid urbanization. The project will provide a sustainable solution that treats the water necessary for both municipal and industrial users to be resilient to the local climate.

Gender-sensitive development impact: The lack of sanitation facilities is even more impactful for young women, as they are more likely to miss out on education due to insufficient access to safe drinking water. This has a significant social and economic impact as they are frequently responsible for collecting the water for use in the household. This and other household responsibilities reduce the likelihood of women receiving education. By

# Needs of the recipients

## Scale: Medium–High

The United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization estimates that over 2 billion people currently live in areas subject to water stress, with water availability in many parts of the world falling below 1000 cubic meters per person per year or less. In addition, up to 80 per cent of illnesses in the developing world are linked to inadequate water and sanitation services, while over 90% of all infectious diseases are transmitted through unsafe drinking water sources which contaminate treated water sources globally. These factors can determine local health security and economic opportunity. G2 will provide much needed infrastructure and technology to improve access to safe drinking water.

The G2 programme targets 25 different countries, spread over Latin America, Asia and Africa, with varying degrees of climate vulnerability (including water stress regions). G2 will assess the intervention required on a case-by-case and per country basis. The 25 countries have been selected based on their share of determined contributions (DAC) and whether G2 products are suitably aligned.

To attract capital into host countries, G2 requires investments from DAC capital to leverage private sector capital from private investors who have not been active in the water, sanitation and health sectors.

The independent TAP assesses this programme as Medium–High in terms of needs of recipients.


# Country ownership

## Scale: Medium–High

CFR has a demonstrated track record from Climate investor ties in building a positive relationship with many of the identified country national development banks (NDBs) and in procuring no-objective letters (NOIs) from such countries. CFR and the AE have already sought NOIs from 25 countries for G2. A summary overview of G2 was shared with each.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Based on the assessment above, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal.
We are pleased to see earlier GCF-approved blended finance models being expanded and applied to harder-to-solve financial barriers: mobilizing private finance for mitigation and adaptation.

The one area that gave us some pause was the level of concessionality requested (10 per cent return = CPI + GCF funding), about the request was in line with the level previously provided by the World Bank. However, we believe that the level of concessionality required to leverage private sector finance is an evolving target, as more firms become experienced and comfortable with blended finance models and their associated risks. What might have been the appropriate amount of concessionality needed to mobilize private funding in 2017 may be greater than what is needed in 2022.

In our discussions with GCF staff, we were able to clarify some misunderstandings and perceptions with which the AE made clear that the perceived risks associated with the water, sanitation and ocean sectors are not as high as they might appear. We also agreed on the need for GCF to provide the requested levels of concessionality. Ultimately, we were persuaded that the risk of over-subsidization is low in this case.

As such, we would prefer to see future blended finance instruments with flexible structures that allow the GCF to make a return if the expected risks and barriers to fund mobilization are not met. We also believe that it is important for the AE to recognize that GCF concessionality strives to be priced at (i) the lowest necessary level to achieve the desired mobilization and climate impact; (ii) to avoid crowding out commercial initiatives that do not require concessional funding, and (iii) to ensure a sustainable return on investment of GCF itself.

We also note approvingly that CPM is providing a General Partner contribution of 1 per cent (up to USD 10 million) into GIZ. Investors in private sector funds typically prefer to see managers allocate some of their fees into the fund itself or otherwise contribute this. It is a sign of commitment and support for the project, which we believe will be appreciated.

Finally, we observe that true paradigm shift will be achieved only if the GCF funds are able to build a track record of performing impactful and successful investments, which increases the confidence of asset owners and encourages new investment managers to


---
**Page 109**

structure and vehicles in these sectors. For that reason, it is important that GEZ’s underlying investments ultimately deliver on the specific climate change adaptation and mitigation impacts sought in the funding proposal. All potential investment projects claiming climate change adaptation and/or mitigation as a key objective must be subjected to an assessment of their likely impact being conducted at investor/activity/site-level prior to making a decision on investment. Owing to the potentially large number of such projects, this assessment is best carried out by the project’s own cost before project-specific investment decisions are made by GEZ. This introduces a level of uncertainty into our overall portfolio that we must be aware of and manage. We rely heavily on fund models where we must rely to a great extent on the capacity of AEs and their selection processes. # Executive Summary

## Overview of Scope and Methodology

The Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plans form part of Climate Fund Managers (CFM) funding proposals to the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for financial support to the Climate Investor 2 (CII) to invest in infrastructure for water and sanitation services, the use and protection of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, and climate change adaptation.

The Gender Assessment explores how, in the context of climate change women and men are affected by problems in three main areas: i) access to and management of water and sanitation (WASH) services; ii) the protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems; and iii) the development of climate change adaptation strategies.

The preparation of the Gender Assessment comprised four main components (Figure 1):

- A global evidence review of 85 studies in the areas of WASH, wetlands, coastal and marine protection and port infrastructure;
- Analysis of national WASH, climate change and gender policies in 11 priority countries: Bangladesh, Benin, Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Morocco, Namibia, Nigeria, Uganda;
- Analysis of selected SDG-related quantitative datasets available for access to WASH; the prevalence of waterborne diseases; and the prevalence of sanitation deficiencies;
- Stakeholder analysis in 8 out of the 11 priority countries to map actors in the WASH sector and engage stakeholders in the Gender Assessment process.

## Overview of Global Evidence Review

### WASH
WASH infrastructure and services for households, in health care facilities and in schools are inadequate in most developing country contexts. Their supply is further undermined by climate change, which affects weather patterns, reducing water availability and damaging WASH infrastructure.

Women and girls are disproportionately affected by inadequate WASH infrastructure and services. Their domestic and care-giving roles mean they are responsible for collecting water, for WASH practices within the household, and for ensuring that children have access to WASH facilities at school. This limits their access to quality WASH services available to them. Women and girls can often travel long distances to collect water, limiting time spent on other activities such as education, income generation, socializing with friends, or engaging in productive activities. It exposes them to the risk of violence whilst travelling to and from water points, and to a heightened risk of disease through greater exposure to poor quality WASH.

Women and girls’ physiology means they have greater need for WASH facilities, for example during menstruation. This is further complicated by cultural taboos around menstruation which can result in women sharing latrines with men, especially during menstruation, thereby limiting women and girl’s access to WASH services at school. In many countries, girls are forced out of school when they start menstruating or miss school because of inadequate toilets facilities. As primary users of health services, women and girls face increased risk of infection and disease as a result of poor WASH facilities in health centres.

Although most affected by limitations in the WASH system, women and girls have had limited influence over the planning and implementation of WASH projects. This is partly because they are often excluded from public consultation spaces or silenced when their voices when they are invited in and on their needs and priorities for WASH services. Women’s participation in decision-making processes around WASH provision is a major challenge in both government and NGO WASH services. Where women are found in the WASH sector workforce, they are more likely to be in junior or unpaid roles, although pioneering initiatives have been made by some NGOs.

### Wetlands, Coastal and Marine Ecosystems
Relevant evidence identified through literature searches for the gender assessment speaks to: (i) women and men’s use of and interaction with wetland, coastal and marine areas; (ii) how climate change is affecting these areas and their users; and (iii) how women and men are involved in decision-making processes around different ways in which men and women are, or are not, and could be involved in the protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine areas.

Key findings include that, as regards their participation in ocean-based industries (of which most of the relevant evidence is from the fisheries sector), women are disproportionately represented in informal and unregulated economies and concentrated in less profitable parts of value chains. Women’s participation is curtailed by a range of factors including lack of access to resources, limited decision-making power within other community members from viewing them as “real fishers”. Women’s participation in the fishery industry is often overlooked and undervalued, resulting in the marginalisation of women who do participate in the management of wetland, coastal and marine areas.


---
**Page 116**
---

At the same time, and whilst the evidence base on the gendered impacts of unsustainable fishing practices is underdeveloped, it is women who are likely to be disproportionately affected by rising sea temperatures, levels and acidification.

As regards gender mainstreaming in sustainability and management initiatives, women’s participation is currently very limited – a hindrance for the improvement of, for example, fishing sustainability because women tend to be more willing to engage in progressive sustainability practices. Policy and decision making remains dominated by men who are less likely to consider the gendered impacts of their decisions and evidence on gender-differentiated effects of innovation and technological advancements lacking. Unsuccessful or poorly designed women’s empowerment interventions, tend to take a segregated ‘glass filling’ approach which fails to address underlying structural barriers, perpetuating discriminatory norms and gendered inequalities.

Ports

The evidence base on the intersection between gender, climate change and port infrastructure is particularly limited, primarily focusing on () the different ways in which men and women use and benefit from available port infrastructure, as well as () those in which men and women are, and are not and could be involved in the development of new ports.

Women are not able to fully reap the benefits of economic growth from ports development due to their exclusion from related employment and, particularly, from operational and managerial positions. At the same time, there is a lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of port’s negative impacts on ecosystems.

The development and expansion of new climate-resilient ports creates jobs and therefore opens up opportunities to train and hire women. Women’s recruitment, integration and progression is starting to be supported by some initiatives in this area but more needs to be done to improve access for women who have an important role to play in promoting climate-smart port development for the good of wider communities – but continue to be marginalized in discussions around new approaches. In the same vein, case on gender-differentiated benefits of optimised infrastructure and energy efficiency remains scant.

Context Analysis in Focus Countries 1

As part of this gender assessment, 16-in-depth country analyses were completed to understand the national policy environment for women’s economic empowerment, as well as to identify relevant issues, opportunities and challenges at the national level to drive more equal outcomes. These analyses present a top-line picture of access to WASH facilities and look at the WASH context to understand how gender is addressed throughout. Summaries of these country analyses are provided in Annex 4 and full details for all the focus countries included in Annex 5, including a stakeholder mapping of the WASH sector.

Overall, most countries have access to improved drinking water for around half their population. However, few sub-Saharan African countries achieve this level of access. The same analysis suggests that the rate of access to different quality levels of service (e.g. either safety managed or basic drinking water services) varies more significantly. In terms of sanitation, access to improved facilities is more varied between countries with some countries having universal coverage and others still working while others are still working to half of their population access to improved sanitation facilities.


1 This section is based on detailed gender and energy profiles for each programming country. Key insights as they relate to the renewable energy sector are included in (Annex A).


---
**Page 117**
---

Draft Report

The policy frameworks across different focal countries address a range of aspects related to water, sanitation and hygiene. All of them place a strong emphasis on the impact of climate change in exacerbating already existing inequalities. However, it is important to note that while all countries have recognized that climate change exacerbates the already challenging WASH landscape for women. At the same time, the degree to which women are affected by water policy and practice varies significantly.

For instance, in Kenya, both men and women identify opportunities and challenges related to water issues at different levels; very Coasts' are relatively gender blind and do not propose how to generate gender equality in their WASH projects. In contrast, some of the other countries have identified gender issues in water and sanitation but fail to explore in much detail what role they can play or what opportunities exist for them.

In terms of policy priorities, policies commonly identify a need to create more equal participation and outcomes for women. The notion of inclusion is echoed in national development plans, as well as more specific gender, water, health, and climate change plans. At the same time, many countries do not identify concrete actions that will lead to this inclusion. This Gender Assessment aims to provide a set of common entry points across focal countries to improve the position for women, including greater participation in decision-making processes at all levels (national, regional, local) and in specific areas (such as Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM)) and ensuring that women and young people are consulted and have sufficient voice in policy and investment decisions.

Overview of Gender Action Areas for C12

This Gender Assessment brings together the analysis of the priorities, opportunities and challenges faced by women and girls in relation to water and sanitation projects and community development projects (in country contexts). Building on this assessment, the Gender Action Plan annexed to this Assessment in Annex 3 (available in Section B) identifies action area and entry points C12 and its Project Companies can leverage to enhance participation and equal outcomes for women in C12 WASH projects in these sectors.

Improving women's and girls' access

C12 and its Project Companies will use the investment projects and community development projects to address the physical, financial, attitudinal and safety barriers that limit women’s access to WASH and Oceans Infrastructure.

Using a gender mainstreaming processes (such as Environment and Social Impact Assessment, community needs assessment and targeted Gender Assessments), C12 will explicitly consider the barriers which affect women and girls' access, specifically focusing on indigenous households and women and girls with disabilities. The Gender Action Plan annexed to this Assessment in Annex 3 (available in Section B) identifies entry points for investment projects (and will monitor progress in this area). C12 will seek to use community development projects as a means of addressing the barriers which limit women's access, while also addressing identified barriers which cannot be addressed through the main investment. Examples of potential actions include working with families and communities to build their understanding of and support for good WASH practices and improving water points to ensure access. Additional projects are outlined in the Gender Action Plan.

Giving women voice in the planning, design and implementation of WASH facilities

As a priority beneficiary group for C12 WASH projects, it is essential that stakeholder engagement and consultation with women and girls takes into account their views and experiences of water and sanitation. This is to ensure that investment projects and community development projects can be responsive, as far as possible, to the WASH needs of women and girls. Though public participation mechanisms are in place for many countries, these often devalue and silence women's voices, even when public consultation mechanisms are in place.

## CFM and its Project Companies will adopt an inclusive stakeholder engagement approach that provides conducive spaces for women and girls to voice their needs and experiences, including identifying women and girl leaders in WASH enterprises and communities.

### Community-led WASH Management Committees

In some projects, community-led WASH management committees may play a role in the management of WASH facilities. These committees are often led by men who have little or no experience with WASH issues in a way that provides space for women's voices to be heard, including by identifying and recruiting to the committee local women who are persuaded (by men and other women) to have some legitimacy in speaking out on their behalf.

### Gender Equality Action Plan

Additional action points to ensure women and girls have an equal voice are outlined in the Gender Action Plan.

## Providing women with formal employment opportunities in the WASH sector

Low levels of secondary education completion among girls in several of the G2 focal countries combined with gender norms that limit acceptable active roles for women can make the formal employment of women in the WASH sector challenging.

### CFM Project Companies will be proactive in hiring a gender-balanced team, making adjustments to working practices and policies as necessary. In particular, CFM recognises that in several focal countries there are systemic issues beyond CFM's control which are likely to limit the Company's ability to recruit senior female staff. In such contexts, CFM will scope out opportunities for women to be employed at lower levels of responsibility or in areas where they can embark on tertiary education in relevant Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) fields.

### At the project level

Project Companies will identify employment opportunities for women in WASH enterprises and put in place appropriate mechanisms to recruit, train and support women in these roles. Opportunities may include recruitment of female workers in areas such as water supply, sanitation, construction or cleaning, among others. Additional action points are outlined in the Gender Action Plan.

### Creating an enabling environment for gender mainstreaming in the G2 Fund and the Project Companies

CFM recognises the need to draw on specialist skills and advice to guide the company's leadership on how to operate in a manner that is inclusive of women, girls and other underrepresented groups. This is expected to be achieved through a network of gender and inclusion specialists who can be hired on an as-needed basis.

### CFM will support Project Companies to put in place essential internal systems which are gender-responsive and gender-transformative, and capacities that will facilitate gender mainstreaming through the project and its operations. This may include developing a Local (Project-specific) gender action plan, agreeing and implementing a Project-specific Gender Equality Action Plan, and ensuring that all staff have access to training on gender equality.

### Additional measures

Project Companies also take steps to require their principal contractors to give due attention to gender issues in their own operations (such as developing an Operational Gender Equality Policy and recruiting a gender-balanced team). Further commitments are set out in the Gender Action Plan.

# Empowerment

The process through which people take correct and action in order to overcome gender inequality which have adversely put them in a disadvantaged position.

## Gender

Gender refers to the social differences between male and female bodies. Gender, on the other hand, refers to the socially constructed attitudes, values, roles and responsibilities that are associated with being male or female. These include beliefs, values and roles as influenced by perceptions and expectations arising from cultural norms, religious beliefs, laws, policies, institutions, education systems, social class, ethnicity and individual and institutional bias. Gender attitudes and behaviors are learnt and change over time.

## Gender analysis

This is the process of examining a situation in order to identify differences in the gender roles, activities, needs, and available opportunities of men and women. It helps to identify the barriers that prevent men and women from addressing gender issues within the problem which being addressed and/or the obstacles to progress towards achieving gender equality. Gender analysis is important in project objectives, in the choice of intervention strategy and in the methods of project implementation.

## Gender-aware policy

A policy which takes into account the social relationships of women and men as well as their different roles, responsibilities and needs. A gender-aware approach explicitly assumes that gender is an influencing factor in projects.

## Gender blindness

This is a situation where there is no consideration given to the differences between men and women in terms of their roles, responsibilities and needs. A gender-blind approach assumes that gender is not an influencing factor in projects.

## Gender division of labour

Is an overall societal pattern where women are allotted one set of gender roles and responsibilities while men are allotted another. This results in a situation in which there is an unequal gender division of reward. Discrimination against women often takes the form of lower wages, fewer opportunities for promotion to the unpaid labor, but men collect most of the income and rewards resulting from labor. In many countries, the most obvious pattern in the gender division of labor is that women are more likely than men to be involved in domestic work while men dominate in agriculture in crop production and waged employment.

## Gender equality

This refers to a situation where there is no discrimination against either sex in terms of access to resources or benefits, or in the access to services. Equality exists when both men and women have equal opportunities for education, training, employment, responsibilities, and have equal access to the means (resources, opportunities) to improve their living conditions. Gender equality does not necessarily mean that there is equality of opportunity, or equality of results.

## Gender equity

Gender equity is the process of being fair to women and men. To ensure fairness, measures must be taken to address the different roles, responsibilities and disadvantages that prevent men and women from otherwise operating on a level playing field in terms of access to resources and opportunities.

## Gender gap

A gender gap is an observable and sometimes measurable gap between men and women with respect to their access to education, health care, employment, attendance at school or participation in the labor force, which is understood to be due to discrimination against one sex. The gender gap can be measured by comparing priorities of women and men are integrated into all policies, programs and

# CLIMATE INVESTORS 2

## CLIMATE FUND MANAGERS

| Projects: It requires: (a) continuous assessment of the implications and effects of energy-related aspects and policies on women and men; (b) designing and implementing strategies and actions that aim to address the specific needs, interests, and priorities of women and men, improve their well-being, and facilitate their participation in the decision-making process. If a measure addressing this is done for one gender only or reporting to managers,
| Gender-neutral and gender blind |
| --- | --- |
| Gender relations |
| Gender roles |

Gender-neutral policies are not specifically aimed at women and men but are assumed to affect both sexes equally, however they may actually be gender-blind. They do not take into account the different needs, interests, and priorities of women and men. Gender-blind policies are based on assumptions that all people have the same (male) needs and interests. Gender-neutral or blind assume those affected by the policy have the same (male) needs and interests.

Gender roles are sets of behaviors assigned to men and women respectively, involving different expectations about what activities, tasks and responsibilities are perceived as male and which female. Gender roles are also shaped by cultural norms, socialization processes, economic and political environment. These roles shape identity, determining how we see ourselves and others in relation to gender, and the way we interact with each other as men and women. Changes in gender roles often occur in response to changing economic, social or political conditions.

# Background to the Assessment

Climate Fund-Managers (CFM) is preparing a funding proposal for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to be presented to the GCF Board in June 2022. If successful, CFM will use its financing facility Climate Invest 2 (CI2) to support projects and programs that promote gender equality and the empowerment of women through their investments.

Both GCF and CFM are committed to promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women through their investments. By adopting a gender mainstreaming approach in investments, GCF and CFM expect to achieve greater impact on both women and men by addressing the barriers they face but also to benefit women and men equally, reducing any gender differentials associated with vulnerabilities exacerbated by climate change and promoting sustainable and equitable development.

This assessment has been commissioned to inform CFM's funding proposal to GCF. It is in line with the assignment of the Task Team on Water and sanitation only. The assessment will provide information on how women and men are affected by problems in three main areas: (i) access to and management of water and sanitation services; (ii) vulnerability to climate change impacts, including loss of wetlands and marine ecosystems; and (iii) the development of climate resilient port infrastructure. The specific issues covered under each area are summarised in Table 1 below.

## Water and Sanitation

### Women and men, girls' and boys' access to and use of WASH services, the barriers they encounter, including gender-based violence, and how climate change is affecting these

- How women and men, girls and boys are affected by my challenges in accessing quality WASH
- The roles that men and women, boys and girls play in the WASH sector, including formal employment opportunities
- Women and girls' participation and leadership in the design, planning and management of water and sanitation services
- National policy relating to gender, water and sanitation
- Stakeholder analysis
- WASH problem tree analysis

### Wetlands and coastal marine ecosystems

- How climate change is affecting these different users and how this will impact on the populations concerned
- The roles that men and women, boys and girls play in wetlands and are, and could be involved in the protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine areas
- Stakeholder analysis

### Port infrastructure

- The different ways in which men and women use and benefit from available port infrastructure
- The roles that men and women, boys and girls play in which men and women are not, and are not could be involved in and can benefit from the development of climate resilient port infrastructure
- Problem tree analysis

# Climate Fund Managers

## Findings from the assessment have informed the development of a Gender Action Plan, which sets out how CFM will mainstream gender and inclusive approaches in order to promote gender equality through the C2 investment and supported projects.

This report presents a gender assessment of the three areas of investment interest. It has been based on over two iterations. The first iteration provided a high-level assessment of gender issues in the water and sanitation sector, including countries such as Bangladesh, India, Indonesia, Nepal, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Djibouti, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Morocco, Namibia Nigeria and Uganda. Responding to GCF feedback, the second iteration focused on the three areas of investment interest: water supply and sanitation; high-level global gender assessment of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems and port infrastructure, stakeholder mapping and engagement for the original 8 priority countries, as well as country level context analysis for an additional 3 priority countries: Botswana, Iraq and Israel.

### 1.1 This report

The findings from the gender assessment are presented in this report in the following eight sections. We start by briefly outlining the methodology used in preparing the assessment (Section 2).

In Section 3, we describe, at a global level, the main gender issues identified in the water and sanitation sector. These include women’s access to safe drinking water and sanitation services affected by climate change, drawing on evidence from academic papers and practitioner literature.

In Sections 4 and 5, we conduct a similar exercise in the areas of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems and port infrastructure development.

In Section 6 we analyse the policy context and data in 11 focal countries in which we describe the policy direction in relation to WASH, gender and climate change, and identify entry points at the national level for C2 investment. These include Bangladesh, India, Indonesia, Nepal, Pakistan, those designated priority countries 1 and 2, and the context analysis also includes a mapping of key stakeholders in the WASH sector from national to community levels.

In Section 7 we present an overview of the country-level analysis into problem tree analyses which unpack the causes and effects of inadequate infrastructure and services for women and girls across the C2 investment areas.

In Section 8, we summarise the problem tree analysis and the evidence received to identify four opportunities for C2 to promote gender equality through large scale investment in WASH, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystem protection and climate resilient port infrastructure development.

In the final section (Section 9), we provide an overview of key indicators to monitor the results and impact of gender mainstreaming activities.

# Gender Assessment methodology

The methodology for this Gender Assessment and the subsequent Gender Action Plan has been guided by GCF’s approach to gender mainstreaming (Figure 2). As part of it, we have conducted gender analysis of climate change policies and strategies, including national climate change adaptation plans, ecosystems and port infrastructure, national policies for priority countries and available quantitative datasets; mapped country level stakeholders; and used this evidence to provide the narrative and problem tree for the Gender Assessment. This information/data gathering has been used to inform a Gender Action Plan, which will guide GCF’s gender mainstreaming efforts in C2. This document provides more in detail the methodology used in preparing the Gender Assessment.

Review of global evidence in WASH, the protection of wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems and girls' education

For the review of global evidence, we identified relevant search terms/keywords for the three thematic areas. These included: "gender," "women," "boys," "girls," "water," "sanitation," "hygiene," "feminist", "climate change", "wetlands", "coastal and marine ecosystems", "protected areas", "safe ports", "climate resilient port" and "climate change". These terms were used to manually search (through trailing different combinations of search terms) search engines and the websites of the organizations involved in the project. The search was conducted on 15th June, 2023.

Through this, we identified relevant online resources from academic and practice-focused sources, which were then used to identify additional sources by reviewing reference lists within the literature found.

A total of 85 studies were identified and reviewed, including 71 focused on WASH, nine on wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems and eight (two being used for both) on port infrastructure (See Annex I for bibliographic details). The review was conducted in a systematic manner using pre-defined inclusion criteria and selected practitioner literature. To narrow down the extensive literature on WASH, the review focused primarily on studies that were published after 2015, as this is when C4F's focus shifted to as higher levels of service, in line with C4F’s focus on infrastructure. Although hygiene is not specified in the assignment TDR, improved water and sanitation infrastructure will facilitate better hygiene practices.

The global evidence review found that, with one notable exception (the Water for Women Fund), research with a comprehensive and combined focus on gender, WASH and climate is relatively sparse. More studies have focused on the impacts of climate change on WASH services than those focusing on gendered impacts on WASH services. The exceptions here are the Water for Women studies which focused on rural settings and community-managed WASH systems in the East Asia and Pacific region. In WASH, there has been a focus on the effects of climate change on water supply infrastructure and the effects over time. For urban settings, utility level reports, complaint registers and WASH programme evaluations have been used to assess the impacts.

The global evidence review also found that most literature on wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems is largely gender blind. Most reports primarily examined benefits and impacts from an economic and biodiversity perspective, with limited discussion of benefits and impacts for people. When people were discussed, they were often in relation to economic benefits, such as those relating to tourism and fishing, with little consideration of social impacts.

*The content analysis concentrated on WASH only, and did not include wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems or port infrastructure, areas that were covered through global dataset analysis.


---
**Page 127**
---

# Policy Analysis in focal countries

This analysis focused on national policies, strategies and action plans in the 11 focal countries in the following areas: overarching national development, sanitation, hygiene, gender, climate change, water and education. The policy review covers a total of 65 national documents, including seven for Bangladesh, five for Botswana, nine for Brazil, six for Djibouti, four for Indonesia, four for Kenya, three for Madagascar, two for Malawi, one for Mozambique, one for Namibia, and one for Uganda (see full list of policies reviewed in Annex 2). All of them were sourced online. Most were in English, but those from Djibouti, Ivory Coast and Morocco were largely in French and those for Brazil were largely in Portuguese.

Once collated, key data on core areas of interest (Box 1) was extracted from the policies into a spreadsheet and then analysed drawing out the overarching WASH policy direction, the extent to which gender issues are considered and tackled in WASH policy, and how climate change is affecting all of this.


## Box 1. Areas of interest in the policy analysis

1. What are the main water and sanitation and hygiene challenges identified?
2. How do these policies seek to address them?
3. How are men, women, boys and girls affected differently by these challenges?
4. What are the main health consequences associated with the water and sanitation and hygiene challenges identified in Box 1?
5. What are the main policy objectives in water and sanitation and hygiene?
6. How do these policy objectives seek to address the impact of climate change?
7. What are the measures taken by policies to ensure that men, women, boys and girls play a role in the water and sanitation and hygiene section, and in adapting water and sanitation and hygiene management strategies to climate change?
8. Does the policy framework encourage and support women and girls to pursue careers in areas relevant to water and sanitation systems development and management?
9. What are the roles of institutions for overseeing and implementing the policy objectives outlined and what are their roles?

## Quantitative data analysis

Quantitative data on population access to WASH facilities and access to WASH facilities in schools and health centres has largely been drawn from the WHO and the UNICEF JMP (2014). This data has been complemented with information from other sources, such as national statistical offices, NGOs, and for data on women's education levels, from UNESCO’s Institute of Statistics. The latter is to understand the extent to which women may be equipped to access formal employment within the water sector and to contribute towards WASH service provision and protection; we draw on SDG5+4 reporting, but very little if it tracked men’s and women’s engagement.

# Stakeholder Mapping

The stakeholder mapping was done through a series of interviews with key sector players in eight of the priority countries. Relevant actors were identified using the team’s personal contacts and through online searches. The interviewees were selected on the basis of their role within government ministries in charge of water, sanitation, hygiene, gender and climate change. CSOs working on gender and/or WASH issues were also included.

The interviews were conducted by email or phone. Interviewers sent a letter to request a short interview. A total of 50 individuals were contacted in the eight priority countries, 29 of whom responded and were interviewed (Table 3).

Stakeholder interviews were used to explore three main issues:

- Current efforts to promote gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure development
- The main actors involved in promoting gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure development and their roles, and
- Stakeholders’ perceptions of the opportunities for C2 investments in their country and feedback on the opportunities identified in the first iteration of the Gender Assessment.

In addition to these annexes, we have used these findings to develop problem tree analyses for each of the three thematic areas, all of which can be found in section 8.

# Analysis Process to Identify Opportunities

This analysis process to identify these opportunities involved two steps. First, as part of the first iteration of this Gender Assessment, we used the findings from the literature review of gender issues in WASH to identify opportunities for improving water and sanitation services. The team then expanded on these using the country analysis in an initial set of 8 focal countries. Overall, there was a good alignment between the two sets of opportunities.

The second step involved combining the two sets of opportunities identified from the literature review of gender issues in the protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems, and in the development of port infrastructure, to identify opportunities for improving these services. This resulted in 10 opportunities that were not already identified for WASH and the two have been combined.

This integrated set of opportunities forms the basis of the material presented in section 6 of the report.

## Limitations

### Availability of Global Evidence on the Protection of Wetlands, Coastal and Marine Ecosystems and Associated Port Infrastructure

The existing evidence base on gender as it relates to oceans and, particularly, port infrastructure is extremely small. Many qualitative reports reviewed were gender-blind and did not explore how benefits from these services are distributed between men and women or how they have differential impacts for boys and girls, with children in general referred to only in relation to food security.

More broadly, most reports primarily examined benefits and impacts from an economic and biodiversity perspective. While some of the qualitative reports reviewed did discuss gender issues, this was mostly associated with economic benefits, such as those relating to tourism and fishing. In addition to the literature review of gender issues in coastal and marine ecosystems authored by IRIOD and other issue-focused organisations who collaborated through groups such as Ocean Panel and WorldFish, many reports are sector specific, particularly fisheries and, to a lesser extent, tourism.

The literature on good practices for involving women in, and maximising benefits from them, for the protection of wetland, coastal and marine areas or the development of climate resilient infrastructure is also limited.

### Availability of National Policies

The research team has encountered some challenges in accessing required data. For example, it has not been possible to access Water and Sanitation policies that are not accessible online. This has led to some significant omissions; for example, we have not been able to access Water and Sanitation policies for Nigeria and Uganda, and we have only been able to review three policies for Ivory Coast. Similarly, there are no policies available on coastal and marine ecosystems or port infrastructure.

Furthermore, the gender analysis in policies can be quite sparse, suggesting that a full consideration of the gender issues in WASH has not been done at the policy level.

**Page 130**
---

Page 127

# Time pressures for conducting the stakeholder mapping

With only three weeks to prepare the stakeholder mapping, the scope for engaging stakeholders has been extremely tight. It is positive that 29 stakeholders have been identified but there are gaps; we have not yet completed a full mapping of all stakeholders in each country. However, we have already conducted a stakeholder mapping for the three countries added in the second iteration of the Gender Assessment, namely Brazil, Botswana and Indonesia, for which we do/seen better data through the late in the process.

# Global Evidence Review: WASH & Climate Change

In this Section, we present findings from the global evidence review exploring the main gender issues in WASH. It considers the following questions, identified as the more pertinent issues based on our review of the literature:

- Women and men's 'girls' and boys' access to and use of WASH services, the barriers they encounter, including gender-based violence, and how climate change is affecting these.
- How women and girls are affected by climate change in relation to their access to and use of WASH.
- The roles that men and women, boys and girls play in the WASH sector, including formal employment opportunities; Women's participation, representation and leadership in the design, planning and management of WASH infrastructure and services.

## Differences in WASH access and consequences

Women and girls are largely responsible for household water collection and/or cleaning, yet they are often excluded from their ability to decide how to use WASH facilities. In 2015, WaterAid estimated that WASH-related diseases were the fifth biggest killer worldwide, causing more deaths than AIDS, diabetes or breast cancer. This section outlines the differences in access to and use of water supply, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) services between men and women, boys and girls, which may influence which access to WASH facilities are summarised in Table 3 below.

### Access to water in household settings

Fetching water is a physically challenging task and is typically carried out by the women and children. It has increased risks of injury from potential injuries, including spinal damage, burns and prolapse, as well as an increased risk of miscarriage[1]. Distance to reaching water and sanitation facilities makes people vulnerable to physical or verbal abuse, rape and assault.[2] Other risks include rough terrain, lack of lighting at night, and the need for women to travel alone. Water collection can affect people's quality of life in a number of ways; time devoted to water-related tasks limits women’s ability to participate fully in other activities.

Increasing access to sufficient quantities of water can also contribute to better hygiene (such as handwashing with soap or face washing) and thus improve health (in terms of diarrhoea and respiratory infections). Women have an increased need for water for hydration, sanitation and hygiene practices. This is particularly true when they are pregnant, breastfeeding, caring for the elderly or young children.[3] Limited supplies of water, as well as competing water needs in the household, can trigger conflict between men and women. In some cases, this may lead to a situation where one partner/family member puts partner/family water needs above their own to avoid abuse. There is some evidence that household piped water helps reduce intra-household conflict[4]. Examples of sextortion (the practice


---
**Page 132**
---

# Climate Fund Managers

## Access to sanitation facilities in household settings

Many communities have strict cultural norms around men and women sharing household or public toilets. Women are often barred from women-only latrines during menstruation; this often compels open defecation. Social expectations of modesty mean that women should not be seen urinating, so they may hold urine until they can find a private place to go. This often results in intake of food/drink (taking anti-diarrhoea tablets). Poor sanitation practices (such as open defecation) and WASH-related diseases can induce stress during pregnancy and may also contribute to malnutrition. Women who are pregnant or breastfeeding have more frequent urination, fewer cubs for women (leading to longer queues), and women use toilets more frequently than men, making them more likely to be caught in the act of defecation. Older women (especially older women) or incontinence (older women) are limited in their ability to travel far from home[1]. Dehydration from not drinking due to poor latrine access (especially for women and girls) can affect personal hygiene, health, and productivity.

Men tend not always to use a toilet for defecation; either when they are at home or away. A range of reasons are cited for this, including: to slow the rate of the pit filling, men having more time available for open defecation than women (especially in urban areas), and reverting to OD when household toilets are in high demand[2]. Open defecation is considered less risky for men regarding security/safety concerns and perhaps, in some cases, less shame about OD. Male urine often attracts flies and other insects, which can spread disease through it can offer public decency; and is often prohibited though by laws and punishable with fines[3].

# Safety

- **Women's access to preferred water sources:** desired water quantity and water treatment methods.
- **Women may report spending money to access water sources with shorter wait times or higher quality**, or to compensate male neighbours, labours or drivers for fetching water.
- **Using public pay-per-use toilets mean women incur higher costs than men due to more frequent need.**
- **Consequences of poor sanitation and waste collection:** can lead to conflicts because of husbands suspecting wives of infidelity.
- **Inadequate access to sanitation (when toilets lack doors, locks, lights or are far away from homes) increases women's vulnerability to various forms of violence by men**. This includes taunting, teasing, name-calling and throwing things at them, especially when they go to latrines and open defecation sites, especially after dark.
- **Women may accompany one another or asked men to accompany them for safety when open defecating:** Women might only let their daughters go out for open defecation at night or go with other women.
- **Women use make-shift arrangements (like potties) within the household to avoid going outside.**
- **Quarrels over contributions toward maintenance of shared toilets affects ability in using toilets.**

# Attitudinal

- **Women are often unable to use sanitation facilities or touch water when menstruating due to perceptions that menstruation is polluting:** Latrines around menstrual waste disposal areas are not used by women.
- **Menstrual hygiene is linked to emotional and psychosocial stress, resulting in feelings of shame and disgust.**
- **Participation in a water project can lead to intra-household conflict and violence**:
  - Husbands become angry or physically abusive when women take too long to collect water, when there was not enough water available for bathing and other personal hygiene needs.
  - Women and girls report shame related to menstruation and menstruation behaviours and experiences.

# Access to hygiene in household settings

Globally, men are reportedly less likely to consistently wash their hands with soap at critical times. In households where women are the primary targets of hand hygiene promotion through WASH programmes (often in their role as mothers). Women play a key role in supporting the hand hygiene of family members, for instance, by ensuring soap and water is available for hand hygiene or reminding family members to wash their hands before eating or after using the toilet. Women, in their role as


---
**Page 134**
---

community health promoters, also play a role in supporting this wider practice within the community.

The Global Scaling Up Handwashing project[1] in Senegal included men in handwashing promotion discussions. The project aimed to (1) increase access to water for soap; (2) men can follow up the outreach session to ensure that household members wash their hands with soap and water after defecation, and sustain behavioural change by washing their hands with soap while encouraging others to do the same.

Adequate menstrual hygiene (MH) relies on using a clean menstrual management material to absorb or collect menstrual blood. It can be changed in privacy as often as necessary for the duration of a menstrual period. MH is also dependent upon access to safe and convenient facilities to dispose of used menstrual management materials[1] (joint Programme for Safe Water, Sanitation and Hygiene). Clean water is a fundamental aspect of safe and dignified menstrual hygiene. Water is needed for (1) washing hands with soap and water; (2) washing of reusable menstrual products like menstrual cloths. MH is limited by inadequate access to water to wash hands and other personal hygiene items, lack of privacy in the home or absence of a private place to wash or change products, poor hygiene awareness and problems arising from stigma and taboo.

Access to WASH in health facilities and schools

Health facilities

According to the JMP[2] (2020), a quarter of health care facilities (HCF) lacked basic water services in 2019. HCF may rely on handpumps on their premises for their water supply. If the handpump stops working, staff have to collect water from nearby communities. Smaller facilities may not have the systems or capacity to store enough water for all users. In some cases, especially if there is no caretaker, medical staff must collect water daily (or more often) for use in the HCF. In times of shortage, they may also need to collect water for other purposes such as cooking and cleaning as well as water for bathing. Water quality may also not be sufficient, and handpumps may not always have the quality of water necessary for use.

Integrating handwashing/basic hygiene is fundamental to primary health care and the prevention of Hospital Acquired Infections. Although clinic staff may have knowledge of the importance of handwashing, they may not practice it themselves or provide it to patients. A study in 2019 found that three HCFs did not have hand hygiene facilities at the point of care. During the COVID-19 pandemic, handwashing became a priority for health facilities and staff, including all visitors, adhering to COVID-19 measures. Health facilities may lack sufficient numbers of functional sanitation facilities, separate for staff and patients. In 2015, 10% of health care facilities had no sanitation facilities at the point of care. The WHO recommends that handwashing facilities meet requirements be accessible for use; be affordable and available when needed; be well maintained and managed by trained personnel; and be used by all visitors and parents. There would also be sufficient numbers of cloths for men and women to reduce queues[3].

Increasingly, development partners are supporting improved WASH in HCF with improved WASH infrastructure and training on proper handwashing and handwashing stations. In particular, WASH can also support dignity in childbirth: a survey by the White Ribbon Alliance (2019) found that women


---
**Page 135**
---

# Climate Fund Managers

## Want WASH in health care facilities for a dignified birth experience: expectations include running water, indoor toilets, clean beds and sheets.

### Schools

Globally, 69% of schools had a basic drinking water service in 2013 while 52% had a basic sanitation service. While there are global and national standard ratios for public latrines, many schools are unable to provide enough toilets for pupils, or their limited budget means that materials are not always provided in schools such as clean toilets or to ensure good cleaning methods and soap are always available. Soap should be included in school budgets but is often overlooked because it costs money to contribute funds.

WASH in schools continues to have a positive impact on child health, thereby reducing absences from school. WASH services in schools can be an incentive for parents to send their children to school, particularly girls in the transition from primary to secondary schools. Adequate consumption of water at school is also important for hydration, which has a number of health benefits as well as benefits for learning (Hunter et al., 2015).

A lack of WASH at home can impact girls’ education, for instance, when they are enlisted to help with water or sanitation-related tasks at home. Where there is no caretaker, students and teachers have a high risk of contracting diseases such as cholera and typhoid fever. In some countries, instead of school, they may also be asked to collect water for the school or clean toilets instead of being in class.

There is a gender gap when it comes to WASH services at home, but this is not always the case; however, they are less impacted than girls. Overcrowding in schools has been associated with both boys and girls avoiding toilets. Students avoid using the toilet due to the anxiety of using a filthy latrine, nausea from poor air quality or fear of being seen by others.

Globally, 57% of schools had a basic hand hygiene service in 2019. Handwashing in schools is promoted through a number of WASH-in-school programmes and by school Health Clubs that promote health among students and the community. Female teachers are often the main champions of handwashing with soap in the school environment.

Self-reporting by girls suggests that inadequate toilet facilities in schools (lack of toilets, filthy toilets, insufficient water and limited access to menstrual materials) are a contributory factor for truancy, falling into early marriage or pregnancy, and dropping out of school. Girls also report being verbally abused in staff retention/return rates. WASH programmes are increasingly tackling taboos and stigmas about menstruation through education and increasing as well availability of WASH facilities at school by, for example, providing menstrual cups.

### 3.2 Effects of climate change on access to and use of WASH services

At its most basic level, changing weather patterns affect WASH facilities by reducing water availability and damaging WASH infrastructure (Figure 1). Low-income households are more likely to be situated in areas prone to flooding or droughts where toilets may become unusable during floods. They are also more likely to use WASH facilities that can be easily destroyed or disrupted by climate hazards.


---
**Page 136**
---

The poorest typically have the least capacity to cope with and adapt to shocks[1]. This section reviews the general impacts of climate change on access to and use of WASH services, before reviewing the specific effects on water, sanitation and hygiene services.

Figure 3: How WASH issues caused by climate change are exacerbated by other urban challenges. WUSF, 2021

| | |
|---|---|
| **Low quality infrastructure** | **Poor maintenance** |
| | |
| | |
| | |
| | |

The literature review suggests that climate change affects access to and use of WASH facilities in five main ways:

- **Nuisance and disruption:** Disruption to residents is an immediate implication of climate-related damage to WASH infrastructure. For instance, flooding could lead to sewage back-up in homes connected to the sewerage network, overflowing pit latrines, as well as flooding of homes and businesses. The maintenance and repair of WASH systems can also be affected by the nuisance caused by the maintenance and repair of WASH systems and disruption to WASH services caused by climate change, for instance, in ensuring the cleanliness of water points during heavy rainfall or ensuring that latrines are not likely to cause temporary closure of markets, roads and leading to disruption for women in particular.

- **WASH health outcomes:** Waterborne diseases are predicted to become more prevalent due to an increase in temperature and humidity. The increased temperatures associated with climate change lead to the release of pathogens from water sources into drinking water supplies. Warmer water also leads to faster decomposition of organic matter, which results in higher levels of faecal sludge or wastewater posing an immediate threat to public health, including faecal-oral disease and vector-borne diseases. Increased faecal sludge contamination is associated with health risks such as diarrhoea, cholera, typhoid fever, dysentery, and malaria. Women are more likely to be caring for sick with WASH-related diseases and elderly relatives within households.
