# Climate Fund Managers

- Elevated levels of depression and other mental health outcomes: Water insecurity and scarcity, as well as open defecation, are considered significant stressors for individuals.
- Women and girls are particularly vulnerable to water-related stressors because they often bear the burden of collecting water. This is particularly the case for older women.
- High stress levels are a result of perceived unfairness in water distribution. Lack of access to safe drinking water can lead to increased rates of illness, reduced functionality, and reduced mental health for residents.

## Increased WASH costs: Inadequate WASH can also increase financial strain
When men are the decision makers over household budgets, this can increase stress for women.
- Climate-related damage to WASH infrastructure will require increased maintenance, repair and replacement. This will lead to increased water service fees and water quality management costs that are likely to be passed on to the household through tariffs.

## Loss of income due to climate events
- Extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and storms can cause damage to homes, schools, WASH facilities, and businesses.
- This can lead to increased clean-up costs for households. Businesses that require water for operations (e.g., catering) are also likely to be affected by loss of income.

## Access to water in household settings
Following extreme weather events, water scarcity can result from resource limitations (i.e., physical hydrological constraints or affordability constraints).

### Damage to piped water networks
- Damage to piped water networks means that water is supplied through mobile tankers instead.
- Women and girls will have to queue to collect the water which is then stored in the household and its use can be rationed. Women typically have higher water needs for hygiene and are thus impacted more. This can lead to increased stress levels, especially if they are already stressed due to other factors such as lack of access to sanitation.
- When men use too much water for bathing or when children spill water. Households without many storage containers will be particularly affected by this. This can also increase demand for drinking water with the poorest city residents particularly at risk due to the phenomenon of urban poverty.

### The poor and women
The poorest people in cities, already reliant on intermittent services, are most affected by water shortages. Augmentation strategies include the use of multiple water sources to meet household


---
**Page 138**
---

water needs, and this has implications for water quality, cost and the time required to collect water. Residents might have to pay private water providers for additional water deliveries or find other private water sources such as wells or boreholes. To save costs, the poorest may use unsafe sources such as surface water or hand-dug/shallow wells. Increasingly, households are using rainwater harvesting systems and/or water tanks. However, those who are less likely to have resources to store water (e.g., safe storage tanks). Water borrowing from neighbours is a strategy to overcome access constraints, particularly for those with longer round-trips to both collect and transport water. This has implications for the quality of water used at home, fear and indebtedness.

Access to sanitation facilities in household settings

Climate change will exacerbate existing weaknesses in sanitation services. In many cities, little of a household's sewage reaches the treatment plant due to poor design or poor management, or as a result of inadequate maintenance and operation of the sewerage system and/or the wastewater treatment plant. Instead, faecal sludge is dumped or discharged into open drains, bodies of water and the surrounding environment. These practices are likely to increase in frequency and intensity with climate change e.g., increased faecal contamination of water sources and residential areas. Bad practices in rainy season include taking advantage of flooding to flush out pit latrines (instead of paying for a proper emptying service). This increases the risk of disease transmission, as well as other health risks from working with inadequate sanitation services without properly health and safety equipment.

Where toilets are no longer usable because they've been destroyed or damaged by climate hazards, poor households may be forced to revert to open defecation. During periods of water scarcity in a certain community or between seasons, residents with toilets connected to a sewer reused to visit pit latrines**. The poorest live on land at risk from flooding and/or landslides, which can destroy sanitation infrastructure. Rebuilding damaged facilities or purchasing new devices after extreme weather events can be difficult, particularly for the poor who may not have access to credit or savings accounts. This is especially true where toilets are shared by several households. In such cases, it's important that those who provide products and services. Gendered norms, such as men being responsible for decision making on household purchases or re-constructing damaged facilities, can result in delay rebuilding if male Figure 2: The impact of climate change on sanitation services (adapted from UNICEF, 2015). Low household priority**. Continued damage or destruction of a facility has an impact on WASH behaviour, and it can be difficult to convince people to use toilets when they are not functioning properly and handwashing facility, or it may result in reversal to open defecation (permanently) or on a seasonal basis. Women who do not have access to water are likely to face issues with privacy or harassment while defecating or managing menstrual hygiene.

Adaptations for climate change include the scheduled emptying of latrines in advance of flood seasons, low-water use toilets and improved construction quality to reduce the infiltration of water into pits/latrines.

Access to hygiene in household settings

Hygiene improvements create demand for water. Where water supplies are limited, households typically prioritize consumption over hygiene, and this has implications for women and girls in


---
**Page 139**
---

particular. This section reviews hand and menstrual hygiene in the context of increased high intensity rainfall, decreased rainfall, different types of flooding and tropical cyclones and other weather events.

Extreme weather events (such as heavy rain, landslides, floods and cyclones) can disrupt or damage hand hygiene facilities such as water points, toilets and washing stations. This may lead to physical limitations to access facilities. For example, where households have their handwashing stations outdoors or next to their toilet, storms could cause damage to the station. Handmade devices are more likely to be damaged than commercial ones. In addition, extreme weather events can disrupt the supply of handwashing facilities served by a piped water supply. Climate change also affects people's journeys to wash their hands. This can be a particular problem for children, people with disabilities or older people.

Handwashing with unsafe water may increase the risk of diarrhoea*. For instance, handwashing with water contaminated with high levels of E. coli is likely to transfer more E. coli onto hands than it removes from them. Similarly, washing hands in water that is contaminated water can cause skin lesions that can lead to infections entering the bloodstream**.

Climate hazards can mean that access to urban centres or other places to purchase soap or commercial menstrual products may not be accessible all around. Climate-related events can disrupt transport networks, affecting the availability of essential goods and services such as sanitary pads in the district town from reaching the village shop or household in sparsely populated rural areas.

Water scarcity can force choices about how water is used at the household level. With menstrual hygiene products being more expensive than other items, this choice may become even more problematic for women and girls in the face of climate-induced water shortages.

Water shortages at home and at school make personal hygiene more difficult to maintain during menstruation. Hotter days, more rain and higher humidity can increase the risk of reproductive tract infections such as urinary tract infection (UTI) and sexually transmitted infections (STIs). Handwashing with unsafe water may possibly increase the risk of infections. Sexual intrusion in coastal areas can make washing hands more difficult because of the salt on hands. This can cause skin irritation, redness and chafing skin, which necessitates the usage of single-use, disposable products instead. Hand washing after changing a sanitary product or handling menstrual waste is often promoted as a critical time for hand hygiene.

Prolonged shortage of water affects how reusable menstrual product are used; the frequency and manner in which they are washed and whether fuel is available to make it possible to sterilise them with boiling water. This can lead to poor quality washing, which may not remove bacteria effectively. Prolonged rainfall or very windy weather can make it impossible to dry reusable cloths in direct sunlight as recommended for hygiene reasons since drying and storing cloths in a dark or shady place (or under the bed) is more common.

Climate change may make conventional disposal options for single-use products irrelevant, both those that are safe and hygienic (disposal in solid waste systems, incineration, composting and deep burial).


---
**Page 140**
---

or improved (flushing products down the toilet, shallow burying of menstrual waste and throwing used products into drainage channels or open spaces). High-water table, heavy rainfall and overflowing water systems can exacerbate climate change if they block sewers or drainage systems, cause flooding in homes and schools, and increase the risk of disease. The use of disposable sanitary pads and menstrual products can exacerbate climate change if they block sewers or drainage systems, cause flooding in homes and schools, and increase the risk of disease.

### Access to WASH in health facilities and schools

Staff, students and patients in schools and health care facilities require sufficient WASH facilities. Increasingly deep boreholes are being installed with overhead tanks to provide a higher-level of climate-resilient water supply. Solar-powered pumps can be used for accessing alternative energy sources (such as solar energy) for pumping systems can further optimize their resilience. Storage containers can also be used in schools and health care facilities, and can provide a gravity-fed water supply to handwashing/drinking water tap stations. Simple adaptations to existing WASH infrastructure can improve efficiency and leakage management.

Moreover, the greater rooftop area and storage capacity can mean that installations provide greater efficiency and resilience. Solar-powered pumps can then be used for toilet flushing, cleaning washing hands and also drinking, with adequate filtration and proper treatment. In coastal areas affected by salinity, water storage facilities based on the rainwater harvesting can provide continuity of safe water supply to schools and health care facilities. Water can be harvested for non-potable uses such as irrigation of trees.

Repair works are likely to be a temporary issue of schools. Additional water sources (such as wells) have been installed in some schools, and this is likely to affect girls in particular. During weather events, schools are often used as shelters for temporarily displaced people, and this can place strain on WASH services. When schools re-open for students, latrines may need to be emptied and cleaned and supplies of toilet paper and soap need to be replenished. If the school is closed due to flooding or if schools are closed, girls might not be able to collect their entitlement of sanitary pads. If the government does not provide these, this can have a negative impact on the girl child’s ability to access their preferred menstrual product, or to change the product as often as needed.

### 3.3 Agency in accessing and managing WASH facilities

Women, men, boys and girls have different roles in WASH services in household, community and schools and health care facilities. Where women have relatively lower social and legal status and hold less political power within a community, this limits their influence on decisions about how families and communities manage WASH in accessing, using, developing and managing services against climate change impacts.

#### Household

Women are very often given responsibility for household WASH, for example, fetching water for the household, storing water safely, washing plates and cleaning toilets. Women and girls are usually


---
**Page 141**
---

Page 138

tackled with filtering or boiling water before drinking[2]. Women also take care of the toiletting needs of children and older people. They also spend considerable time caring for family members ill from water-related diseases such as cholera, typhoid fever, dysentery, and diarrhea[3]. In many settings, women are responsible for collecting water and maintaining household sanitation facilities. The construction and maintenance of household level sanitation facilities was done exclusively by the male head, attributed to women's lack of education and limited exposure to the world beyond their home and inability to influence the household decision-making process.

Care and the domestic WASH-related work burden falls disproportionately on women in all cultures, but the degree of this inequality varies. A shift in gender roles, with men taking some responsibility for cleaning the toilet facilities, and the environment can help women gain and take on other roles and options within and outside their homes, including those of school children. Men more often take on household roles related to both building and financing WASH facilities.

Community

Women's perspectives or voices are not always reflected in WASH decision making. Particularly in indigenous communities, women often hold important traditional knowledge about how best to manage and use both water and land resources, making them valuable experts[4]. Community WASH committees (CWCs) are the most common form of community organization for sanitation, for example in Nepal's 2011 Sanitation Master Plan requires 33% of WASH committee members to be women; however, women are often not included as equal partners or decision makers. Women's groups are rarely identified as stakeholders in the process of developing City Sanitation Plans, and so have limited input to guide strategic planning and investment decisions. Unless men are engaged in facilitation and advocacy efforts, women's voices may remain unheard[5]. This lack of representation inadvertently run the risk of increasing burdens for women. Time spent by women on community WASH activities can also impact their ability to participate fully in other roles, particularly if this is viewed as impacting on women's time for household and family responsibilities[6].

When the household is invited to attend village meetings, typically the male head-of-household will go and women will stay at the home to take care of domestic responsibilities[7]. Facilitating men's ability to support women's active participation in WASH decision-making is an emerging area in the WASH sector.

Workforce

UN Global Analysis and Assessment of Sanitation and Drinking Water (GAAW) data indicates that a large majority of countries lack sufficient human resources to implement national WASH plans. There are insufficient workers with the appropriate competencies in the right positions, including in rural or hard-to-reach areas or communities.

Men hold the majority of professional positions in the WASH sector. Studies in Vietnam, Indonesia and Timor-Leste have shown that men hold the majority of professional positions in water and sanitation companies and utilities[8].

Female representation in the workforce is a major challenge in most countries in both government and non-governmental WASH services. While men take on more paid roles, women are more likely to be offered unpaid roles as community health volunteers or health promoters. In many countries, women are also underrepresented in technical positions within WASH sector organizations. The 2015 Capacity assessment in Bangladesh found that the HR composition of government and WASH sector agencies is dominated by engineers and technocrats, most of whom are men. The number of women employed as community health volunteers or health promoters is very low. In addition, there is a lack of capacity at the community level hygiene promotion activities. Data from the NERITI database2 reveals that Bangladesh has made some progress in increasing female participation in WASH sector roles. However, the number of female engineers in the WASH sector has grown steadily over the past three decades. Sri Lanka’s Women in Water and Sanitation (WWS) project has impacted the sector, improving the ratio of women on engineering and technical water and sanitation courses. In other countries such as India, Nepal, and Sri Lanka, international agencies prefer female candidates for WASH activities, and there are numerous rural water supply projects led by women.

SAS Water’s pioneering work in 2010 documented the experiences of female water professionals in the region and shared their personal stories3. Since then, participation of women in WASH enterprises is increasing4. In Nepal5, having female sanitation sale agents is found to drive higher overall latrine use, and women are more likely than men to be involved with latrines6. Women’s ability to succeed often depends on their having self-belief, technical knowledge and safe workplaces free of harassment7. Women in WASH enterprises can also provide a balance between household work and income generation activities such as cooking, cleaning, childcare, and laundry8. In addition, women are more likely than men to be involved with water collection9. Women also work in water-dependent, income-generating activities like catering, brewing and hairdressing. Additionally, women are more likely to be involved with sanitation-related activities including toilet cleaning, latrine construction, serving as toilet attendants, doing manual scavenging work10. Employing women as sanitation workers is a common practice in India where it is common for Dalit women sanitation worker to clean and remove fecal waste from dry toilets, without the proper safety gear, risking infections11. A women sanitation worker, working as a sweeper on the move, has to carry a bucket of water to wash her hands after cleaning up human excreta in the hot sun—These women do not raise their voice against it, because they feel powerless and replaced by men who have household water and sanitation services have freed up time for other income-generating activities.

Schools and health care facilities

Gendered power issues are at play, not only in how WASH is managed within households, but also in community interventions. For instance, latrine cleaning in schools often remains a female teacher or

# Effects of climate change on agency

In some cases, climate change is extending existing gender norms and roles, but in other cases, it is contributing to a (temporary) shift in norms and expectations around WASH-related work in the household.

## 3.4 Effects of climate change on agency

### In extreme weather events
Due to the dangerous conditions, men and women make more decisions together about when and how to do WASH related tasks, as opposed to normal conditions when women mostly make these decisions. Men and boys may take over water collection (usually woman’s duty) due to heavy rain or snowfall. Women and girls may be responsible for cleaning up slippery surfaces and falling trees. Men may assist women and girls more with washing clothes due to the heavy load of carrying them in the rain. In areas where there are no paved roads, muddy paths are heavy, and therefore, they will help with this heavy work[1]. Men might also support in the bathing of children and cleaning up after a flood or snowfall.

### At the community level
Men may take on more responsibility and work load for public clean-up and for repairing damage to infrastructure after a climate related weather event such as strong winds and heavy rain[2]. Dangerous conditions may also lead to men fixing water systems and building toilets due rather than women.

### Men often have better access to education and information about climate change and water management than women (such as access to climate forecasts, early warning information and emergency and post-disaster services), and this can reinforce their role as WASH agents[3]. Men offer more support for the household in terms of repairing WASH systems following a weather event[4]. Men may attend more community activities and meetings than women in dangerous conditions.

### Women’s work inside the home generally increases with water scarcity due to gender roles and norms that govern housework and decision-making. Women often take on responsibility for managing scarce


---
**Page 144**
---

# Climate

## Water resources in the household<sup>®</sup>. Climate change can also lead to the migration of men to find alternative jobs outside of the village, which increases the burden on women in terms of caring for their households and children.

### Access to information influences how people are impacted by, and respond to, climate change.

Women are less likely to have access to information about climate events (e.g., early warning systems), and therefore are likely to be less able to prepare (e.g. to store clean water in their home before a flood). Women also tend to be responsible for household sanitation and hygiene practices and are often excluded from decision-making processes on the maintenance of water resources and assets that could help them adapt to climate change.

### The role of women in decision making

The gender gap in decision-making is linked to the perception of the man's role as physical response, such as building back infrastructure, as being more vital to decisions than the woman’s role in caring for the house, family, and children. This can lead to a lack of awareness among men about climate change issues and foster individual behavior change around climate and WASH at the household level.

# Global Evidence Review: Gender & wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems

In this Section, we present the findings of the global evidence review in the protection of wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems. It considers the following areas of discussion, identified as the more pertinent to gender:

- Women and men's use of, and interaction with, wetland, coastal and marine areas
- How climate change is affecting these different uses and interactions, and the impact on the populations concerned.
- The ways in which men and women are or are not, and could be involved in the protection and management of wetland, coastal and marine areas.

## 4.1 Different interactions with wetland, coastal and marine areas

### Gendered roles in the fishery industry

Fishing at sea with specialised equipment is usually carried out by men, while women generally fish on shallow reefs, tidal flats, and coastal estuaries. Formal and paid fishing activities are usually dominated by men, who have access to larger boats, nets, and other fishing gear. Women, however, only have access to the less profitable parts of the fish for selling, and have limited trade connections, as well as less access to boats, fishing gear, and limited financial capital due to barriers to taking out loans and credit. In many cases, women are paid less than men for their work, earning on average 30%–50% of men's wages in the aquaculture industry for carrying out the same work. Within value chains, women predominantly perform informal activities such as sorting fish, cleaning and packaging fish, while many agencies focus on the production aspect: 15% of harvesting jobs and 90% of fish processing jobs are carried out by women globally.® Moreover, women are disproportionately employed in the informal and precarious sectors of work, including artisanal fisheries and food processing. This can lead to exploitation, as there is little oversight and labour trafficking and sanitary issues can be easily masked.®

Gender norms as a barrier to women's participation in formal activities

Women’s participation in the fishery industry is often overlooked and undervalued, resulting in the marginalisation of women who are particularly active in informal fishing activities in coastal and marine areas. Studies show that fishing is seen as a “man’s job”, and women are not expected to fish for work or the arduous labour and long hours that keep them outside of the home for long periods of time.® For example, in many countries, women are not allowed to fish at sea because they are considered too weak.® In Zambia, fishing gear is often only owned by men.® As mentioned before, in many countries, women

# Effects of climate change on different uses and interactions

## Rising sea temperatures are likely to disproportionately affect women’s livelihoods and food security

Rising sea temperatures due to climate change causes changes in the behaviour of fish stock, permanent changes to ecosystems, and extinction of some species. The elimination of important ocean habitats is a major concern for fisheries. While these effects may not be explicit in the oceans literature, we know that women tend to bear the responsibility for managing crisis situations and are often the primary caregivers and income earners within households and communities in contexts of food insecurity. Job opportunities will increase in a context where there are already limited opportunities for employment, especially for women who are excluded from much of the fishing industry. Climate change disproportionately affects coastal communities who do not have access to land or other resources.

Rising sea levels are likely to disproportionately affect women's livelihoods and food security.
Heat-induced expansions of water lead to a rise in sea levels which increases the risk of storm surges for islands and coastal communities. Exposed infrastructure is also at risk."Rising sea levels leads to submergence of low-lying areas, increasing the vulnerability of women who live on these islands and coastal regions at risk of significant economic damage, especially due to damage to ports and harbours. Women and children are especially vulnerable in these instances as it is more difficult for the former to relocate and find work outside their communities.

Rising acidity in oceans are likely to disproportionately affect women's livelihoods and food security
Greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions have increased acidify in oceans by 26% since the industrial revolution."This harms shell-forming animals such as corals, phytoplankton, bivalves, and zooplankton. These organisms are critical to marine ecosystems and provide a foundation for food chains. This causes shifts in fish stock behaviour and movements, which leads to food insecurity for many coastal and island communities (OECD 2016). Fish supply will shift locations, and coral reefs that serve as tourist attractions will be damaged or destroyed. Women who are already economically vulnerable, and who have already face difficulty in finding jobs, are unlikely to have the resources necessary (or for women, sufficient mobility) to follow the movement of fish stocks.

*Using their ideas to go unheard in community-based management and protection of marine and coastal life.*

Women's exclusion is a hindrance for the improvement of fishing sustainability because they are often more cognizant of the need to make conditions better for future generations, and are more willing to engage in progressive sustainability practices. For example, when First Nation Heiltsuk women in British Columbia took on leadership roles in the management of wetlands, they helped transform the governance structure of their community.

However, as developments in the ocean economy progress, there have been more pushes to include local voices and developing communities in decision making processes – with some attempts at inclusion being made. In these cases, however, women are often excluded from the process. Instead, women are more likely be included in discussions, but empowerment of civil society and localised institutions (still new developments in the ocean economy) is necessary for women to exercise agency and decisions on their own terms.

Gender-blind policy and decision making

Institutions need to be agile, adaptable and follow 'top-down', bottom-up' practices that are informed by science. Women face barriers to accessing governing institutions, with their voices often left out of decision-making processes. This is compounded when women are excluded from the development of advisory bodies, causing decisions to be made that only benefit some groups (Osterblom et al., 2020), making it difficult to understand how women are affected by industry developments.

Data collected by governments often only includes information on employment in the paid harvesting sector. The fishery industry has been slow to engage women's engagement (Adams et al., 2021). Such conventional gender-blind approaches to research and policy development for the fishery industry have resulted in policies that are not designed with women in mind, or that do not account for women's livelihoods, which are largely unaccounted for, with the resultant policies, including those concerning spatial policies that designate certain areas as 'no-take' zones, women have had to resort to fishing illegally elsewhere. In these cases, women have had to resort to fishing illegally, which removes them still further from the formal fishing industry.

Limited evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of technological advancements

Innovations in marine resource management have often been assumed to be "gender neutral", meaning that they are equally beneficial for both men and women. However, such initiatives ("gender-neutrality") has contributed to male bias in their design.

Climate Smart Aquaculture (CSA) is a new technology that is used to ensure sustainable fishing and the continuity of the fish supply chain. Limited research has been conducted to see how women

#### Page 37 of 143


---
**Page 149**
---

engage with and benefit from this new technology, despite the fact that climate change affects rural, low-income, communities of women particularly adversely.**Innovation in feed and seed breeding technologies has not been evaluated through gender analysis**, resulting in a lack of evidence as to how new technological advancements are affecting women.

However, there have been some studies on gender differences in CSA initiatives. When a study was conducted in Bangladesh to investigate the potential role of women in LSA, several conclusions were made about the importance of women's participation and their ability to make decisions regarding that they had experienced positive changes in their decision-making power.**In Bangladesh and India**, women were found to relate fish trait preferences to household food and nutrition security.

Prevalence of gender-segregated interventions which fail to address underlying barriers Capacity building for women is essential, but it needs to be done in a way that will allow them to maximise profits while reducing overfishing.**Effective fishing practices that include women would not only generate wealth but also allow for better distribution of resources**, and an ocean economy where all voices have been heard. Women's participation in the fisheries sector is essential, as it will ensure that the fishing industry will protect against inequity, and prevent inefficiencies and undemocratic decision making. Including women in all segments of the fishery industry requires transparency, and monitoring and evaluation to ensure that they are not being exploited for their labor or other reasons such as labour trafficking.

However, existing training programs in the fishing sector largely follows a "gap-filling" approach when women are accommodated (but, critically, not addressing the normative barriers that women face)**Training tends to be held close to women's homes and often the technology or strategies introduced relate to jobs that can be performed from home.** These interventions target women separately from men and do not address the underlying gender inequalities in the fishing industry. These interventions also fail to recognize that women are not viewed as "true" fishers, therefore treating women separately and as different from men.

In select instances, gender transformative measures which address these underlying normative issues have been introduced in natural resource management, as seen in a study in the implementation of women's empowerment programs in fisheries. However, the overall impact on women's livelihoods continues to remain difficult. Barriers to gender-transformative approaches include low political and social will, poor messaging about gender norms and a lack of assistance from governments and NGOs.

# Global Evidence Review: Gender & climate resilient port infrastructure

In this section, we present the findings from the global evidence review on climate resilient port infrastructure. It considers the following areas of discussion, identified as the more pertinent issues based on the literature:

- The different ways in which men and women use and benefit from available port infrastructure;
- The different ways in which men and women are, or are not, could be involved in and can benefit from the development of climate resilient port infrastructure.

## 5.1 Different uses and benefits from available port infrastructure

Women do not benefit equally from the employment benefits of port infrastructure Ports are a major hub for trade, and are vital for national economies as they provide job creation and employment opportunities. In Eastern and Southern Africa, seaborne trade accounted for 80% of total trade volume in 2016. **Seaports have contributed to the expansion of urban populations and growth of local economies**, but women continue to be excluded from the benefits of economic growth due to their exclusion from operational and managerial positions.

### Lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of ports' negative impacts on ecosystems

Activities related to port infrastructures can damage city port, delta and river ecosystems through land reclamation, dredging and construction. As ports grow in number, it is difficult to find areas that have the space for sustainable expansion. There is a need for port infrastructure development that respects the environment and takes into account climate change and other forms of ecological change**. Ports were originally built with the prioritization of trade and economic growth in mind, but little consideration of environmental and health impacts. No gender-disaggregated impact analysis could be found in this specific literature.

## 5.2 Different involvement and benefits from the development of climate resilient port infrastructure

Women's (potential) role in employment and leadership roles in climate resilient ports The expansion and development of new climate-resilient ports creates jobs and therefore opens up opportunities to train and hire women. However, according to reports from seafaring institutions, female trainees only made up 6% of training participants in 2021.


---
**Page 151**
---

# Women's (potential) roles in promoting the importance of ecosystems

Women broadly have an important role to play in promoting climate-resilient port development for the benefit of wider communities.

## Through the utilisation of ecosystem-based management approaches and the promotion of sustainable practices, women can contribute significantly towards more sustainable port development. This involves the development of ports which have no negative impact on the environment, and recognises the complexity of ecological systems, as well as the intricate ways in which stressors such as climate change, pollution, and invasive species interact with these systems.

## Women are often better placed to understand the impacts of these stressors on ecosystems. They can also be more attuned to the need for sustainable practices that reduce environmental impact while still meeting economic needs. By promoting these practices, women can help ensure that ports are developed in a way that is both economically viable and environmentally friendly.

## Women's (potential) roles in promoting the importance of ecosystems

Women broadly have an important role to play in promoting climate-resilient port development for the benefit of wider communities.

## Through the utilisation of ecosystem-based management approaches and the promotion of sustainable practices, women can contribute significantly towards more sustainable port development. This involves the development of ports which have no negative impact on the environment, and recognises the complexity of ecological systems, as well as the intricate ways in which stressors such as climate change, pollution, and invasive species interact with these systems.

## Women are often better placed to understand the impacts of these stressors on ecosystems. They can also be more attuned to the need for sustainable practices that reduce environmental impact while still meeting economic needs. By promoting these practices, women can help ensure that ports are developed in a way that is both economically viable and environmentally friendly.

## Lack of evidence on gender-differentiated benefits of optimised infrastructure and energy efficiency

Increased trade capacity and employment opportunities can stimulate local economies. The expansion of ports will create many jobs in the areas of operations and building, as well as increase business to the port itself. However, these benefits are not always evenly distributed across all groups of people.

## Better ports will lead to a decrease in the cost of sea and inland transport and operational expenses, increasing profits for post-operators and decreasing prices for users. "The optimisation of port infrastructure can benefit men and women differently, with some studies showing that better ports while also minimising the impact of port activity on the environment. However, the literature does not explore whether or how the optimisation of port infrastructure can benefit men and women differently.

Through the production and sourcing of local renewable energy, encouragement of clean energy initiatives and increasing energy-efficient port equipment, negative environmental externalities of ports can be reduced. These measures also improve the health and safety awareness of employees and local communities. However, the literature does not explore whether or how these changes benefit women and men differently.

GHG emissions from ships can be reduced through onshore power supplying, incentivising best-performing vessels to reduce their emissions, and encouraging ship owners to invest in more energy efficient through innovative processes, production technologies and the implementation of clear and transparent environmental policies. The IMO has recently introduced a new tool as an Environmental Ship Index that incentivises best-performing vessel initiatives. Concession agreements and lease clauses could be used to encourage clean energy initiatives from third parties. However, as stated above, women are often underrepresented in port management positions and are often excluded from technical training and leadership positions. In order to limit inequities in port infrastructure development, it could be beneficial to engage in mentorship and training programmes for women first, before enacting any technical improvements.

# National WASH policy and context in focal countries

In this Section, our focus shifts to the country level to present a summary of national WASH policy and context in the 11 focal countries: Bangladesh, Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, Ivory Coast, Kenya, Namibia, Nigeria, Pakistan, and Senegal. This is based on the findings from the country-level rapid situational analyses that were completed and which can be found in full in Annex 5. These country analyses provide a top-line picture of access to WASH facilities, drawing data from the WHO/UNICEF Joint Programme on Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (JPI) Global Database of water and sanitation indicators and the WHO/UNICEF Joint Monitoring Program for Water Supply and Sanitation (JMP) ladder for drinking water and sanitation service levels (Table 4) as a means of differentiating the level of water and sanitation access in each country. The analysis also draws on the findings from the JPI’s WASH policy context to understand the extent to which, and how, current policy frameworks identify and seek to address barriers to improving WASH services and systems resilience. In 10 out of the 11 country summaries, we also include a stakeholder mapping.

## Table 4: Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene ladders for drinking water and sanitation service levels

| Drinking water ladder | Sanitation service |
| --- | --- |
| Safely managed | Use of improved facilities that are not shared with other households or removed and treated offsite |
| Basic | Use of improved facilities which are not shared with other households |
| Limited | Use of improved facilities shared between two or more households |
| Unimproved | Use of pit latrines, without a slab or platform; hanging latrines or bucket latrines |
| Surface water | Open Defecation |

* A joint WHO/UNICEF initiative to improve global monitoring of drinking water, sanitation and hygiene in the context of the new 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development.
* Stakeholder mapping could not be conducted for Brazil, Botswana and Indonesia as No Objection letters were received late in the Gender Assessment process.

# Bangladesh

## Key Messages

Bangladesh generally has high levels of access to improved drinking water sources and relatively high levels of access to some form of sanitation facility. Improving water, sanitation and hygiene is a key part of Bangladesh's national policy framework as well as further, more specific water strategies.

The policies recognise the increasing impacts of climate change, rapid urbanisation and water contamination on equitable access, identifying these as challenges for future development in water, sanitation and hygiene.

Bangladesh’s location makes it highly susceptible to the adverse effects of climate change, hindering its development, including access to safe and economic opportunities for women and other vulnerable groups. Strategic objectives to combat climate change and reduce vulnerability include integrating gender into the negative impacts caused by increased weather extremities. Strategic objectives to combat climate change and enhance resilience in water, sanitation and hygiene promotion seek to include gender mainstreaming in all the service planning, implementation and monitoring objectives.

The policies also recognise that women play a critical role as decision-makers and advocates of their own role in hygiene promotion, both as a token role and also as an empowered advocate.


---
**Page 155**
---

Most of the policies reviewed did not explicitly consider advancing women’s formal roles in the WASH sector, but they still discussed the need for community involvement in the planning, implementation, and monitoring of water supply projects. Some policies highlighted potential opportunities to consider for the advancement of women's involvement in, for example, entrepreneurship activities, such as microfinance schemes or small-scale enterprises. With a strong emphasis within some sectors on career development opportunities for women employees and equal participation of women in decision-making and governance at all levels. Additionally, due to only 21% of women having access to secondary education compared with 35% of men having higher education, women are limited in the careers prospects within the WASH sector, and a focus is placed on opportunities in low-skilled jobs.

### Stakeholder Engagement

In Bangladesh, the Water Supply branch of the Ministry of Local Government, Rural Development and Cooperatives has primary responsibility for Water and Sanitation Supply and Strategy. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, for Hygiene Promotion; Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR), covering irrigation, flooding, and climate-related water protection.

## 6.2 Botswana

### Key Messages

Botswana has high levels of access to at least basic sources of drinking water and basic sanitation services. In the policy landscape, key challenges identified in the WASH sphere include water shortages due to the increasing demand for water from agriculture, industry, and urbanization; and pressure on sanitation and health services because of increasing levels of urbanisation, facilitate access to safe drinking water and sanitation services. The interplay between climate change, the water sector and public health. No specific policies related to women and WASH are identified, but the National Development Plan calls for equal participation and representation in all sectors. Some key challenges and opportunities for advancing gender equality identified in the policy landscape include the currently low participation of females in STEM subjects, a need for more inclusive policies climate change and including gender and climate change into academic curriculum.

### Headline data on access to WASH services

Access to drinking water: 92.3% of the population has access to at least basic level of drinking water; 17.8% of the population relies on unimproved drinking water while 1.3% of the population has access only to surface drinking water.

Access to sanitation: 80% of the population has access to basic sanitation services and 5.6% of the population has access to a limited level of sanitation services; 9.8% of the population practices open defecation in sanitation services.


---
**Page 156**
---

# Climate Investors

## Policy framework

Botswana’s WASH policy landscape is focused around its 11th National Development Plan, including Botswana’s Vision 2036. Some of the key development challenges facing Botswana’s population include water scarcity and droughts, and urban migration and urbanization. Challenges identified here, and further elaborated on in the National Water Policy relate to water shortages and water quality, as well as the potential for increased malnutrition, food insecurity, pressure on sanitation and health services because of increasing levels of urbanisation, as well as further WASH-related health consequences.

Future objectives are set around frameworks that will facilitate access to water of suitable quality and standards. This is seen as a key element in laying foundations for sustainable development of water resources in support of economic growth, diversification and poverty eradication. Climate change is a key consideration in the National Water Policy, with particular emphasis on the impact of climate change, droughts and water supply for agriculture and industry as key issues, and expanding on adopting ‘smart agriculture’ as an opportunity. Between documents, interplay between climate change, WASH and food security is identified, as well as the potential for increased malnutrition on the impact of water shortages and their potential for causing malnutrition, and also identifies the negative public health consequences of a lack of access to adequate sanitation and safe drinking water.

The National Development Plan largely ignores specific gender considerations in the water and sanitation sectors. However, it does state that the government recognises the need for equal participation by women and men in all aspects of development. In relation to WASH sector policies, an opportunity is identified in working towards gender and social equity in accessing water resources and sanitation services. This is seen as a key element in laying foundations relating to sustainable development and management of water resources. Although it is identified often as an important consideration, throughout all policies it is not further elaborated how this will be operationalised.

There are some key gender challenges and opportunities identified around over the policy landscape. First, the low participation of females in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) subjects has been identified as a challenge to women’s employment prospects, particularly for those with high school completion rates. However, they fail to articulate how this challenge impacts women's employment prospects or what measures should be taken to address it. It is suggested that such measures should be gender sensitive, by empowering women and youth to actively participate in the implementation of climate response measures, adopting strategies that are targeted at increasing women’s participation with respect to climate change, and including gender and climate change into academic curriculum at all levels.

## Brazil

### Key Messages

Brazil has a high rate of access both to improved water sources for drinking water and to improved sanitation services. Brazil’s National Policies for Water and Sanitation identify the importance of WASH in the fight against poverty and inequality. The country is also committed to gender equality, with their policies presenting a number of opportunities within the sector, for instance working to increase environmental awareness among women and girls, as well as promoting gender equality by incorporating gender equality in all actions and to develop ongoing training that promotes female


---
**Page 157**
---

# Climate Fund Managers

## Leadership
In addition, at the Federal level, policies emphasize the need to achieve equality, including from a gender perspective. There are few specific recommendations with regard to WASH and gender at either federal or state levels.

### Headline data on access to WASH services
Access to drinking water: 99.3% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which the majority, 85.8%, has access to safe managed drinking water, and only 0.1% has access to a limited supply.

Access to sanitation: 90.4% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 48.7% has access to safely managed sanitation service, while 9.8% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 63.3% who practice open defecation?

## Policy Framework
Brazil’s National Policies for Water and Sanitation both discuss in-depth the importance of WASH in daily life and lay out clear objectives to ensure the impact on human livelihoods and activities, such as business, is limited. They examine the negative impacts that water scarcity creates in vulnerable communities, including women and children, and highlight the need to consider environmental characteristics from high degradation. They add the impact of climate change leaders to further alternatives for water management and sanitation services.

They present innovative opportunities within the sector, all working to increase environmental education and capacity building on WASH issues. The policies also emphasize the importance of incorporating gender equality in all actions and develop ongoing training that promotes female leadership and empowerment, but does not provide any detail on practical implications of this intention.

In terms of sanitation, Brazil’s policies identify issues with parts of the population continuing to lack access to basic sanitation facilities and/or proper disposal of solid waste and associated health consequences. The policies also highlight the need for a comprehensive approach that includes increasing and improving infrastructure, management, and monitoring of sanitation facilities, and the suggestion of creating an intersectoral committee on WASH in order to ensure the inclusion of gender in the policy objectives.

The cross between health and climate change is brought up through the country’s National Climate Change Plan. The plan highlights the importance for both human health and livelihoods of people, which includes the effect it has on water reserves in the country.

As a federal state, Brazil's lower tiers of government play an important role in the provision of sustainable services to its citizens. The policies at these levels emphasize equality within regions and between regions. It seeks to ensure equal access to economic opportunities within all the regions, both from a socio-economic and gender perspective. However, there are no specific recommendations with regard to WASH and gender at either federal or state levels.

There are also gender-specific strategies within outlined climate action measures. Brazil’s policies further outline the importance of valuing women's contribution to farm work and equal participation in leadership and decision-making. Moreover, the Education and Health Plans aim to reduce inequality in their respective systems.

# 6.4 Djibouti

## Key messages

Djibouti generally has high levels of access to improved sources of drinking water and relatively high levels of access to improved sanitation services. Its policy framework identifies a number of areas of connection between WASH and climate change and although connections between WASH and gender aren’t typically discussed, it’s important to consider how women and girls’ changing needs, new behaviors and practices as well as the importance of engaging men to change their views on women’s role. Policies should be designed in such a way that they recognize that women and youth, should have a larger role in the policy-making process. Finally, policies recognize opportunities to incorporate women into the workforce due to their higher levels of education.

### Headline data on access to WASH services

Access to drinking water: 90.8% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking water, although this is not necessarily a basic or even a basic access[2] and women are the main collectors of water.[3]

Access to sanitation: 73.8% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 37.1% has access to safely managed sanitation services.

## Policy framework

There are a number of key policies which relate to WASH and gender in the Djibouti policy environment and which offer opportunities for engagement. For instance, the Djibouti Vision 2035 (2010) includes a specific section on gender which acknowledges women’s role in enforcing new behaviors and practices; however, it emphasises how “in order to improve women's situation, men should be involved”[4] and that “the government must adopt a participatory approach towards women and their approaches with them.”[5]

The 2010 Development Plan for the Primary Sector and the 2015 Strategy of Accelerated Growth and Promotion of Employment echo the focus on access to water for agriculture and livestock, as well as the hygienic conditions in schools. The Djibouti Vision 2035 (2010) acknowledges that policies touch on gender and the need to address gender inequity throughout the primary sector and for growth, but it does not specify how this is done or what actions are taken. Women’s health consequences and hard-to-reach water points, are known to generally affect women more, but this is not specified in the policies. They do acknowledge that various stakeholders, including women and youth, should have a larger role in the policy-making process.


---
**Page 159**
---

Education is a strong focus of Djibouti’s 2011–2021 Gender Policy, the policy that most identifies how water and sanitation challenges are an extra burden on women. The policy covers accessibility to water and sanitation facilities for women in their immediate environments. Currently, the completion rate for upper secondary is higher for women than for men; this presents a great opportunity to incorporate women into the workforce due to the high level of education they have achieved.

### 6.5 Indonesia

**Key Messages:**

Indonesia has both high levels of access to improved water sources for drinking water and access to improved sanitation services. Faced by the challenges of climate change and rapid urbanization, in terms of the policy framework, it sees water supply and increased sanitation as a key component to self-reliance and development for all people. National climate policies highlight that climate-related changes disproportionately impact women due to issues such as limited access to water resources and sanitation services.

WASH and health policies recognize the need for gender mainstreaming to reach the country’s long-term goals but entry points in WASH are not explored, such as providing career opportunities in the WASH sector. Women have a significant role in decision-making processes, especially in health, education, and the labour market.

**Headline data on access to WASH services:**

Access to drinking water: 93.8% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which almost 92.4%, have access to a basic level of drinking water.
Access to sanitation: 92.0% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 86.5% has access to a basic level of sanitation service, and 61.5% practice open defecation.

**Policy framework:**

As a country with over 200 million spread out over 6,000 islands, and a major reliance on raw water supply to provide for their water resource, Indonesia has placed natural resource promotion and recovery as one of the main pillars in its National Climate Change Policy. This is due to climate change, rapid urbanization, and contamination of water supply it sees water supply and increasing sanitation facilities as a key component to ensuring self-reliance and development for all people. Whilst it


---
**Page 160**
---

recognizes the need for gender mainstreaming in policies to reach the country’s long-term goals, the specific gender dimensions of WASH are not explored.

The National Plan talks about using the 3R approach - Reduce, Reuse, Recycle - and the circular economy to increase effective waste disposal, to address cross-cutting issues of climate and WASH. They aim for 90% of the population to have access to safe and proper sanitation. As for water resources, they aim for 100% coverage in terms of access to improved sources of drinking water and access to safe drinking water. Policies see community involvement as a main way to ensure implementation of these goals. However, there is no mention of gender in the National Plan. This includes increasing women’s role in development and decision-making, especially within health, education, labour market, economy, and public sectors. National climate policies highlight that different groups are affected by climate change differently. These include women who have limited access to water supplies for domestic and economic use, limited access to safe sanitation facilities, and an imbalance in capacity for understanding the importance of safe water and sanitation practices.

Health outcomes are also linked with gender equality. The low levels of health outcomes can be attributed to the lack of progress by local governments. The national Health Strategy presents opportunities for governments to partner with NGOs and the private sector, subsidise communal sanitation facilities, and provide training on hygiene and sanitation. However, there is no mention of gender-related issues in community-based sanitation and there is no consideration to the specific sanitation needs of women. However, all the policies discuss increasing the capacity of communities and working towards gender equality. The National Plan does not mention the importance of these issues or a limited or no discussion about providing women specifically with career opportunities in the WASH sector.

### 6.6 Ivory Coast

#### Key Messages
Ivory Coast has average levels of access to improved sources of drinking water as well as improved sanitation facilities, although they perform stronger on drinking water access. A high proportion of their population still practice open defecation. It’s policy framework has a strong focus on structural economic development and poverty reduction, with a particular emphasis on the importance of human capital. The two largest problems that are brought out are very low water availability, worsening due to climate change, as well as high levels of disease due to poor water and sanitation facilities. There is no mention in any policy document about gender equality or women’s rights, better consideration of gender in public policies. However, all policies are quite gender blind and do not propose how to create a more inclusive environment for both men and women, other than brief mention of increasing inclusion of women in the employment sector.

#### Headline data on access to WASH services:
Access to drinking water: 79.8% of the population has access to an improved water source, of which only 35.2% have access to safely managed drinking water. 20.2% of the population access unimproved

drinking water, including 6% who only access surface water.[18] Women are the main collectors of water.[19]

Access to sanitation: 57.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of whom 34.4% has access to basic sanitation service, while 25.3% of the population still practices open defecation.

### Policy framework

Ivory Coast's national development plan has a strong focus on structural economic reforms, which is balanced with an understanding that these go hand in hand with the development of human capital, one of its key pillars.[20] The plan identifies three main areas of intervention: governance and institutions; range from governance of resources, quality of health services and the population's health; and lastly, lacking infrastructure. Beyond the general lack of access to water and sanitation services in Ivory Coast, it is acknowledged that urban populations are more vulnerable than rural ones as cities are rapidly urbanizing. Consequences, such as the rapid spread of diseases like cholera, and food shortages due to water scarcity are also a concern.

### Climate change

Climate change programmes further discuss health consequences of water scarcity and cleanliness, in changing circumstances. Suggested strategies focus on more than just the construction of new facilities but also on the strengthening of institutional frameworks and regulatory mechanisms that have demonstrated that urban populations are more vulnerable, but there is no mention of the differentiating impact between women and men. The National Development Plan does, however, clearly recognize the need to integrate gender equality into all policies and programmes affecting the environment in public policies. With regard to gender, the strengthening of the institutional and regulatory framework will be necessary, as well as the development of the skills of actors who apply it in plain, operational terms.

### Stakeholder Mapping

In Ivory Coast, the Ministry of Hydraulics, Sanitation and Hygiene has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Environment of Urban Sanitation and Sustainable Development, which have a key role in the adaptation to climate change, and the Ministry of Health and Public Hygiene, leading on hygiene policy and programmes.

# 6.7 Kenya

## Key Messages
Kenya has average levels of access to improved sources of drinking water as well as improved sanitation services, although they perform stronger on drinking water access. Its policy framework focuses on capacity building, public participation, adoption of innovative technical approaches and multi-sectoral collaboration. Women's contributions in the planning, implementation and management of water and sanitation projects/programmes and the provision of adequate water and sanitation facilities are recognised throughout and a big emphasis is put on job opportunities for women and the empowerment of women in leadership. The impacts of climate are discussed but do not relate directly to gender.

## Headline data on access to WASH services
Access to water: 71.1% of the population has access to an improved water source, of which 63.6% has access to basic drinking water and 5.5% has access to drinking water categorised as "limited".

Access to sanitation: 58.2% of the population has access to improved sanitation services while only 44.8% of the population has access to a facility that provides adequate sanitation.

## Policy framework
The drive to advance livelihoods in Kenya is laid out through the four key areas, dubbed the Big Four Agenda, within the National Development plan: Water, sanitation and hygiene continues to be a key sub-focus area. The main challenges are inadequate water supply infrastructure and lack of water conservation strategies as hindrances to the development of a clean, safe environment. The policy framework aims at addressing these challenges by promoting capacity building, public participation, adoption of innovative technical approaches, multi-sectoral collaboration and adequate water and sanitation facilities.

There is mention of the under-valuation of women's contribution to the economy, underemployment and lack of access to education and training in areas such as health care. Factors such as mortality and morbidity burden associated with unsafe water and sanitation and the risk of rape and domestic violence are also discussed. In addition, girls who attend school for longer periods in schools, girls tend to be more affected by inadequate WASH facilities, and this impacts their attendance and can expose them to increased risks of gender-based violence. To limit the impact on children's education, the policy framework discusses the need for adequate water supply services, the construction of facilities for safe and clean drinking water in schools and separate sanitation facilities for boys and girls.

In addition to these issues, they intend to scale up access to improved rural and urban sanitation and build governance and leadership capacity for sanitation. The policy discusses an increase in education, training and skill development for sanitation, including increasing the availability of skilled personnel and providing them with adequate tools and resources. These efforts are focused on any one gender/sex. Interventions throughout these policies is the continual discussion of women's empowerment and job opportunities for women as well as their role in the planning, implementation and management of water and sanitation projects/programmes and the

# Climate Fund Managers

## Push for Participation of Female Leaders on Community-Led Committees through Gender Mainstreaming

While most policies include opportunities for job creation, many do not discuss career opportunities or gender equality in detail. This is particularly true when it comes to encouraging women's participation in the technologies and sciences STEM-based courses.

With climate change being a major discussion in most of the policies, the National Climate Policy examines how increases in weather extremes, including droughts, floods and water salinization, will impact water availability and infrastructure. The policy also focuses on inadequate sanitation systems which lead to clogged drains and water contamination.

### Stakeholder Mapping

In Kenya, the Ministry of Water and Irrigation has primary responsibility for WASH. Other National Ministries with partial or total responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for sanitation and Hygiene provision and policy; and the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, who set environmental standards, and the Ministry of Housing, who handle WASH infrastructure.

## Namibia

### Key Messages

Namibia has a high level of access to improved sources of drinking water, with over 90% of the population having access. However, only about half have access to sanitation services. Its policy framework looks at water and sanitation as two separate sectors, with the former focusing on water availability and infrastructure and the latter focuses on health consequences and more on delivering through WASH systems. The policy landscape recognizes that women and marginalized groups are impacted by both water and sanitation sectors, none explore in any great detail how women, youth and marginalised groups are impacted nor the roles that these groups can play in these sectors. The policy landscape recognizes that women and marginalized groups are impacted, and highlights the importance of education.

### Headline Data on Access to WASH Services

- **Access to drinking water:** 91.4% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking water, of which 84.3% has access to basic drinking water and 7.1% has access to limited drinking water.
- **Access to sanitation:** 48.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 35.3% has access to basic sanitation services. 47.2% of the population continues to practice open defecation.

### Policy Framework

Namibia's National Development Plan looks at water and sanitation as two separate sectors, rather than looking at water and sanitation through a combined WASH lens. As such, analysis of the challenges faced in the water and sanitation sectors fall within these two separate pillars. The first is economic, focusing on improving access to improved sources of drinking water and sanitation infrastructure. The second is social transformation, focused on improved sanitation systems and infrastructure as well strengthening advocacy.

Namibia's Water Supply and Sanitation Policy (2008) goes into detail on the challenges themselves, the context, the health consequences and Namibia’s policy objectives in response to these challenges.


---
**Page 164**
---

They continue to handle water availability and infrastructure separately from sanitation challenges. Namibia’s 2011 National Policy on Climate Change also analyzes the issues of water supply and provision of sanitation services, but focuses on the impact of climate change on both sectors, as well as the potential health consequences and Namibia's vulnerability of water supply to climate change is recognized.

While all policies reference gender as a cross-cutting issue in the water and sanitation sectors, none explore it in detail. The policy framework does not provide any information about the roles that these groups can play in these sectors. Namibia’s National Gender Policy explicitly tackles the gender dimension in the water sector, but does not address the role of women or girls who are under-represented in policy and decision-making forums on the utilisation of natural resources, environmental development and water management. The policy also explores the roles that women play within households with regard to water collection and sanitation practices, as well as their access to water and sanitation. A lack of these basic amenities has a detrimental effect on the health of rural communities, and this disproportionately affects women and female-headed households.

The policy states that the government will include gender and climate change, including issues such as water availability and infrastructure, in its national development plans. However, Namibia’s policies are very limited in their consideration of women's employment levels in the water and sanitation sectors and in terms of their access to land and resources for livelihoods. The policy explicitly states that, as a strategy for gender mainstreaming, women should be involved in interventions to achieve the objectives of environmental sustainability, including in the water and sanitation sectors.

Stakeholder Mapping

In Namibia, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water and Land Reform has primary responsibility for WASH. Other stakeholders with some WASH responsibility include Ministry of Urban and Rural Development, ensuring implementation of national WASH strategy for urban settlements, and the Ministry of Health and Social Services, implementing and enforcing hygiene practices.

### 6.9 Nigeria

#### Key Messages

Nigeria has relatively high levels of access to improved water sources, although much of this is still at a basic level. The country’s policy framework presents a balance of WASH-related issues between economic impacts, governance and coordination issues, and health care. The importance of including women in the planning, implementation and monitoring of WASH projects was highlighted by all policies reviewed. There was also a gender perspective in their solutions. In all policies reviewed, there was a clear theme around capacity building and job creation. Their policies do not go much further than recognising the importance of women’s participation in specific policy and opportunities.

#### Headline data on access to WASH services

Access to drinking water: 78% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which the majority, 55%, is access at a basic level of drinking water. 40% of the population are women and this is the main collection method used.


---
**Page 165**
---

Access to sanitation: 43% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 30.5% has access to safely managed sanitation service. 18.7% practice open defecation.[16]

### Policy Framework

As Nigeria continues to experience rapid urbanisation and growth, they also aim to socially develop in areas of water resources and sanitation, water and gender equity and employment, and job creation.

Challenges identified include ensuring economic diversification, private sector investment in WASH services, lack of coordination and communal disputes, negative impacts of climate change, constrained financial resources, and the need for a more inclusive policy formulation culture. Some of the main ways they seek to overcome these challenges is by continuing to improve sanitation service delivery through the implementation of an integrated planning and policy formulation approach, while also increasing training, developing the bioeconomy and circular economy, and increasing access to finance and technical support.

One recurring theme in their policies is the concern in negative impacts on Nigeria’s water brought about by climate change. The Climate Change Policy[17] highlights that extreme weather places an already strained water, sanitation and hygiene sector. Nigeria seeks to overcome issues related to climate change and inadequate water and waste infrastructure through the implementation of a National Water Resources Strategy (NWRs). This includes the establishment, management, operation and maintenance of water supply and sanitation schemes and services. They recognise the importance of including women in the planning, implementation and management of WASH projects, and seek to integrate a gender perspective in the adoption of new technologies and infrastructure.

In all the policies reviewed, there was a clear theme around capability building and job creation. Within the Climate Change Policy, there is discussion on collaboration with the Federal Ministry of Women Affairs to develop capacity building programmes for women’s participation in the planning and management of climate change programmes. This includes investment in gender equality security and climate change resilience growth strategies to sustain jobs, employment, economic development and social inclusion.

The National Gender Policy[18] 2006 looks at the importance of including women in the work force and actively participating in decision making, as well as social inclusion throughout.

### Stakeholder Mapping

In Nigeria, the Ministry of Water Resources has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include The National Council on Water Resources, a water resources policy formulating body, and the Ministry of Health and Ministry of Education, who have roles formulating community sanitation and hygiene.


[16] Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020)
