### 6.10 Morocco

#### Key Messages
Morocco has a very high level of access to improved water sources and relatively high levels of access to improved sanitation services. Its policy framework is centred around creating a sustainable water and sanitation system as it identified the important crossover between climate change and WASH. Throughout its policies, there are references to gender equality, but these are often quite gender blind and do not present concrete action points on how to create gender-sensitive programmes. There is also a lack of recognition that women are more vulnerable to WASH-related health and climate impacts, and places importance on joint participation, as well as capacity building and training for all communities.

#### Headline data on access to WASH services
Access to drinking water: 90% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water. Of this group, 85%, has access to safely managed drinking water.
Access to sanitation: 87% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, while only 9.5% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 1.7% who practice open defecation.

#### Policy Framework
Due to continued threats to available water resources throughout the country, the National Sustainable Development Policy 2017 (NSDP) recognises the importance of water, sanitation and hygiene programmes in the development of the country. Not only does it seek to improve facilities, access and programmes around water, sanitation and hygiene, it identifies the opportunities for transformational change that can be achieved through these improvements. It also recognises the need for human development and combat social inequalities and promotes equality. However, it remains quite gender blind with regard to the incorporation of gender in policies.

Moroccan Climate Change Policy 2014 highlights the impact of climate change on how people access to water and sanitation services. The policy recognises that women are typically responsible for managing water. It also points to the importance of increasing financing and programmes focused on combating climate change, including education and awareness on alternative livelihoods and adaptation strategies. The Climate Change Policy 2014 also highlights the need to protect communities against floods, especially for sanitation facilities and agricultural drainage systems.

Within all these policies, there is no clear examination of how to increase job opportunities for women. Similarly, they discuss increasing employment opportunities more generally. The Gender Strategy sees the value of ensuring there is equal participation of women and men in communities, especially around decision-making, and identifies the impact that inadequate water and sanitation facilities have on women’s lives. It also recognises the importance of equal access to education. The Climate Change Policy further discusses the importance of capacity building and training for individuals, communities, interest groups and public and private enterprises.


---
**Page 167**
---

# Stakeholder Mapping

In Morocco, the Ministry of Interior has primary responsibility for WASH. Other National Ministries with some WASH responsibility include The General Directorate of Hydraulics, the lead agency for water resources management, and The Ministry of Health, responsible for drinking water quality control and water-related laws.

## 6.1 Uganda

### Key Messages
Uganda has a relatively high access to improved sources of drinking water, but has low access to improved sanitation services. Its policy framework strikes a balance between a good quality of life for all and economic productivity, therefore focusing their water programs on investing in the effective management of the entire WASH value chain segments. The country's policies are built around the waternomics approach, which is based on the premise that women and girls bear the brunt of water-related issues quite explicitly without, with emphasis on the disproportionate burden that women and girls suffer. There is a strong commitment to gender equality in Uganda’s policy framework, with particular focus on including women in employment or income-generating opportunities. Although there is a lack of practical points to implement gender-sensitive policies, there is a commitment to stakeholder participation and consultation.

### Headline data on access to WASH services:
- Access to drinking water: 83.1% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which a large part, 92%, has access only to a basic level of drinking water.
- Access to sanitation: 37.4% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, while 62.6% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 4.7% who practice open defecation.

### Policy Framework
There has been a recent decreased attention to water, sanitation and hygiene in national policy in Uganda. Nevertheless, water, sanitation and hygiene remain a core area of Uganda’s Human Development Strategy (HDS). The HDS is aimed at improving the quality of life for all Ugandans by increasing competitiveness and better quality of life for all. It also foresees investing in the effective management of the entire WASH value chain segments, including containment, emptying, transportation and disposal.

It is recognised that all development will be more challenging due to the effects of climate change, with more extreme weather patterns leading to increased water stress, impacting water availability and quality. The country has already experienced some of these impacts in recent years, which are likely to increase already in place. The Uganda National Climate Change Policy (2015—2020) articulates the challenges further and highlights that, given their domestic role, women are likely to experience the effects of climate change more acutely than men. Given this reality, it commits to mainstream gender issues across climate change adaptation and mitigation actions including ensuring climate change-related policies and activities are gender sensitive.


---
**Page 168**
---

The National Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy seeks to 'empower' men, women, boys and girls vulnerable groups through ensuring equal access to control of resources in the water and sanitation sector. The strategy highlights the barriers that women and girls face when they deal with poor access to water, sanitation and hygiene. In Uganda's Water Policies, the most direct benefits to women and girls are likely to come through the rehabilitation and expansion of water and sanitation facilities. This will provide opportunities for women and girls to participate in income-generating opportunities. The Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy recognises the importance of building the capacity of gender-sensitive work environments and encourages women's participation in formal roles in the planning, development, management and maintenance of water and sanitation projects. It also highlights the need to address barriers that prevent women from expanding education provision in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics and increasing the number of graduates in these areas, but places no emphasis on encouraging women specifically into educational careers.

There is a commitment to stakeholder participation in the planning, implementation and maintenance of water and sanitation projects. Whilst this commitment provides opportunities for women and girls to participate in decision-making processes that affect their communities, they bring an additional time burden which women and girls may struggle to take on. Social traditions which encourage women and girls to defer to men may inhibit women and girls' abilities to participate fully.

The Ministry of Health and the Ministry of Education and Sports Strategic Plan also highlights that, as the main users of primary health centres, women and girls can be severely affected by inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene practices in these centres. Similarly, the Water Sector Development Strategy and the National Water and Sanitation Sector Plan include, amongst its objectives, the expansion and improvement of safe water supply infrastructure in government supported primary schools as well as the construction of sanitation facilities primarily in primary schools.

Stakeholder Mapping:

In Uganda, the Ministry of Water and Environment has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for sanitation services at health centres; the Ministry of Education and Sports, which is responsible for sanitation in schools.

# Problem Tree Analyses

The global evidence reviews for WASH, Wetland, coastal and marine ecosystems, and port infrastructure have identified the most pertinent issues related to gender and climate change in these sectors. To provide a more comprehensive understanding of the issues from a gender and climate lens per sector, problem tree analyses have been created per sector. They have been prepared in line with GCF guidance and draw on and distill the main issues emerging from the global evidence reviews. The purpose statement for each sector is provided at the bottom of this page. This document sets some of the central issues that C2 investments need to address to promote gender equality, issues which have directly informed the opportunities presented in the section 8 of this report.

## 7.1 WASH

The WASH global evidence review and national policy review have both highlighted the multiple ways in which women and girls are affected by inadequate WASH infrastructures and services. They have also pointed to some of the causes of this situation. While national policies in focal countries commit to tackling the core issues, there is a lack of climate change adaptation strategies or plans through policy action, few countries appear to have a considered and comprehensive approach promoting gender equality in WASH infrastructure and services. The problem tree analysis which is presented here seeks to unpack the causes and effects of inadequate WASH infrastructure and services from a gender perspective.

### Figure 4: WASH Problem Tree Analysis

| ROOT CAUSES | EFFECTS |
| --- | --- |
| Governments have been slow to take action to protect women and girls against climate change and build resilience | Women and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change. They face increased risks from natural disasters, such as floods and droughts, which can lead to displacement and loss of livelihoods. |
| Weak state capacity and institutional constraints inhibit implementation of WASH infrastructure and service management | Women and girls have limited access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities, leading to poor health outcomes and increased vulnerability to disease. |
| Insufficient investment in WASH infrastructure and service management | Women and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change. They face increased risks from natural disasters, such as floods and droughts, which can lead to displacement and loss of livelihoods. |
| Gender norms relegated women and girls to the periphery of decision-making processes and underinvestment in girls' education and empowerment | Women and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change. They face increased risks from natural disasters, such as floods and droughts, which can lead to displacement and loss of livelihoods. |
| Women and girls have limited access to information about WASH infrastructure and services | Women and girls are disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change. They face increased risks from natural disasters, such as floods and droughts, which can lead to displacement and loss of livelihoods. |

### PURPOSE STATEMENT

INADEQUATE WASH INFRASTRUCTURE AND SERVICES PLACE A HIGH BURDEN ON WOMEN AND GIRLS

# Climate Fund Managers

## Women and girls spend more time on household chores and family care giving

### Immediate Causes
- Women and girls face increased risk of sexual harassment and gender-based violence.
- Families are often unable to provide adequate food, clothing, shelter, or education for their children.

### Effects
- Girls, especially adolescent girls, do not attend school consistently due to the burden of household chores and family care giving.
- Women and girls are less likely to participate in income-generating activities such as farming, fishing, and small-scale trade.
- People, especially women and girls, have a poorer physical and mental health status.

### Gender Norms and Inequalities
- Few women have the education and skills to be involved in the WASH sector.
- Gender norms and inequalities are sustained or exacerbated.


## 7.2 Wetland, coastal, and marine ecosystems

As regards wetland, coastal, and marine ecosystems, the Gender Assessment found that women are mostly excluded from the formal economy and decision-making processes of value chains or ocean-based economies, as well as from dialogue and decision making around community-based protection and management initiatives.

At the same time, and in spite of gaps in the evidence base on climate-related impacts, women and girls are disproportionately affected by the adverse effects of these situations. The effects of this situation are summarized in the Problem Tree Analysis below (Figure 5).

### Figure 5: Wetland, coastal, and marine ecosystem Problem Tree Analysis

| Root Causes | Immediate Causes | Effects |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Climate change damages wetland, coastal, and marine ecosystems. | Women are mostly excluded from the formal economy and decision-making processes of value chains or ocean-based economies, as well as from dialogue and decision making around community-based protection and management initiatives across relevant sectors. | Design of community protection and management initiatives does not take into account women's access to land, women's access to credit and markets, and women's participation in the formal economy. |

### Purpose Statement
WOMEN ARE MARGINALISED IN THE USL PROTECTION AND MANAGEMENT OF WETLAND, COASTAL AND MARINE ECOSYSTEMS

There is a lack of evidence on how climate change will affect wetland, coastal, and marine ecosystems.

At the same time, rising sea temperatures, levels and acidity are expected to disproportionately affect women's livelihoods in these sectors. Women are more likely than men to be employed in these sectors. Women working in ocean-based industries earn less than men for carrying out the same work. Women working in wetland-based industries face greater risks of exposure to hazardous substances, injury or death at work.

Policy and decision-making on gender equality, women's rights, interests and potential roles in protection and management are not adequately addressed. Women and their communities' access to land and food security is poor. Gender norms and inequalities are sustained or exacerbated.

7.3 Resilient Port Infrastructure

In spite of particularly limited evidence on the intersect between gender, climate and port infrastructure, the Gender Assessment likewise identified widespread exclusion of women from the development and operation of (climate-resilient) ports, particularly in planning, operations and management. The Problem Tree Analysis below summarises the causes and effects of this situation (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Resilient Port Infrastructure Problem Tree Analysis

| ROOTCAUSE | EFFECTS |
| --- | --- |
| Governments have been slow to take action to mitigate women's vulnerability to climate change and build resilience. | Gender norms dictate that port employment is overwhelmingly dominated by men |
| | Women comprise a minority of the workforce, recruits and managers in the port industry |
| | Female employees are often underrepresented in decision-making roles and management |
| | There is a lack of opportunities for women's integration and participation as female employees are not promoted through leadership programmes. |
| | Working environments are insufficient to accommodate the needs of female employees (e.g., by providing sanitation facilities and breastfeeding facilities). |

PURPOSE STATEMENT

WOMEN ARE EXCLUDED FROM THE DEVELOPMENT AND OPERATION OF (CLIMATE-RESILIENT) PORT INFRASTRUCTURE

| CAUSES | EFFECTS |
| --- | --- |
| There is a lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of ports' climate impacts on ecosystems, as well as on women's vulnerability to these impacts. | Women do not benefit equally from the employment benefits of port infrastructure development and operation (e.g., better access to education and health services). |
| Women are often excluded from decision-making roles in the planning and management of port infrastructure projects, despite tending to be more willing to engage in sustainable or environmentally friendly practices. | Policy and decision-makers tend not to consider gender equality as a priority when developing climate-resilient port infrastructure. |
| Gender norms and inequalities are sustained. |

8 Opportunities for promoting greater gender equality

Although global evidence with a combined focus on gender, WASH and climate remains relatively sparse, our review has highlighted the significant burden placed on women and girls by inadequate access to WASH, access that challenges will be exacerbated as climate change is exacerbating. Our national policy paper on water and sanitation points to the fact that in many countries, women and girls spend time collecting water and their increased risk to disease and gender-based violence. Distinguisihing between men and women in terms of their vulnerability to climate change can help promote resilient WASH investment which women and girls have the opportunity to influence so that it responds to their needs and from which they should expect benefit.

The existing evidence base on the interact between gender, climate and, such as the protection and management of water resources, is limited. The evidence for more climate-resilient poor infrastructure is more scant still, but likewise suggests a lack of understanding of the gender-differentiated impacts of climate change. There are also limited data available to understand how women and girls benefit from participation in formal economies, as well as dialogue and decision-making around relevant initiatives.

Despite this sombre picture, our policy analysis shows that national governments in the focal countries have made important commitments to continue improving access to WASH for households, as well as in health facilities. In addition, many of these governments have included gender equality as a priority with an intention to do this in a gender-responsive way, with opportunities for women to shape service design, delivery and management.

Drawing on the analysis above and the evidence we have reviewed, we can identify four areas of opportunity for C4F to promote gender equality through WASH investments. These areas of opportunity are discussed in section 8 of the gender assessment. The gender action plan in Annex 6 summarises how these opportunities will be addressed at the C4F level, and at fund level, in order to mainstream gender issues in the investment lifecycle of the C4F fund.

### Opportunity Area 1

Making better quality, climate-smart WASH facilities more accessible in urban and rural communities and in schools and health facilities

Building on the progress already made to improve the population's access to WASH facilities has the potential to reduce women’s time spent collecting water and cleaning water, freeing up time for other activities, such as attending school, or engaging in income generating activities.

- Reducing the time women and girls spend collecting and cleaning water, freeing up time for other activities, such as attending school, or engaging in income generating activities.
- Reducing the incidence of illness in the family and the associated care burden that women and girls carry.
- Reducing women and girls’ risk of being subjected to GBV as a result of travelling long distances to collect water from unsafe sources, and to conflict in the household associated with expenditure on WASH services.
- Increasing women and girls' physical and mental well-being for better being able to care for themselves, including during menstruation, or an improved sense of personal dignity.


---
**Page 173**
---

* Development of WASH facilities in primary and secondary schools to contribute to improving girls' school attendance and their completion of basic education. This brings benefits for individual girls themselves, but as evidence suggests, also brings it to their families and to society more generally.
* Development of WASH facilities in health centres and hospitals, not only to reduce the incidence of waterborne diseases among patients, but also to improve staff morale.

IPWASH data suggests that in many of the focal countries, good progress is being made in providing the population with access to an improved water source for drinking water, although there is clearly scope for further improvement. However, there are still significant gaps in access to improved sanitation services and hygiene practices.

While improving physical access to WASH services is essential to benefit women and girls, maximising the benefits of this also requires financial, attitudinal and social barriers to be addressed. This calls for infrastructure development that is gender-sensitive as well as addressing these barriers. Examples of complementary initiatives which could be considered include:

* Building family and community understanding of the health benefits of good WASH practices.
* Interventions to help households prioritise WASH services over other uses for scarce financial resources to cover costs associated with WASH services. Such interventions could also usefully cover the importance of good hygiene practices in an effort to reduce the incidence of waterborne diseases and improve overall health, control financial decision-making in households, involving men in these conversations and generally improving gender equality.
* Cross-subsidisation approaches to make sustainable WASH facilities accessible to poorer sections of the community: in such scenarios, weathered sections of the community would pay higher rates so that user rates for the poorest households could be reduced or eliminated.
* Adapting WASH facilities so they are physically accessible to women and girls with disabilities.

It should be noted that working with households and communities on good hygiene practices, including menstruation, can help increase demand for WASH facilities and therefore the return on investment.

### Opportunity Area 2

Giving women voice in the planning, design and management of facilities and initiatives of WASH facilities, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystem protection and management initiatives; and climate-resilient port infrastructure are to meet the needs of women and girls, then their planning, design and management need to be done with an awareness of those needs and a sensitivity to them. As has been shown in many countries, women have a different set of priorities than men within the sector. There are, however, strong social norms which devalue and often silence women's voices, even when women form the majority of users.

A different approach to public consultation and participation is needed to truly provide space for women and girls to inform the planning, design and management of WASH facilities, wetland, coastal and marine ecosystem protection and management initiatives; and climate-resilient port infrastructure. This approach needs to first and foremost identify women and girls as a specific


---
**Page 174**
---

# Stakeholder Group (rather than part of 'the community') and put in place an engagement strategy which counters the power dynamics which often silence women and girls to allow them to be heard and understood.

- Recognising that within the broad category of "women" and "girls", there are intersecting sub-groups with different needs e.g. indigenous women, women with disabilities, children, etc., it is important to ensure that these sub-groups have their voices heard.
  - Methods: Ways to reach out to and convene representatives of these sub-groups need to be identified.

- Conducting a needs assessment approach focusses on a range of women's and girls' voices about their needs and challenges. Leaders within critical sub-groups of women and girls could also be identified as a conduit for engaging the wider group.
  - Working with community leaders and men's leaders to help them understand the rationale behind the WASH facilities and ocean ecosystems protection initiatives, and to build their support for that, thereby avoiding any backlash.

- Holding consultation meetings at times and at venues which are convenient for the women and girls in question. This will ensure that the voices of those who are most likely to be left out, particularly for the chronically poor and for women and girls with disabilities.
  - Taking this approach to engaging with women and girls has the potential to bring real insights into their needs and challenges. It is important to consider the gender balance in the design of activities and initiatives if those employed in designing them are sensitive to the importance of benefiting women and girls, and most critically, decision-making gives due attention to such issues.
  - Enabling this will require the development of a strong relationship with community leaders and men's leaders. It is important that they have had sufficient exposure to women's experiences and taking action to ensure those expectations are met.

- If women are to influence decisions on WASH facilities, and oceans ecosystem protection initiatives, it is sometimes intended, strategic consideration of how to create the space for women's voices to be heard will similarly be needed. The same logic applies in relation to ocean ecosystems protection.
  - The approach is likely to be context specific, but could involve:
    * Identifying and recruiting to the committee women in the community who are perceived by other women as having a strong voice and speaking out on behalf of other women, including women from different sub-groups.
    * Building the skills of the women representatives on the committee to consult with a diverse range of women within the community to get their feedback on the WASH facilities or oceans ecosystem protection initiative and to represent these views in committee meetings.
    * Building male committee members' understanding of the need for management to be informed by women and girls' experiences and developing the skills to ensure women's voices inform committee decision-making.


---
**Page 175**
---

### Opportunity Area 3

Providing women with formal employment opportunities

Low levels of secondary education completion amongst girls in several of the focal countries can make the formal employment of women in the WASH sector challenging. There is, however, positive experience from a number of projects that have successfully employed women within WASH sector enterprises e.g. as sales agents, in meter reading and in rainwater harvesting tank construction.

Opportunities for promoting women's employment in low-skill positions in the WASH sector warrant exploration in the design of CI supported projects. This strategy is not well documented in available literature and further investigation into available experience would be beneficial. Understanding how employers perceive the value of employing women, as well as the barriers to their employment, independence, mobility and safety were allayed are important aspects to explore.

Challenges in employing women in the technical design and development of WASH facilities are particularly acute for those working on projects that focus on water abstraction and even fewer focusing on STEM subjects such as engineering, although data on this is hard to find. These challenges will not be overcome by simply increasing the number of women employed within these roles; rather they remain in education and to pursue careers in STEM. Possible support might involve scholarships for women to study technical subjects at university level or work experience placements for women STEM students to gain practical experience in the workplace.

The literature review suggests that if citizens or caretakers are not in post to maintain WASH facilities in schools, health centres and other public buildings, then it is likely that these facilities will cease to serve. WASH facilities in schools and health centres should therefore include a resource plan for on the going cleaning and maintenance of the facilities.

As regards women's livelihoods, given their roles as farmers, fishers, coastal and marine ecosystems, relevant livelihoods initiatives for women have tended to accommodate, but failed to address, social norms which perpetuate gendered inequalities within industries and value chains. Initiatives to support sustainable livelihoods for women in these sectors should therefore be designed with a focus on such restrictions – on women's time of mobility, for instance – but actively seek to tackle discriminatory norms which prevent women from accessing opportunities.

The development of new climate-resilient ports offers opportunities to recruit women as employees. Attracting women recruits, and supporting their meaningful integration and progression means providing them with the skills they need to be successful in a gender-sensitive and child-friendly work environment. These include training incentives, mentorship programmes to connect women to those in management positions, and family-friendly HR policies and safe (separate) sanitation facilities for women. Initiatives should include a focus on areas from which women tend to be most excluded, most notably port operations and management.


---
**Page 176**
---

8.4 Opportunity Area 4

Understanding the gender equality benefits of gender-responsive facilities and initiatives

Our review has suggested that there is a limited body of experience of interventions working at the nexus between gender, climate change and each of WASH, protection and management of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, and port infrastructure. This opportunity area aims to provide guidance for CFM to contribute to building the evidence base on how gender equality can be promoted through climate-smart WASH, ocean ecosystems protection initiatives and port infrastructure, using C2 as the vehicle.

If CFM wishes to capitalise on this opportunity, it will need to operationalise robust monitoring, evaluation and learning frameworks for its new investments. This would involve:

- Having a clear view of how gender equality is to be promoted through the investment and the intended outcomes.
- Specifying indicators with which to measure changes in gender equity and the achievement of those outcomes.

A methodology, which ideally makes use of quantitative and qualitative approaches to measuring change, and which collects data at different time points to understand the pace of change, would also need to be developed. This could include a baseline survey prior to conducting an impact assessment post project completion, when communities have had some time to enjoy the benefits of the investment.

A role for gender experts should be included in the monitoring, evaluation and learning, including team members with gender expertise.

Financial resources to cover the cost of operationalising a robust monitoring, evaluation and learning framework would also need to be secured.


Such an investment would contribute to CFM’s internal learning, but if results could be published, they would be a public good, offering others working in the gender, climate change, WASH, ocean ecosystems protection or port infrastructure sectors valuable lessons on how to promote gender equality.

It is worth noting that data captured on initiatives to protect ocean ecosystems should include relevant informal economies (where women tend to be concentrated) as well as specific parts of value chains within which they are represented disproportionately.


---
**Page 177**
---

# Understanding the results and impact of gender mainstreaming

Monitoring and reporting will be undertaken in accordance with the principles set out in CMF’s Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS). CMF will monitor and evaluate the actions taken to promote gender equality, their results and impact in two ways: firstly, by monitoring and evaluating the progress towards achieving the Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) for individual projects.

Fund Level Monitoring and Evaluation

The KPIs established for individual programmes will supplement the key impact indicators that have been adopted at fund level against which CMF is committed to monitor performance in all projects. These indicators are linked to selected UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The impact indicators that are most relevant to gender are shown in Table 1 below.

| SDG | Gender Relevant Q2 Impact Indicators |
| --- | --- |
| SDG 5 - Gender Equality | * Number of full-time, equivalent female employees |
| SDG 6 - Clean Water and Sanitation | * Number of women in management positions |
| SDG 8 – Decent Work and Economic Growth | * Total number of people employed with improved access to clean drinking water and sanitation |
|  | * Number of new direct jobs created, gender disaggregated |
|  | * Catalyst effect (Eiffel millions) (total GII and commercial financing) |

Project level monitoring and evaluation

Project companies will implement a monitoring and evaluation plan, which sets out:

* The indicators to be used to monitor how women and girls, as well as other priority social groups e.g., indigenous women, women from chronically poor households and women with disabilities, are being affected by the project activities, including their access to resources, and the impact these projects are having on their lives.
* The process for collecting and analyzing monitoring data, as well as reporting and using the findings.

Each monitoring and evaluation plan will be bespoke to individual projects. However, they will contain comparable quantitative and qualitative indicators and data collection methodologies where appropriate. Depending on a project’s life cycle, monitoring data will be collected wherever possible at regular intervals throughout the project period. The purpose of this ongoing monitoring is for project monitoring to be provide timely information to make project adjustments and address any negative effects.

Relevant outputs of the monitoring and evaluation plan may be incorporated into contracts for EPICs or other sub-contractors, with reviews of the plan’s implementation forming part of the contractor's normal review process.

There is a need to monitor the effectiveness on effective approaches to promoting gender equality through WASH investments. In response to this, CMF may publish some of the results and learnings from its C2 investments.

# Annex 3: Stakeholder mapping interview guide

## Climate Investor 2 Gender Assessment Stakeholder Engagement Interview Guide

### Introduction

Interviewer introduces her/himself and asks respondent to introduce her/himself. Note down:
- Name
- Organisation
- Role

We are working with Climate Fund Managers and the Green Climate Fund on a project proposal to leverage private sector investment into the development of climate resilient water and sanitation infrastructure in developing countries, particularly in Africa where there is a need to leverage large scale private sector investment. As part of the proposal development process, we are seeking to understand how private sector actors can be engaged in this process. The project aims to help promote gender equality, for example by improving access to quality water and sanitation services, enabling women to participate more fully in economic activities, increasing their earning opportunities and enabling women to benefit from the infrastructure development, including through formal and informal employment opportunities. We are keen to learn from the experience of experts in this field and would like to invite you to share your views on how private sector actors can be engaged to inform the proposal to be submitted to the Green Climate Fund for financial support and the design of private sector engagement strategies with Climate Fund Managers. The interview will take approximately 45 minutes. Are you happy to proceed?

### Interview Questions

1. (In Country X), what work is being done to promote gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure development? Explain:
   - Employment of WASH services to reduce domestic and health burden on women and girls;
   - Involvement of women in the design and management of water and sanitation services;
   - Employment of women in the development of water and sanitation infrastructure and services.

2. Which organisations are involved in this work, and what are their roles?

3. From your perspective, which organisations are most involved in promoting gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure and services? What do they do to promote gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure and services development? At what level(s) do they operate (national, sub-national, community)?

4. The assessment is in the process of conducting identified four opportunities for promoting gender equality in water and sanitation infrastructure and services, which we shared with you in advance of this interview. We would like to discuss two of them in more detail with you.

# Annex 5: Full Country Analyses

## Bangladesh

### Water, sanitation and hygiene at a glance in Bangladesh

#### Access to Drinking Water:
- **98.9%** of the population has access to an improved source for drinking water.
- Of which **53.5%** has access to safely managed drinking-water facilities.
- And **39.2%** has access to basic drinking-water facilities.

#### Time spent collecting water:
- Spending about 30 minutes to collect water.

#### Access to Sanitation:
- **78.1%** of the population has access to an improved facility, with which **56.7%** has access to safely managed sanitation services.
- And **23.1%** has access to basic sanitation facilities.

#### Open defecation:
- The remaining proportion uses unimproved sanitation services.

#### Hygiene:
- **58.4%** of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.

#### WASH in schools:
- Nationally, **82.76%** of schools have access to basic service for drinking water.
- And **56.3%** of schools have access to basic service for sanitation.

#### WASH in health facilities:
- **64.4%** of health care facilities have basic service for drinking water.
- And **31.3%** has basic service for sanitation.
- And **37.6%** has basic service for hygiene.

### Policy context
Improving water, sanitation and hygiene is a key part of Bangladesh's national policy framework as identified in the National Sustainable Development Strategy 2010. Recognising the increasing impacts of climate change, rapid urbanisation and water contamination, including saltwater intrusion in fresh water sources, the National Sustainable Development Strategy 2010 addresses water, sanitation and hygiene under strategic priorities 7 on environment, natural resources management and biodiversity and priority 9 on the urban environment. To address the challenges faced, the policy aims to:
- Diversify water supply with a shift to surface water and encourage rainwater harvesting.
- Encourage reuse of treated wastewater.
- Expand the use of technology to improve the efficient use of scarce resources, including water.
- Enhance investment in safe drinking water.


---
**Page 192**
---

Two other policies, the National Strategy for Water Supply and Sanitation (NWSWSS of 2014) and the National Hygiene Promotion Strategy for Water Supply and Sanitation Sector (NHPSWSS of 2012), focus on climate change adaptation. The NWSWSS seeks to promote the sustainable use of improved water supply and sanitation infrastructure, while also addressing challenges related to climate change such as increased vulnerability to safe water and sanitation facilities, especially for women and people with disabilities.

Bangladesh's location makes it highly susceptible to the adverse effects of climate change, hindering its development. The Bangladesh Climate Change Action Plan (BCCAP) of 2008 looks further into the negative impacts of climate change on water resources in Bangladesh. These include a decrease in water availability and an increase in water contamination from rapid urbanisation, saline intrusion, and pollution. Women are particularly vulnerable to these changes due to their role as primary caregivers for children and their responsibility for water management within households.

The NWSWSS outlines 10 strategic objectives to combat climate change challenges, including ensuring safe drinking water, undertaking specific approaches for vulnerable people, gender mainstreaming, improving hygiene promotion, adapting to climate change and undertaking integrated and accountable development approaches. Strategy 10 seeks to include gender mainstreaming in all service planning, implementation and monitoring activities. It also aims to ensure that women are involved as decision-makers at all levels of the organization.

Improving hygiene awareness in schools is addressed in the National Education policy. This includes hand washing and installing modernized toilets for both girls and boys to reduce the spread of water-borne diseases.


| 1. Food security, social protection and health; |
| --- | 
| 2. Climate-resilient Disaster management; |
| 3. Infrastructure; |
| 4. Research and knowledge management; |
| 5. Monitoring and evaluation; |
| 6. Capacity building and institutional strengthening. |

Page 81 of 143


---
**Page 193**
---

Pillar 1 places an importance on women and girls by increasing community involvement in planning and increasing resilience of vulnerable groups, including women and children, through development of community-based disaster risk reduction (CBDRR) plans and strengthening of local governance for social protection. Programme T3P also seeks to complete a comprehensive study on the impact of climate change on water and gender relations and to develop recommendations to address these in all actions under the BCCSA.

None of the policies reviewed explicitly consider advancing women's formal roles in the WASH sector, but many discussed the need for community involvement in the planning, implementation, operation and maintenance (PIM) of WASH services. The policy on Water Resources Management also considers the advancement of women’s involvement in entrepreneurial activities, with a focus on the non-farming uses in rural areas; training and use of technology to improve water quality and reduce micro-socioeconomic status gaps for women; and for ensuring proper waste treatment before industrial waste disposal; and gender mainstreaming in all aspects of water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) services. The policy on Water Resources Management also considers the advancement of women’s formal roles within the WASH sector under the Sustainable Development Policy, there is a broader emphasis, within all sectors, on career development opportunities for women at all levels, including in leadership positions, and gender equality and governance at all levels. Additionally, due to only 21% of women completing upper secondary schooling* and 19.8% enrolment in tertiary education**, “women are limited in the careers prospects within the WASH sector” (Ministry of Local Government, Rural Development and Cooperatives, 2016).

Stakeholder Mapping

In Bangladesh, the Policy Support branch of the Ministry of Local Government, Rural Development and Cooperatives has primary responsibility for Water and Sanitation Supply and Strategy. Other national agencies involved in WASH services include the Department of Public Health Engineering (DPHE), for hygiene, and Ministry of Water Resources (MoWR), covering irrigation, flooding, and climate-related water protection. There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national level, including the District Water Supply and Sanitation Office (DWSSO) at district level, as well as the private sector, national and international CSOs, and community-based committees. A wide range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private sector and CSO engagement in WASH. Figure 6 provides a visual mapping of the actions involved.

# WASH stakeholders in Bangladesh

## Key actors and their roles:

### Government:
- **Ministry of Water Resources (MOWR):** Responsible for water resources management.
- **Ministry of Health & Family Welfare (MOHFW):** For health and family welfare, including WASH.
- **Ministry of Finance (MoF):** For budgetary allocation.

### Development Partners:
- **ADB:** Provides technical assistance and funding.
- **UNICEF:** Supports WASH programs.
- **World Bank:** Coordinates WASH programming through WASH Committees.
- **International Organization for Migration (IOM):** Works on migration-related WASH issues.
- **Bilateral Donors:** Such as USAID, DFID, etc.

### Community:
- **Community-WASH Unit:** Engages with Water Users Associations/Committees/Sanitation and Hygiene Clubs.
- **Children and youth groups:** Participate in WASH activities.

## Governance Institutions:

- **Ministry of Environment, Forests and Climate Change (MEFCC):** Central to WASH governance.
- **Department of Public Health Engineering and Local Development (DPHEL):** For water supply and sanitation.
- **Water Development Board (WDB):** For water resources management.
- **Bangladesh Water Supply and Sanitation Authority:** Under Ministry of Power & Energy.
- **Fauji Fawaneh Foundation:**
- **Bridges for Bangladesh Company Ltd.**

## Sub-national government stakeholders:

### District level:
- **District Health Office (DHO):** Coordinates WASH programs in the district.
- **Local NGOs:** Such as BRAC, Grameen Bank, World Vision.

### Upazila level:
- **Upazila Development Committee (UDC):** Coordinates WASH projects at this level.

# 9.2 Botswana

Water, sanitation and hygiene in Botswana at a glance

Access to drinking water - **92.2%** of the population has access to a basic level of drinking water and **4.7%** of the population has access to a limited level of drinking water. **1%** of the population relies on unimproved drinking water whilst **1.3%** of the population has access only to surface drinking water sources. There is no data available for the proportion of the population with access to safely managed drinking water.

Access to sanitation - **80%** of the population has access to basic sanitation services and **5.6%** of the population has access to a limited level of sanitation services. **4.6%** of the population relies on unimproved sanitation facilities, whilst **3.8%** of the population practices open defecation in sanitation services. There is no data available on the proportion of the population with access to safely managed sanitation services.

Data on hygiene, WASH in schools and WASH in health facilities is not available.


Policy context

Botswana's 11th National Development Plan covers the period April 2017 – March 2023. It was adopted in December 2016 and follows the country’s celebration of the fifty years anniversary of Independence. The plan sets out Botswana’s vision for the next twenty years, providing an opportunity for the country to define its roadmap for the next twenty years. With an all-encompassing theme of “Achieving a Prosperous and Equitable Society”, the 11th National Development Plan is anchored through the expansion of the domestic economy while empowering Botswanans to meaningfully participate in the development of their country. Achieving the goals and objectives of Vision 2036 will require a shift from the current export-led development model towards an export-oriented, labour-intensive, and private sector-driven model.

Chapter two of the 11th National Development Plan looks at Botswana’s population situation and development challenges. The plan outlines that Botswana has one of the youngest populations in Africa with **40%** of its population below the age of fifteen years old. This includes a subsection on population and water resources and a subsection on migration, population distribution and urbanisation. In these subsections, some of Botswana’s key water-related challenges are outlined including: high levels of unimproved sanitation; poor access to the water sector, and the inadequate capacity of sanitation infrastructure, and pressure on sanitation services from rapid urbanization.

The plan outlines the negative health consequences of poor sanitation and waste management issues such as pollution, littering and illegal dumping. It also outlines the negative health consequences of water shortages and droughts, which cause food insecurity leading to malnutrition. The plan states

[103] Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2018)

# CLIMATE INVESTOR

## FUND MANAGERS

The Government will continue with measures to address environmental health risks associated with poor sanitation, hygiene and waste management and pollution throughout the value chain from the source of raw materials through to the end user in the affected sectors.

In light of its impact on the agricultural sector leading to food insecurity and malnutrition, the plan states that consideration must be given to the role of women and men in agriculture. The approach is to transform our agricultural systems towards more productive, sustainable and resilient systems.

The plan targets specific gender considerations in the water and sanitation sectors. The plan does however state that the Government recognises the need for equal participation and representation of women and men in all development processes, and that NDP 11 provides an opportunity for all sectors to articulate gender issues that they will address in the execution of their respective policies.

Although the policy largely ignores gender issues related to employment in the water and sanitation sectors, NPD 11 does discuss the low participation of females in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) subjects. The plan states that this is due to a combination of factors including having an upper secondary completion rate of 66.3% (compared to 62.6% for men) (UNIS), and a tertiary enrolment rate at 14.95% (compared to 20.96% for men) (UNIS). The policy fails to articulate how this challenge will be addressed in the execution of its respective policies.

While the National Development Plan discusses the water and sanitation sectors, it does so in general terms within the broader context of Botswana's development as a whole. Botswana's National Water Policy however provides a more detailed framework for addressing these issues. The policy aims to facilitate access to water of suitable quality and standard for the citizenry and provide the foundations for sustainable development of water resources in support of economic growth, diversification and poverty reduction. The policy also sets out a strategic framework for the following goals:

- Promotion of conservation and restoration of the nation's water resources
- Promotion of effective, efficient, sustainable management of water resources
- Promotion of the equitable and efficient use of water resources
- Reduction in water-related disasters and impacts on people
- Assurance of access and affordability of water for all
- Protection and restoration of the environment
- Promotion of water security

Like the National Development Plan, the National Water Policy (2012) identifies water shortages and droughts and water supply for agriculture and industry as key challenges facing the sector. The policy also raises concerns about the quality of water resources in Botswana. In response to these issues, the policy offers policy statements and objectives in a variety of thematic areas across the sector including Water for Growth and Development, Water for Environment and Tourism, Water for Agriculture, Water for Mining and Industry, Water for Energy, Information Management and Dissemination, Research and Development, International Cooperation and Water Security.

However, also like the National Development Plan, women's roles in the water and sanitation sectors are largely ignored. The policy does state that gender and social equity in accessing water resources will be ensured and, in particular, women shall be empowered to participate fully in issues and


---
**Page 197**
---

decisions relating to sustainable development and management of water resources but does not go into specifics on how this will be achieved.

As the National Water Policy offers a strategic policy framework for Botswana's water and sanitation sectors, it also (indirectly) offers the same for the health sector. The National Health Policy offers valuable insight into the consequences of the challenges facing the water and sanitation sectors on public health. The National Health Policy discusses the impact of water shortages and their potential to increase the incidence of cholera outbreaks in Botswana. It also highlights the consequences of a lack of access to adequate sanitation facilities and access to safe drinking water.

The National Health Policy also discusses the interplay between climate change, the water sector and public health. The policy states that water scarcity is expected to increase as temperatures rise and conditions are more conducive for mosquitoes to breed during rainy seasons. Cholera is also likely to increase in Botswana due to increased rainfall and flooding. These factors will lead to an increase in vector-borne diseases and may lead to malnutrition in the longer term. The policy does not however discuss the gender dynamics of these issues.

Botswana's draft Climate Change Response Policy (2016) and the National Climate Change Strategy for Botswana (2018) continue to look at the effects of climate change on the water and sanitation sectors. The draft Climate Change Response Policy is a statement of intent and vision of what Botswana will do in response to climate change, while the National Climate Change Strategy aims to help operationalise the tenets of the draft policy and provide necessary guidance on how the policy objectives will be achieved.

The draft Climate Change Response Policy discusses several issues related to the water and sanitation sectors. These include increased temperatures leading to stress leading to food insecurity and water and sanitation management strategies. Like the National Health Policy, the draft Climate Change Response Policy also highlights that climate change is likely to increase the incidence of cholera outbreaks through increased temperatures, drought and floods, and also through the spread of water borne, water-related and vector borne diseases.

The policy recognises the need to mainstream gender considerations into development planning to ensure that climate change response measures are gender sensitive. It states that this shall be achieved through empowering women and youth to actively participate in the implementation of climate related projects and programmes. The draft Climate Change Response Policy also aims at building Botswana's resilience to climate change, including gender and climate change into academic curriculum at all levels.

The National Climate Change Strategy for Botswana helps to operationalise the draft Climate Change Response Policy's discussion of the water and sanitation sections through the below policy goals to be realised for the sectors:

* Utilising renewable water sources for the benefit of Botswana
* Integrating climate change response measures in the water planning processes across all economic sectors.
* Conserving and defining potential water aquifers and adopting appropriate measures of protection for water security and sustainability


---
**Page 198**
---

# Climate Fund Managers

* Promotion of rainwater harvesting, water re-use, and recycling for domestic, agricultural, industrial, and commercial purposes
* Promoting appropriate livestock systems for livestock, particularly in rural areas
* Employing accounting and valuation tools to support water management decision systems

These specific policy goals for the water and sanitation sectors feed into the broader policy objectives of the strategy, which are:

* To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of Botswana's Vision 2030 of prosperity for all.
* To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of current and future Botswana National Development Plans (NDPs).
* To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of the global goals set out in the Paris Agreement.
* To ensure climate change adaptation and mitigation action supports the achievement of Botswana's NDC, including the GHG emissions reduction target of 15% by 2030.

To frame these policy goals, the strategy outlines a framework for climate change adaptation and mitigation actions, allowing for a spectrum of actors to be involved in implementation.

The National Climate Change Strategy devotes special attention to gender equality and ensuring that it serves as an enabling factor for climate change adaptation. Gender is not only a cross-cutting issue than in the draft Climate Change Response Policy, and actionable gender-based strategies with strategic interventions including:

* Developing the Climate Change Gender Action Plan (CCGAP), with an emphasis on women as drivers of climate resilience, and women's role in water and energy and healthcare provision.
* Promote equitable participation of women farmers and female-headed households in Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) programmes, agri-tourism, and access to conservation agriculture technologies.
* Ensure equitable gender access to the proposed endowment fund providing low-cost finance to climate change adaptation projects, aligned to existing similar projects.
* Ensure equal representation of women in natural resources management through their equitable participation in CBMRR processes.
* Ensure that gendered differences of climate change are mainstreamed into climate change adaptation.
* Ensure the full participation of women and female-headed households in disaster management and preparedness, to address both the higher vulnerability of women and children, and to plan for their post-disaster burden placed on them due to their dual roles as producers and carers.

## Policy Context for Water Resources (2012) and National Policy for Basic Sanitation (2019)

The National Policy for Water Resources (2012) and the National Policy for Basic Sanitation (2019) both discuss in-depth the importance of WASH in daily life and outline clear objectives to ensure the impact on human health, environment, economy, and society. The National Policy for Water Resources reviews the lack of subterranean water resource development, and waters used in agriculture, tourism, and industry are not being properly managed or protected. This leads to a situation where vulnerable communities, including increase in conflict, situation of water pollution, and changes in natural characteristics of water resources, are more exposed. The National Policy for Basic Sanitation aims at improving access to sanitation services, including safe and private open defecation.

Data on hygiene is not available.
WASH in health facilities: Nationally, 80.2% had a basic service in drinking water, data on access to hygiene and sanitation is insufficient.
WASH in schools: Nationally, 61% has a basic service in hygiene, data on access to drinking water and sanitation is insufficient.

The National Policy of Basic Sanitation identifies issues with women and girls participating consistently in the current resources that are available, but not keep up the demand in solid waste disposal. Due to this there remain significant challenges for WASH services in terms of gender equality and the adverse effects caused by poor or inadequate sanitation. The national policy lays out clear objectives and programmes to overcome these challenges.

The National Environmental Policy (1981), with several amendments, with the most recent being issued in 2013 and the National Water Policy for Conservation and Development elaborates on the importance of mitigating climate change policies, practices, and action plans. It assesses the impact of climate change for human and economic activity but remains gender blind in both the challenges and objectives of the policy. The policy also highlights the need to improve access to safe drinking water and sanitation facilities, improve infrastructure to capture and store water resources, and create access to water in non-conventional ways such as the use of oases. It also sees a value in the utilization of the circular economy for WASH services and the importance of gender equality.

Page 88 of 143


---
**Page 200**
---

these challenges through rationing the use of water, expanding the use of environmental resources licensing for activities that utilise environmental resources, increase education on climate change and the environment, and promote the development of new technologies to improve the quality of life and protection of the environment. The National Policy on Climate Change discusses the impacts extreme weather has had in Brazil since 2015, including droughts, floods, and heatwaves, which have been more frequent in the country. It reviews the idea of reusing water in urban planning and calls for action to protect water resources in the National Policy for Water Resources and National Policy for Basic Sanitation. It also considers the need to promote sustainable development through policies that reduce greenhouse gas emissions.

The National Plan of Policies for Women (2013) explores the importance of including gender lenses in the sustainable development agenda, as well as the need to implement policies to defeat water as a public good, ensure access to water by building cisterns, ensuring agricultural security, and utilization of environmental resources. The plan also seeks to promote women's participation in decision-making processes work and equal participation in leadership and decision making. Finally, the Plan seeks to amplify access to education and labour market, including within the agricultural sector and formal education in science and technology.

Though neither the National Education Plan (2014) nor the National Policy for Health (2017) discuss specific policies on water or sanitation, they both seek to promote gender equality and women's rights and access to basic services. In Brazil 67.32% of the population has completed Upper Secondary school, of which 72.73% are female and 62.18% are male. While only 55.14% of the population is enrolled in tertiary education, women make up a majority (59.0%) of students at this level. The National Education Plan seeks to reduce discrimination and inequality, of all forms while also promoting socioenvironmental sustainability. The National Policy for Health aims to improve access to health services, including to healthcare and teach on the intersection of health, sustainable development, and the environment.

Several of the policies reviewed touch on employment opportunities e.g., the National Sanitation Fund promotion of employment within the sector, and the National Water Resource Policy promoting education and training in water resources management. The National Plan of Policies for Women for women in WASH-related sectors. Brazil has a high percentage of entrepreneurship activity, with about 21% of the population in early stages of entrepreneurial activity, and 10% had established businesses. It is to this end that the National Plan of Policies for Women (2013) seeks to promote economic autonomy for women in Brazil (2013) wishes to promote economic and financial autonomy of women seeking entrepreneurship.

As a federal state, Brazil's lower tiers of government play an important role in the provision of sustainable WASH services, and most federal policies are created with broad objectives leaving each regional government to interpret them as they see fit. The National Policy for Regional Development (2007) placed greater importance on climate resilience measures and general environmental protection than previous policies. It also sought to promote gender equality within regions and between regions, strengthening science, technology, and innovation sectors, and climate action. The policy outlines 8 main principles to focus on in regional development, including the need for a participatory approach. The policy seeks to ensure that women have equal access to economic opportunities within all the regions, both from a socio-economic and gender perspective. There are no specific discussion of WASH-related challenges and objectives in the policy, nor are there any gender-specific strategies within outlined climate action measures.

### 9.4 Djibouti

Water, sanitation and hygiene in Djibouti at a glance

**Page 201**

Access to drinking water: **98.8%** of the population has access to an improved source of drinking water, although for the majority (95%), this is basic access.[12] None of the population has access to drinking water classified as safely managed. The remaining 9.2% of the population has access to either other improved drinking water or surface water only. Women are the main collectors of water[13]. **88.6%** of the population has access to improved sanitation services, which takes 30 minutes or less to get to an improved drinking source.[14]

Access to sanitation: **73.8%** of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which **57.1%** have access to basic sanitation services and **26.7%** have access to basic sanitation services and 7.1% have access to limited sanitation services.[10] Only 40% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services while 18.2% practices open defecation.[15]

Data on hygiene, WASH in schools and WASH in health facilities is not available.

**Policy context**

After several decades of implementing various developmental policies that focused on the short and medium term, the Djibouti Vision (2010–2035) presents a long-term developmental vision for sustainable development. The vision sets out an overall strategy within which various ministries are tasked with setting up a more detailed action. The vision focuses on the following policy objectives for water, sanitation and hygiene:

- Providing good sanitation for the urban environments to have clean cities.
- Developing a national water supply system that is based on renewable resources.
- Modernization of infrastructure (sanitation, water access and port development) and increasing the capacity of existing facilities.
- Simplifying the irrigation systems to ration water use.

The vision focuses primarily on water supply throughout the country and its implications for other areas of the economy such as agriculture, industry, tourism, health, education, and sanitation and hygiene. This is complemented by the climate section of the vision, which includes sustainable water management as an area of priority, amongst other objective strategies unrelated to WASH. Furthermore, Vision 2035 identifies the link between water availability and food security in the country, taking into account past recorded instances of droughts and flooding which will impact food production and supplies throughout the country.

The 2010 Development Plan for the Primary Sector and the 2015 Strategy of Accelerated Growth and Promotion of Employment echo the focus on access to water for agriculture and livestock, as well as


---
**Page 202**
---

The hygiene risks that a lack of sanitation infrastructure poses in cities. The main consequences identified in these policies are the spread of disease and other contamination of water sources and structural barriers to women's participation in development. Although less explicit about addressing gender and the need to address gender inequality throughout the primary sector and for growth, they do not directly link WASH issues to gender. The problems identified, such as health consequences and built-in inequalities, are more implicit than direct.

They do acknowledge that various stakeholders, including women and youth, should have a larger role in the policy-making process.


Beyond water access, Vision 2035 also identifies sanitation and hygiene issues related to water insecurity. Liquid and solid waste management is identified as one of the main WASH challenges, identifying that few households in Djibouti are connected through a collective waste management system. The policy does not directly address this issue or its implications for women; however, it does indirectly link the strategy also identifies the disproportionate effects of poverty on women, including the provision of health care as evidenced by the growing impacts of the AIDS epidemic on women and children. This is an important consideration in relation to water access and sanitation, which can be an obstacle for women, while water provision to health infrastructure (e.g., hospitals) also presents a challenge. The policy does not provide any information about how they will ensure that all households have good sanitation facilities and modern water and sanitation infrastructure, with no real specifics on how they will achieve this. They also emphasize the importance of identifying more water sources and increasing access to them, but do not mention the need for improved sanitation or hygiene.

Education is a strong focus of Djibouti's 2011–2021 Gender Policy, the policy that most identifies how water and sanitation challenges are an extra burden on women. The policy covers accessibility to water provision and sanitation facilities, as well as education about good hygiene practices in their immediate environment. It identifies the woman’s role is mainly within the domestic sphere, meaning they have a greater responsibility for managing water and sanitation at home, particularly when there are shortages within the household. Women, moreover disproportionately suffer the effects of desertification, caused by changes in the climate, in accessing water. The gender policy also highlights that women's education is important to ensure their ability to manage water and preserve quality of drinking water at home and proper use of latrines in schools and in communities. The Gender Policy 2011-2021 also mentions that women are more likely than men to be illiterate, and consequently, the informal role they might be able to play is reinforcing negative sanitation practices in their household. This informal role can be supported through capacity building programmes for women and by relieving the time-poorness with women’s experience due to the burden of other households.


The section on gender in Vision 2035 also acknowledges women's role in enforcing new behaviours and practices; however, it emphasizes how "in order to improve women's situations, men should be included as well". The policy highlights that women are more likely than men to be illiterate. Women are moving towards women and their approach with themselves" (Djibouti Vision 2010, p. 58). Both policies further highlight the importance of education for women in relation to water access and sanitation. Local authorities are mentioned as needing to play a decisive role in the planning, programming and budgeting of development actions to ensure gender mainstreaming. Tribal chiefs and traditional leaders should also be involved in the process. The policy highlights that the inclusion of women in budgeting processes as an important starting point to establish their role in


---
**Page 203**
---

managing resources, both within households as well as at higher community or government levels.

Lastly, the political, economic and social introduction of youth and teenagers to equality and equity concerns is a key element in the development of human capital for the future.

Currently, the completion rate for upper secondary is higher for women than for men; this presents a great opportunity to incorporate women into the workforce due to the higher levels of education they can take into the workforce. The 2018–2022 National Strategy for Social Protection identifies a role for women in social protection and specifically mentions their participation in the informal sector and agriculture. The social protection strategy does not further specify jobs for women, but states that it aims to create jobs for women in the informal sector (i) by creating more opportunities within the sector in creating jobs and (ii) seeking to integrate the maximum of informal sector assets into the formal sector, a woman-dominated sector. Moreover, they suggest that the issue of water and food scarcity can be better addressed if women are involved in decision-making processes. In line with all other policies, they identify that the awareness of all the institutional, private and associative actors, the seriousness of the situation and how their contribution is most important. Djibouti's 2020–2035 National Development Plan (NDP) also identifies the need for women to be taken care of specifically as their access issues are especially severe. The Sanitation policy does not further identify any issues or opportunities specific to women or men, making the policy gender blind.

Stakeholder Mapping

In Djibouti, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water, Fisheries, Livestock and Fisheries Resources and the National Office of Water and Sanitation have primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, the leading ministry in implementing the 2018–2022 National Strategy for Social Protection, and the Ministry of Planning, Urbanism, Environment and Territorial Development, who monitor WASH delivery service. There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including regional and district health offices, water supply companies, and local authorities. A number of development partners are involved in supporting government, and international and national CSD engagement in WASH. Figure 8 provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

# Indonesia

## Water, sanitation and hygiene in Ivory Coast at a glance

Access to drinking water: 93.3% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water201; 87.4% have access to a basic level of drinking water, and 0.5% has access to a limited level of drinking water201. The remaining 6.2% of the population uses unimproved sources for drinking water201.

Access to sanitation: 93.6% of the population has access to an improved sanitation facility201; 86.5% has access to a basic level of sanitation service, while 6.1% have access to a limited level of sanitation service. 7.5% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 6.1 who practice open defecation201.

Access to hygiene: 94.1% of the population has access to basic hygiene while 5.9% has limited access201.

WASH in health facilities: Nationally, 80.2% have a basic service in drinking water, data on access to hygiene and sanitation is insufficient201.

WASH in schools: Nationally, 72.7% has a basic service in drinking water; 40.4% has a basic service in sanitation, and the 58.9% has basic access to hygiene201.


As a country with over 200 million spread out across 6,000 islands, and a major reliance on rainwater supply for its population of more than 300 million people, Indonesia places natural resource management and recovery as a priority in its Long-Term National Development Plan (2050). With the growing effects of climate change impacting the water cycle, rapid urbanization, and contamination of water supply it is essential to ensure that all citizens have access to safe drinking water, sanitation and hygiene for development and well-being for all people. While it recognises the need for gender mainstreaming in policies to make them more inclusive and effective, there are still many challenges.

National Medium-Term Development Plans serve to identify policy priorities in a given period, within the overarching framework of the Long-Term National Development Plan. The most recent National Medium-Term Development Plan (2020) elaborates further the importance of maintaining natural resources for future generations and ensuring access to safe drinking water, sanitation and hygiene.

The plan lays out 5 directions: human resource development, infrastructure development, regulatory simplification, institutional strengthening and capacity building. It also highlights the three pillars of the 3R approach - Reduce, Reuse, Recycle - and the circular economy to increase effective waste disposal. The plan sets a target for 90% of households in urban areas to have access to improved sanitation services by 2025, with an emphasis on improving wastewater management.

As for water resources, it seeks to improve infrastructure, including dam capacity and forest restoration, to meet the goal of 100% of the population having access to safe drinking water. The policy also sees community involvement as a main way to ensure implementation
