# Climate

## Fund Managers

and management of safe and adequate water and sanitation facilities and practices. This includes increasing women’s role in development and decision-making, especially within health, education, labour markets, and agriculture.

While the Long-Term National Development Plan establishes the importance of improving water and sanitation access and infrastructure, the Long-Term Strategy for Low Carbon and Climate Resilience 2050 (2021) explores further the impacts that climate change has on the country’s development, especially in terms of water resources. The increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are causing increased flooding and longer dry seasons, resulting in reduced availability of raw water supply, which is a major constraint to economic activity and human well-being as well as reducing economic activity. The Strategy identifies the limited access to channelled wastewater sewerage for treatment of grey water as one of the main reasons for the persistence of open defecation that disproportionately affects women due to limited access to water supplies for domestic use, limited access to safe sanitation facilities, and an imbalance in capacity for understanding the innovative technologies required. The Strategy outlines a number of strategies for mitigation and adaptation to overcome these growing challenges while ensuring an equal balance to continue economic growth, improving access to infrastructure and increasing community knowledge and capacity on water and sanitation management.

Alongside the Long-Term Strategy for Low Carbon and Climate Resilience, the National Strategy for Community-Based Total Sanitation (2008) touches on ongoing sanitation challenges in Indonesia, with lack of access to safe drinking water, poor hygiene practices, contamination of surface water, water contamination, and lack of progress by local governments being the main reasons given for the persistence of open defecation. The National Strategy aims to improve access to clean drinking water and private sector subsidies communal sanitation facilities, and encourage community engagement to ensure every household has safe drinking water treatment and access to handwashing stations in their homes. It also seeks to increase awareness on the importance of community-based sanitation and there is no consideration to the specific sanitation needs of women.

All the policies discuss increasing the capacity of communities and workers to lead to increased community participation, with a particular focus on women’s empowerment. The National Strategy providing women specifically with career opportunities in the WASH sector. The Long-Term Strategy foresees supporting sanitation entrepreneurship in areas with potential, facilitating entrepreneurship to create jobs for women and men. The Long-Term Strategy for Low Carbon and Climate Resilience seeks to improve job activities with the consideration of gender equality. Based on available data, women are more likely than men to be employed in the informal sector (52% of the 62.7% of women completing upper secondary education, compared to 58.4% of men). Whilst employment rates for women and men were relatively low at 36.3%, 19.8% of women enrolled in tertiary education, compared to 33.7% of men.

The Presidential Instructions on Gender Mainstreaming (2006) emphasise the need to mainstream gender considerations in all policies and programmes. Most policies do recognise this but do not map out how they will consistently differentiate how strategies will specifically target the different needs of women and men.

### 9.6 Ivory Coast

[1st Jan 2020]

[3rd Oct 2018]

Water, sanitation and hygiene in Ivory Coast at a glance

Access to drinking water: 79.8% of the population has access to an improved water source, of which 35.2% has access to safely managed drinking water, 35.7% has access to basic drinking water and 8.9% has no access to drinking water at all. Of those with access to drinking water, 60.2% of the population accesses unimproved drinking water, leaving 60% who only access surface water.174 Women are the main collectors of water.175 Those with safely managed drinking water have water access on premises. Those with basic drinking water have water access within 30 minutes and those without access to water whilst those with a limited level of access to drinking water spend more than 30 minutes collecting water.

Access to sanitation: 57.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of whom 34.6% has access to basic sanitation services and 22.5% has access to limited sanitation services. 25.3% of the population practices open defecation whilst the remaining 17.6% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services.176

Hygiene: 21.6% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.179 There is insufficient data on WASH in schools and health facilities available.

Policy context

In line with its 2040 Vision, the overall aim of Ivory Coast’s 2016—2020 National Development Plan is to create a state in which the population as a whole is better off, translating into a significant reduction of poverty. The plan aims for higher class performance and economic growth, being more proactive than previous plans, and as such it is a reflection of the country’s relative stability and the increased levels of productivity the country has enjoyed in recent years. The 2016—2020 National Development Plan identifies three main areas that focus on:

* Quality institutions and governance
* The preparedness and capacities needed for women and men to forge an emergent and prosperous future
* Changes in production and consumption patterns so as to bring Ivory Coast onto the global stage

Development of strategic infrastructure acting as a lever for Ivory Coast to move onto the global stage while abiding by environmental sustainability principles.

Beneficial integration into regional and global trade and other networks.


Although the plan has a strong focus on structural economic reforms, this is balanced with an understanding that these go hand in hand with the development of human capital. Water and sanitation is a cross-cutting issue that is identified under several of these five pillars of the plan, most


---
**Page 208**
---

# Climate Fund Managers

## Prominently, under pillars 1, 2 and 5. This highlights the challenges that Ivory Coast faces in water and sanitation, ranging from governance of resources, quality of health services and the population's health, poor infrastructure, lack of access to clean drinking water and sanitation systems, the general lack of access to water and sustainable sanitation systems, the national plan clearly acknowledges the heavier burden on populations in cities which are rapidly urbanizing and the lack of social safety nets for vulnerable groups. Consequences such as the spread of diseases like cholera, and food shortages due to water scarcity often impacts women more severely.

### Health Consequences

The National Development Plan (2012-2025) further discusses the health consequences of water scarcity and cleanliness. They have identified a positive correlation between variations in rainfall, temperature and the incidence rate of malaria, respiratory infections and diarrheal diseases. They have also highlighted that these diseases are more prevalent during the dry season than the rainy season, due to inadequate wastewater drainage infrastructure. These challenges stem from the differences in rainfall between wet and dry seasons increasing, a drop in surface water availability and further decline in groundwater levels.

### Women's Health

There is no mention of the differentiating impact between women and men, but the significant health consequences for women are clear. The lack of access to clean drinking water and sanitation is a disadvantage in this area. The programme promoted the introduction of climate change adaptation in all new legislation and policy instruments, identifying the importance of including diverse actors in their creation, including women's associations and NGOs who have localised knowledge of challenges and opportunities.

### National Development Plan

The National Development Plan (2012-2025) outlines that Ivory Coast should aim to increase accessibility to drinking water, build and repair pump stations, transfer the responsibility and ownership of adopting laws and regulations on communal land. Local communities, local authorities, women's associations and further diversified representation. The wider goal in sanitation and drainage is to create a sustainable system for all communities. This includes the creation of a national strategy for improving the hygiene and sanitation conditions in the most vulnerable communities. Ivory Coast’s Sectoral Note on Sanitation (2012-2025) goes into more detail on improving sanitation in the country, with a focus on increasing access to improved sanitation facilities in rural areas, mainly in rural areas, and many schools and health centres do not have improved sanitation facilities. Where there is an improvement, it often does not meet the minimum standards for being considered as sustainable in a way, therefore upkeep is difficult. In terms of sanitation, there is a strong focus on eradicating open defecation, which has many negative impacts on women in terms of insecurity as well as the lack of access to clean drinking water and sanitation improvements.

### Infrastructure Development Programme

The infrastructure development programme proposes raising the following considerations:

- Create demand for sanitation and create competition between communities' territories to promote on-site sanitation.
- Strengthen partnerships with public and private actors.
- Strengthen the implementation and operating capacities of public and private actors and create a culture of social responsibility for these actors.
- Strengthen financial frameworks.

These suggested strategies focus more on just the construction of new facilities and assure uptake of these new facilities to ensure their effectiveness. These strategies do not specifically


---
**Page 209**
---

mention the inclusion of women or men in different ways. The National Development Plan does, however, identify how improved governance will result from better consideration of gender and the environment. A strong institutional framework, including a dedicated WASH sector policy and regulatory framework will be necessary, as well the development of the skills of actors who apply it in plan, project and programme implementation. In addition to these efforts, there is also a need for women to include them in the employment sector, boosted by the improvements in water and sanitation infrastructure. Women and girls' education levels are low, however, with only 14% of girls completing secondary school (2016) and 8.5% of women enrolling in tertiary education. [1]

### Stakeholder Mapping

In Ivory Coast, the Ministry of Hydraulics, Sanitation and Hygiene has primary responsibility for WASH. Other National Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of the Environment of Urban Sanitation, which is responsible for adaptation to climate change, and the Ministry of Health and Public Hygiene, leading on hygiene policy and programmes.

There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including local government authorities, water utilities, private sector companies, international NGOs, wide range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private sector and CSD engagement in WASH. ## 9.7 Kenya

### Water, sanitation and hygiene in Kenya at a glance

Access to water: **71.3%** of the population has access to an improved water source, of which **61.8%** has access to basic drinking water and **9.5%** has access to drinking water categorised as "limited". The remaining **28.8%** of the population uses unimproved water sources for drinking water, of which **13% only have access its surface water.**

Access to sanitation: **40.7%** of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which **32.7%** has access to basic sanitation services and **25.5%** has access to limited sanitation services. The remaining **41.8%** of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, with **8.5%** practicing open defecation.

Hygiene: **26.8%** of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.

WASH in health facilities: **78%** of health care facilities have basic facilities for drinking water. Data is not available on sanitation and hygiene facilities in health facilities.

Data is not available for WASH in schools.

[2] Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020)

[3] Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020)

Policy context

The drive to advance livelihoods in Kenya is laid out through the four key areas, dubbed the Big Four Agenda, within the National Development plan, named the Kenya Vision 2030 (2008–2030). These include food security, universal access to quality education, universal health coverage, and environmental sanitation and hygiene continue to be a key sub-focus area within the Social Pillar outlined in the Third Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) Strategy Paper (2015–2030) and the Kenya National Development Plan of 2030. Within this plan it sees environmental degradation, climate change, inadequate infrastructure and lack of water conservation strategies as hindrances to the development of a clean, safe environment for Kenyans. The policy acknowledges that these challenges are interlinked with each other; however, these challenges come from capacity building, public participation, adoption of innovative technical approaches, and the need for political will.

While this policy does not discuss how inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene facilities and practices impact women and girls in Kenya, it does highlight the importance of gender equality, including under-employment and skills mismatch, and the implementation of the two-thirds gender rule that ensures that no more than 66% of one gender is represented in the national assembly and senate.

The Kenya Environmental Sanitation & Hygiene Policy (KESHAP) 2016, launched two years before the MFP it, outlines a people-centred approach to sanitation. The policy highlights key issues such as rapid urbanization and poverty, which overburden water supply and sanitation infrastructure at risk of rape and domestic violence against girls due to inadequate sanitation facilities. To address these issues, the Policy intends to scale up access to improved rural and urban sanitation services (Strategic Objective 1) and improve the quality of sanitation services (Strategy 5). The policy discusses an increase in education, training and skill development for sanitation workers and service providers (Strategic Objective 2), as well as the establishment of a network of sanitation facilities, though it does not suggest these efforts are focused on any one gender/sex.

While the Policy discusses the importance of improved facilities, it does not discuss the negative impacts inflicted by climate change. However, the Ministry of Water and Sanitation (MOWAS) Strategic Plan 2015–2030 highlights that Kenya is vulnerable to climate change due to its geographical location and intensity of precipitation leading to an increase in natural disasters; it also highlights the decrease in available water resources as a result of climate change. The policy discusses the need for improved water infrastructure on focusing on water resources and management, and the impact that inadequate water and sanitation infrastructure and management have on the health of the population. In order to combat these issues, the Ministry of Water and Sanitation (MOWAS) has partnered with Water and Sanitation Coverage (KRA 4 and 5), Human Resource Capacity (KRA 6), and Research and Technology (KRA 7). The policy also highlights that there are many opportunities for business development opportunities and handling the adverse effects of climate change, though there is no clear discussion on a focus of women and girls.

The National Policy on Water and Development (NPWD) elaborates on the burden women and girls face due to water resource deficits, with the need to travel long distances to collect water for domestic use, which affects girls' attendance at school and women's opportunities for income-generating activities. The NPWD seeks to reduce these burdens by ensuring the availability of water for multiple


---
**Page 212**
---

users to women and to develop and review the environment management laws to ensure gender and equity compliance.

With climate change being a major discussion in most of the policies, the National Climate Change Policy recognizes that increases in weather extremities, including droughts, floods and water salination greatly impacts the water, sanitation and hygiene sector, with the biggest focus being on inadequate sanitation systems which lead to clogged drains and water contamination. The Ministry of Water and Irrigation has therefore developed a number of policies and strategies aimed at ensuring safe water and sanitation by strengthening capacity building in vulnerable communities.

Interventions throughout these policies is the continual discussion on the inclusion of equal opportunities for women and girls, particularly in the areas of education, participation in the management of water and sanitation projects/programmes and the full participation of female leaders on community-led committees through gender mainstreaming. While most policies include opportunities for women’s empowerment, there are still significant gaps to be addressed; with only 9.3% enrolment of women in tertiary education[1], there continues to be a gap as highly skilled job opportunities are not available to women.

The Ministry of Education and Sports has developed the National Education Sector Strategic Plan which includes training programmes for artisans and operators in sanitation and job creation in areas such as labour-intensive construction, sustainable livelihoods and long-term entrepreneurial activities. Finally, the Kenya National Curriculum Development Centre (KNCDC) has included gender and STEM based courses.


Whilst the KESHIP and MOHNS Strategic plans also discuss the impact of inadequate water, sanitation and health infrastructure on children, the National Education Sector Strategic Plan and the National Health Policy examine the importance of safe water, sanitation and hygiene within school and health facilities. Within schools, girls tend to be more affected by inadequate WASH facilities, and this impacts their attendance and learning outcomes as well as increasing their vulnerability to gender-based violence. To limit the impact on children’s schooling, the National Education Sector Strategic Plan includes a number of interventions such as provision of safe and clean drinking water in schools and separate sanitation facilities for boys and girls.


As the main users of primary health centres, poor WASH facilities in health centres disproportionately affects women and girls. The National Health Policy seeks to improve their WASH facilities to limit water borne diseases and reduce the burden on women and girls through pregnancy, childbirth and implementation of programmes to minimise impacts on women and girls.

Stakeholder Mapping

In Kenya, the Ministry of Water and Irrigation has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for sanitation and hygiene provision and policy; and the Ministry of Environment and Forestry, who set environmental standards and regulations. The Housing and Urban Development (HUDA) ministry is a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including local water boards, urban water utilities and rural water supply schemes. At the national level, the National CSD, a wide range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private sector and CSD engagement in WASH. Figure 0 provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

# Namibia

## Water, sanitation and hygiene in Namibia at a glance

Access to drinking water: 91.4% of the population has access to an improved source of drinking water, or 85.2% have access to piped water (urban) or standpipe (rural). The remainder of the population relies on unimproved water sources, with 4.9% of the population relying on surface water and 3.7% relying on groundwater.

Access to sanitation: 48.1% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 35.3% has access to basic sanitation services and 12.8% has access to limited sanitation services. 47.2% of the population continues to practice open defecation while 4.8% of the population uses unimproved sanitation facilities.

Data on hygiene: Data on the proportion of the population with access to safely managed sanitation services is not available.

Data on hygiene, WASH in schools and WASH in health facilities is not available.


## Policy context

Namibia's 5th National Development Plan (NDPS) focuses on the period 2017/18–2021/22 and charts a course toward the cumulative targets outlined in Namibia’s Vision 2030. The plan is organised around four key pillars for achieving sustainable development, namely Economic Prosperity, Social Transformation, Environmental Sustainability and Governance. These are supported by five 'game changers' that will move Namibia from a reactive, input-based economy towards a proactive, high-performing economy.

The plan looks at water and sanitation as two separate sectors—rather than looking at water and sanitation as combined WASH items. As such, analysis of the challenges faced in the water and sanitation sectors fall within two separate pillars of NDPS. Discussion of the water sector falls under the Economic Prosperity pillar while discussion of the sanitation sector falls under the game changer selection 'Increase investments in infrastructure development'. The plan highlights key issues around safe water access in the water sector, including:

- Availability of water and reliable water supply.
- Increasing water demand for economic activities and urban consumption.
- Water demand from the agriculture sector for irrigation.
- Provision and financing of water infrastructure such as desalination plants in coastal areas.
- Improving management of existing water sources.


---
**Page 215**
---

# Discussion of issues related to sanitation and hygiene fall under the Social Transformation pillar of the policy, under the 'social development' subsection.

The key issues in the sanitation sector that the 5th NDP highlights include:

- Accelerating improved sanitation systems in rural areas.
- Promoting coordinated investment on sanitation infrastructure.
- Strengthening advocacy to tackle sanitation challenges.


While NDPS summarizes the key challenges of safe water supply and sanitation services access, particularly for women and children, the Water and Sanitation Policy (WSP) goes into much more detail on the challenges themselves, the context, the health consequences and Namibia's policy objectives in response to these challenges.

The Water and Sanitation Policy states that essential water supply and sanitation services should be available to all Namibians; that the improvement of water and sanitation services should be achieved equitably; that communities should have the right to determine which water and sanitation services are provided within their area, and that utilization of water resources should be environmentally sustainable. NDPS then translates these broad policy objectives into actionable strategies as part of NDPS's results framework.

As with the NDPs, the Water and Sanitation Policy deals with water supply and sanitation in isolation, concentrating on the provision of safe drinking water and sanitation services to all Namibians within the water sector, as well as provision of rural and urban sanitation services in the sanitation sector. Namibia has indicated its commitment to responding to climate change challenges through its National Climate Change Strategy Action Plan (NCCSAP) that was finalized in 2015 for the period 2013–2030.

Like the NDPs and the Water and Sanitation Policy, Namibia's 2014 National Policy on Climate Change also analyzes the issues of water supply and provision of sanitation services but focuses much more heavily on the impact of climate change on both sectors, as well as the potential health consequences. The policy argues that climate change will have a significant impact on water availability in Namibia due to unpredictable rainfall patterns being worsened by climate change. The policy argues that this in turn will have a significant impact on water supply and sanitation.

Namibia ratified the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 1995 and thus became a party to the UNFCCC. Since then, Namibia has submitted communications, completing national communications in 2002, 2013 and 2015. Namibia's Fourth National Communication to the UNFCCC, submitted in 2020, covers the policy framework of the NPPC and summarizes progress against the NCCSAP. The policy argues that climate change will have a significant impact on water availability in Namibia relies on rainfall as its 'natural water source' as well as dams, ephemeral rivers and aquifers. In the absence of rain, these sources are likely to dry up or become unreliable. Evaporation rates coupled with the country's semi-arid climate means that Namibia is now at a water deficit.

While NDPS, the Water and Sanitation Policy, NPPC, the National Policy on Climate Change and the Fourth Communication to the UNFCCC all reference gender as a cross-cutting issue in the water and sanitation sectors, they do not specifically address how women and children are being impacted nor the roles that these groups can play in these sectors. In contrast, Namibia's National


---
**Page 216**
---

Gender Policy explicitly tackles the gender issues affecting the water and sanitation sectors. The policy argues that women continue to be under-represented in policy and decision-making forums on the utilization of water resources and access to sanitation facilities. It also explores the roles that women play within the water and sanitation sectors. For example, in most rural communities, women and girls constitute 75% of the workforce responsible for fetching water. Existing infrastructure is often not designed with women's needs in mind; only about 10% of boreholes compared to 15% which have fensh toilets.

In addition, the policy explores the health inequalities related to water and sanitation. As defined in the other document, this includes access to improved infrastructure with which to provide people, especially rural populations, with access to water and sanitation facilities. A lack of these basic amenities has a detrimental effect on the health of rural communities, with diseases linked to water and sanitation being more prevalent among women than men. For example, in Namibia, it is estimated that more than men live in rural areas and female-headed households tend to be poorer than male-headed households; a lack of rural water and sanitation infrastructure disproportionately affects women and female-headed households.

There is some discussion of women's education on water and sanitation sector issues in the National Policy on Climate Change for Namibia. The policy states that the government will include gender and climate change considerations into all national policies, strategies, plans, and programmes as well as capacity building programmes. However, the policy stops short of discussing women's positioning for formal employment in the water and sanitation sectors. Currently, the completion rate for upper secondary education is 30% compared to 52% for males. The policy also does not mention any strategy for WASH literacy policies that position women for employment in the sector to be successful.

All of the above policies are very limited in their consideration of women's employment levels in the water and sanitation sectors, which are considered key sectors for poverty reduction and economic growth in Namibia. However, the National Gender Policy (2010-2020) does put forward that, as a strategy for gender mainstreaming, women should be involved in interventions to achieve the objectives of environmental sustainability, including in the water and sanitation sectors. The policy stops short of discussing how this engagement could take place.

Stakeholder Mapping

In Namibia, the Ministry of Agriculture, Water and Land Reform has primary responsibility for WASH. Other National Ministries with some WASH responsibility include Ministry of Urban and Rural Development, Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Health and Social Services, and Ministry of Environment and Tourism. The Ministry of Health and Social Services, implementing and enforcing hygiene practices. There are a range of other stakeholders involved in the delivery of WASH services at district level including district-level government agencies and traditional bodies, the private sector, national and international CSOs. A wide range of development partners are involved in supporting government, private sector and CSO engagement in WASH. Figure E provides a visual mapping of the actors involved.

## Nigeria

### Water, sanitation and hygiene in Nigeria at a glance

**9.9 Nigeria**

Page 106 of 143


---
**Page 218**
---

Access to drinking water: ~78% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which 31.7% has access to safely managed drinking water; 55.8% has access to a basic level of drinking water and 5% has access to a limited level of drinking water. The remainder of the population uses unimproved sources for drinking water, including 5.6% who only have access to surface water, 10.2% who use groundwater from dug wells or hand-dug pits, and 34.7% who are still ~32.7% of the population, with improved drinking sources, 30 mg or less to collect water.

Access to sanitation: 64% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 30.1% have access to safely managed sanitation service while 59.8% have a basic level of sanitation service while 19.5% has access to a limited level of sanitation service. 19.3% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 18.7% who practice open defecation.

Hygiene: 33.2% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.

WASH in schools: Nationally, 36.4% of schools have a basic service for drinking water; 37.8% have a basic service for sanitation and 27.5% have a basic service in hygiene.

WASH in health facilities: 44.0% of health care facilities have a basic service for drinking water, 17.7% have a basic service for sanitation and 69.3% have a basic service in hygiene; 45.8% of health care facilities have basic service for hygiene.

Policy context

As Nigeria continues to experience rapid urbanisation and growth, it seeks to improve infrastructure throughout the country to provide development opportunities, establish a strong foundation for a diversified economy and alleviate poverty. One of the main ways they aspire to achieve this through social development is by improving access to water supply, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) services and employment job creation, as outlined in the Mid-Report of the National Development Plan (2021). The MDG Report also highlights that Nigeria has made progress towards achieving the MDGs but are still reviewing continued challenges. Challenges identified include ensuring economic diversification, private sector investment in WASH services, lack of coordination and communal disputes, negative impacts on the environment, poor water quality, inadequate financing for WASH projects, poor operation and maintenance culture. The report also touches on the importance of WASH in light of the COVID-19 pandemic. Some of the main ways it seeks to overcome these challenges is by continuing to leverage the MDG Report as a guide for policy formulation and implementation, adopting an integrated approach and policy formulation approach, while also increasing training, developing the bioeconomy and circular economy, and increasing access to finance and technical support for businesses and projects in environmentally sustainable sectors.


[Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020)](https://www.un.org/waterforlifedecade/programmes/joint-programme-for-water-supply-sanitation-and-hygiene)

[Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020)](https://www.un.org/waterforlifedecade/programmes/joint-programme-for-water-supply-sanitation-and-hygiene)

[Joint Programme for Water Supply, Sanitation and Hygiene (2020)](https://www.un.org/waterforlifedecade/programmes/joint-programme-for-water-supply-sanitation-and-hygiene)

One recurring theme in the National Development Plan is the increase in negative impacts on Nigeria’s development brought about by climate change. In order to address this increasing threat, the National Climate Change Policy was developed and launched in 2011. The policy addresses these challenges through ten strategic objectives, including implementing adaptation and mitigation measures that promote low-carbon development (Strategy 1); mainstreaming gender, youth and other vulnerable groups into all aspects of climate change planning and implementation; and the promotion of construction and infrastructural development (Strategy 6). This policy examines the impact that extreme weather events will have on Nigeria’s economy. It also addresses the need for an increase in droughts and floods. Nigeria seeks to overcome issues related to climate change and inadequate water and waste infrastructure through the implementation of a circular economy, as well as increasing investment in water supply systems, including planning, design, construction, management, operation and maintenance of water supply and sanitation schemes and services. The policy recognises the importance of including women in the planning, implementation and management of water resources and sanitation services. This is to ensure that investments are made environmentally friendly and gender responsive technologies, and the development of gender-responsive, socially inclusive and rural water and sanitation infrastructure.

One of the biggest challenges facing Nigeria faces with implementing adequate WASH facilities and ensuring access to the 3-tiered government structure which leads to a lack of coordination in policy and programme implementation as discussed in Nigeria Water and Sanitation Profile (p.10).

In all the policies reviewed, there was a clear theme around capacity building and job creation. Within the Climate Change Policy, there is discussion on collaboration with the Federal Ministry of Women Affairs to ensure equitable policies and programmes. This discussion seeks to encourage women’s participation in the planning and management of climate change programmes. This includes investment in education and training for women to increase their skills and knowledge base to sustain jobs, employment, economic growth and social stability. With 66.8% of women completing upper secondary or higher education compared with 75.2% of men having completed tertiary education, there continues to be discrepancies in access to some highly skilled careers in WASH.

While most policies reviewed discussed the importance of WASH and ensuring water security in the country, the National Gender Policy of 2006 looks at the importance of including women in the work force and actively participating in decision-making. The policy calls for equal opportunities during implementation and management of all programmes and projects. It also highlights the key role women play in water, sanitation and hygiene as women are usually the collectors of water and are in charge of managing household water supplies. Women are disproportionately affected by the ones greatly impacted by the consequences of unsafe drinking water. This health risk is highlighted further in the National Health Policy which recognises that women and children face higher mortality and morbidity rates from poor access to safe drinking water, sanitation and hygiene services and sanitation. Although not specifically related to WASH, both policies emphasise the importance of the equal participation of women and increased education and awareness. They also encourage community engagement in best practices.

In Nigeria, the Ministry of Water Resources has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include The Federal Ministry on Water Resources, a water resources agency that is responsible to the Federal Ministry of Environment and Ministry of Health. These roles formulate community sanitation and hygiene. There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including state-level government agencies, national government agencies, local governments, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and private sector and CSG engagement in WASH.

9.10 Morocco

Water, sanitation and hygiene in Morocco at a glance

Access to drinking water: 90% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which 79.9% has access to safely managed drinking water, 10.5% has access to a basic level of drinking water service while only 3.6% have no access at all. 84% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services for drinking water, including 1.5% who only have access to surface water.

Access to sanitation: 89% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 39.3% has access to safely managed sanitation service, 47.9% has access to a basic level of sanitation service while 1.5% has access to a limited level of sanitation service. 93.6% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 1.7% who practice open defecation.

Data on hygiene and WASH in health facilities is not available.

WASH in schools: Nationally, 83.4% of schools have a basic service for drinking water, 69.9% has a basic service for sanitation and 85% has a basic service for hygiene.

Policy context

Due to continuing threats to available water resources throughout the country, the National Sustainable Development Policy 2017 (NSDP) recognizes the importance of water, sanitation and hygiene improvements for the development of the country, as outlined in Strategic Focus 6: ensure the conservation and rational management of water resources. Strategic Focus 14: promotion of integrated water resources management is also important to meet the needs of the population while meeting the needs (by ensuring needs are met) and strengthen water resources integrated management. Not only does it identify the need for a more efficient use of water, but it also identifies the opportunities available for transitioning towards a circular economy through recycling water usage. It also examines the need for human development and combat social inequalities within Stake 6: gender equality. The National Sustainable Development Policy 2017 (NSDP) touches briefly on the importance of equity for all, it remains quite gender blind with regard to the incorporation of gender in policies. The NSDP further connects the importance of fighting climate change or adapting to its effects and the need to fight poverty and social inequality within the country, especially in regard to discrepancies between regions within the country.

Moroccan Climate Change Policy 2014 highlights the impact of climate change on health and water access in the country. The increase in temperatures and extreme weather is leading to salinization of water, decreasing its quality and quantity. Women are more likely than men to be responsible for managing water. It also points to the importance of


---
**Page 222**
---

increasing financing and programmes focused on combating climate change, including education and awareness on alternative solutions and the re-use of water resources.

While the Climate Change Policy touches on the importance of tackling climate change to ensure equitable access to water, sanitation and hygiene, the National Water Plan (2013) examines the current situation in Morocco. The plan identifies several challenges: a high rate of evaporation in the Sahara depression and the anticipations of the Azores leading to greater extremities when weather patterns change; the lack of rainfall in some regions; the increase in water demand due to population growth, a decrease in the quality of surface water, negative impacts from climate change and water contamination. Morocco has placed water as an important factor in its development. It outlines the importance of mobilizing water resources through the construction of dams, reservoirs, and other infrastructure for drinking water treatment and reusing wastewater. It also sets out the need to protect communities against floods, especially for sanitation facilities and agricultural drainage systems. Within the health policy that was reviewed as part of the National Water Plan (2013), the document highlights the increase in water-borne diseases and the need to ensure adequate facilities.

Within all these policies, there is no clear examination of how to increase job opportunities for women. However, the National Sustainable Development Plan discusses increasing employment opportunities more generally by promoting gender equality and empowering women through education and training with resource consumption (such as organizing the flow of water and waste) and adapting training to meet the transformational needs of the sector. The plan also mentions that the Water Plan is in line with what was outlined within all sectors as outlined in the National Strategy for Gender Equity & Equal 2006. The Strategy sees the value of ensuring there is equal participation of women and men in communities, especially around decision-making processes. It recognizes that women are more vulnerable to climate change effects place on women, such as leaving them more vulnerable to illness; it also discusses the importance of equal access to education. With only 29% of females completing upper secondary** and 41.7% of women entering higher education, there is a need for greater investment in education and training to build the skills needed for certain WASH-related careers. The Climate Change Policy discusses the importance of capacity building as part of its strategy to adapt to climate change. It also mentions that waste enterprises be better able to mitigate climate change; it seeks to do so by strengthening human and technical capacities of stakeholders, taking public action to take ownership of the vision, concepts and strategies for adaptation and mitigation, and promoting awareness raising on how to combat climate change and strengthen STEM and climate-related education and training. Additionally, the Water Plan discusses the strengthening of capacity building, including skill building for highly skilled positions, through the development of a national strategy for water resources management, and advancement of techniques, construction and management adapted to a national context.

Stakeholder Mapping

In Morocco, the Ministry of Interior has primary responsibility for WASH. Other National Ministries with some WASH responsibility include The General Directorate of Hydraulics, the lead agency for water resource planning, and the Ministry of Health, responsible for drinking water quality control and water-related laws. There are a range of agencies involved in the delivery of WASH services at the

# Climate Fund Managers

## WASH stakeholders in Morocco

### The Ministry of Interior
- Involved in water management through the Water and Irrigation Department (DAG) and the General Directorate of Local Authorities (DGCL).
- The DAG has a mandate to manage drinking water issues.
- The DGCL supervises municipalities and provides technical assistance.

### Other key government ministries
- Ministry of Agriculture: Responsible for drinking water quality control and has an important role in the development of irrigation systems, which are crucial for public utilities and the cultivation of crops, such as rice and wheat.
- Ministry of Women, Family, and Social Integration: Focuses on policy-making and implementing gender issues.

### Other government institutions
- National Office for Electricity and Potable Water (ONEE): Manages water resources in urban areas and produces and national rural water supplies. The ONEE and its subsidiaries are autonomous entities.

### Local authorities
- Responsible for the management of drinking water services, including the assignment to public or autonomous commercial utilities.
- The Ministry of Interior and the Ministry of Finance supervise the regional and local authorities responsible for the provision of urban water services.
- There are 13 regional authorities, which are supervised by the Ministry of Interior and the Ministry of Finance. These authorities manage a total number of subscribers in urban water services.

### Non-State Actors
- Private companies: The private concessionaires manage water distribution and supply to households and businesses.
- Local associations: The Moroccan Association for Water Supply and Sanitation (RAMAS) is involved with WASH until it is a Water User association, meanwhile, unions/communities are represented by the General Confederation of Moroccan Workers (CGTM).

### Community level
- Water User associations, townhalls, unions/communities.

### Development Partners
- UN agencies: UNESCO, UNDP, UNICEF, WHO.

# 9.11 Uganda

## Water, sanitation and hygiene in Uganda at a glance

Access to drinking water: 83.4% of the population has access to an improved water source for drinking water, of which 16.6% has access to safely managed drinking water; 39.2% has access to a basic level of drinking water and 27.3% has access to a limited level of drinking water. The remainder of the population uses unimproved sources for drinking water, including 4.5% who only have access to surface water.[1]

Access to sanitation: 37.8% of the population has access to improved sanitation services, of which 29.6% has access to a basic level of sanitation service while 17.0% has access to a limited level of sanitation service. 62.6% of the population uses unimproved sanitation services, including 4.7% who practice open defecation.[1]

Hygiene: 22.8% of the population has access to handwashing facilities with soap and water.[1]

WASH in schools: Nationally, 68.4% of schools have a basic service for drinking water; 79.6% has a basic service for sanitation and 80.1% has a basic service for hygiene.[2]

WASH in health facilities: 44.3% of health care facilities have a basic service for drinking water. Data is not available on sanitation and hygiene facilities in health facilities.[2]

## Policy context

The shift away from a focus on poverty reduction to one of wealth creation through industrial development, as outlined in Uganda’s National Development Plan III (2020–2025), has resulted in decreased attention to water, sanitation and hygiene in national policy. Nevertheless, water, sanitation and hygiene remain a core area of Uganda’s Human Development Programme, a sub-programme in the NDP III, which aims to improve access to safe drinking water, sanitation and hygiene services for increased competitiveness and better quality of life for all, with the following targets having been set:

- Increased access to safe water supply from 70 to 83% (rural) and from 74 percent to 100% (urban)
- Increased access to basic sanitation from (improved toilet) 19 to 40% and hand washing from 34 to 50%.

In addition, the NDP III also foresees investing in the effective management of the entire WASH value chain segments, including containment, emptying, transportation, treatment, safe reuse and disposal.

The NDP III recognizes that achievement of these, and other development targets, will be more challenging due to the effects of climate change, with more extreme weather patterns leading to


---
**Page 225**
---

increased water stress, impacting water availability and quality for both domestic and commercial uses as well as the water and sanitation infrastructure already in place. The Uganda National Climate Change Policy (2013) recognizes that women play a critical role in climate change adaptation and mitigation actions, particularly in relation to water resources. This policy commits to mainstreaming gender issues across climate change adaptation and mitigation actions, including increasing access to water and sanitation facilities for women and girls, and developing a gender-sensitive approach in monitoring and evaluating systems.

The National Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy (2018) launched in 2018 (three years earlier than the National Climate Change Policy), aims to address the disproportionate impact of climate change on impoverished women, boys, girls and vulnerable groups through ensuring equity in access and control of resources in the water and sanitation sub-sector. The Strategy highlights the burden and risks for women and girls as a result of their responsibilities related to water collection and sanitation. On average, women and girls spend up to six hours a day fetching water, limiting their time for economic activities and attending school; that women and girls face shame and risk of attack as a result of inadequate access to safe sanitation facilities in their communities; and that women and girls suffer from diseases such as diarrhoea, dysentery and cholera as a result of their domestic responsibilities. It does not, however, recognize the negative implications climate change is likely to have on the burden and risks women and girls face.

The Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy has five strategic objectives:

1. Integrate gender into all government policy, guidelines, plans and budgets.
2. Build the capacity of the Ministry of Water and Environment as well as other stakeholders to use a women’s rights-based approach in water resources management, hygiene systems and to promote a gender-sensitive work environment at the Ministry of Water and Environment;
3. Economically empower men, women, boys and girls and vulnerable groups, especially those with disabilities, through access to services such as water/sanitation, basic sanitation and hygiene;
4. Collection and reporting of gender disaggregated data, with attention to gender issues in monitoring and reporting;
5. Building partnerships across the sub-sector: Line Ministries, Civil Society, districts and Local Governments, towards the gender agenda.
6. Gender coordination, partnership and networking.

The most direct benefits to women and girls are likely to come through strategic objective 3, which foresees the rehabilitation and expansion of water and sanitation facilities in rural and urban locations, as well as providing training for men, women, boys and girls on income-generating opportunities associated with development of water and sanitation facilities.

This policy and other policies express a commitment to stakeholder participation in the planning, implementation and maintenance of water and sanitation projects, with the National Water Policy (1999) setting aside at least half a hectare of land for each project. This commitment provides opportunities for women and girls to influence the development of water and sanitation infrastructure in their communities; they bring an additional time burden which


---
**Page 226**
---

Page 123

CLIMATE FUND MANAGERS


women and girls may struggle to take on. Social traditions which encourage women and girls to defer to men may inhibit women and girls' abilities to participate further.

Whilst the Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy does not identify inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH) facilities as a gender issue, it is clear that WASH is an issue; the Second National Health Policy and the National Education and Sports Strategic Plan do. As the main users of primary health centres, women and girls can be severely affected by inadequate water, sanitation and hygiene practices in these facilities. This is particularly true for adolescent girls who are more likely to drop out of school, especially during menstruation. With this in mind, the Education and Sports Sector Plan includes a target that 90% of all primary schools will have access to safe drinking water infrastructure by government supported primary schools as well as the construction of sanitation facilities primarily in primary schools. The National Health Policy also foresees improvements in health infrastructure and a range of other measures.

The Water and Sanitation Gender Strategy recognises the importance of building the capacity of Ministry of Water and Education staff on gender and or encouraging a gender-sensitive work environment, but stops short from promoting actions to increase women's participation in formal roles in these institutions. The National Development Plan II outlines clear plans for expanding education provision in Uganda, including the construction of new schools and the expansion of existing facilities into underserved areas, but places no emphasis on encouraging women specifically into education and careers in these areas. Currently the proportion of women who have completed upper secondary school or enrolled in tertiary education remains low, with 20% and 43.5% respectively.

Stakeholder Mapping

In Uganda, the Ministry of Water and Environment has primary responsibility for WASH. Other national Ministries with some WASH responsibility include the Ministry of Health, responsible for sanitation and hygiene, as well as WASH in health care facilities, and the Ministry of Education and Sports, which is responsible for education infrastructure and water provision. The delivery of WASH services at the sub-national levels, including district-level government agencies, the private sector and civil society organisations (CSOs), is a key challenge to achieving progress in supporting government, private sector and CSO engagement in WASH. # Ministry of Water and Development (MWD)
**Key Roles:** Leads efforts for water sector development, including infrastructure projects, capacity building, and policy formulation for their development. Departments include among others: National Environment Agency, National Climate Change Division, National Water and Environmental Sanitation Commission, and works with a mandate on gender and social inclusion.

## Other key government institutions
- Ministry of Local Government: supports local governments in the implementation of water sector policies.
- Ministry of Gender and Social Inclusion: facilitates the integration of gender perspectives into all aspects of water sector development.
- Ministry of Finance, Planning, and Economics: provides financial support for water sector projects.
- Development Partners: includes the World Bank, IFC, IDA, ADB, Asian Development Bank (ADB), Global Environment Facility (GEF), Climate Change Trust Fund (CCTF), UNDP, USAID, SIDA, Austrian Development Agency, French Government, UN agencies e.g. UNESCO, UNICEF, WHO, FAO, JICA, JAPAD, WFP).

## District-level Government Facilitators
- Local Governments: identify issues, sub-national District Water Office, Directorate of health services (sanitation and hygiene), Water and Sanitation Development Project (WASDEP) project in small towns and rural centers. Water Management Zones: monitors water resources, provides technical support to communities, and implements projects.
- Technical Support Units: links MWD with Local Government, sub-county government, and Irrigation Board units (links with Water for Production Regional Centers).

## Non-State Actors
- International CSOs: Caritas, Save the Children, Mercy Corps, World Union Cooperation & Development, International Rescue Committee (IRC), Water for People for Innovation.
- Local CSOs: Action for Women Empowerment, Action for Human Rights and Environment, Community Based Organizations (CBOs).
- Private Operators: association of private water districts, entrepreneurs that provide services to communities, and associations of researchers, academics, and media.

## Community Level
- Community Units within CSOs: Water User Committees/Associations, Catchment based Management Units, Women’s Groups, Youth Groups, Community-based Organizations (CBOs), households, (citizens) consumers, men, children and youth, including vulnerable groups.

## Development Partners
- World Bank, IFC, IDA, ADB, The Foreign, Commonwealth & Development Office (FCDO), Global Environment Facility (GEF), Climate Change Trust Fund (CCTF), UNDP, USAID, SIDA, Austrian Development Agency, French Government, UN agencies e.g. UNESCO, UNICEF, WHO, FAO, JICA, JAPAD, WFP).

# Ministry of Health (MoH)
**Key Roles:** The local ministry for sanitation and hygiene services, including the provision of water supply and sanitation facilities, and the promotion of hygiene practices. It is responsible for the implementation of the National Sanitation and Hygiene Policy, the National Water Supply and Sanitation Sector Development Strategy, and the National Action Plan on Drinking Water and Sanitation. The MoH also monitors the performance of service providers in public places and WASH is responsible in schools.

## Government Stakeholders at the Local Level
- Local Governments: District Water Office, Directorate of Health Services (Sanitation and Hygiene), Water and Sanitation Development Project (WASDEP) project in small towns and rural centers. Water Management Zones: monitors water resources, provides technical support to communities, and implements projects.
- Technical Support Units: links MoH with Local Government, sub-county government, and Irrigation Board units (links with Water for Production Regional Centers).

# Community Level
- Community Units within CSOs: Water User Committees/Associations, Catchment based Management Units, Women’s Groups, Youth Groups, Community-based Organizations (CBOs), households, (citizens) consumers, men, children and youth, including vulnerable groups.

# Annex 6: Gender Action Plan

## Introduction

This document sets out Climate Fund Managers (CFM) action plan for promoting gender equality in water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH), protection and management of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, and climate resilient port infrastructure through investments by the Climate Investor Two (CIT2) fund. CFM expects not only to mitigate the risks for women and men associated with funded projects, but also to benefit from gender equality in the long term. The document identifies opportunities that are exacerbated by climate change, and promoting sustainable and equitable development.

CFM is committed to promoting gender equality and women’s empowerment externally (in countries and communities where CFM’s Managed Vehicles are invested) and internally, within CFM itself. It does so through two main approaches: first, by ensuring that gender equality considerations inform its investments;[1] and secondly, by putting in place organisational systems to promote gender equality within CFM. The measures relating to the former are described as CFM’s Environmental and Social Management System.

The development of this Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been guided by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) approach to gender mainstreaming (Figure 1). It has been informed by a Gender Assessment which comprises:

i) A review of global evidence in the areas of WASH, the protection of wetlands, coastal and marine ecosystems, and climate resilient port infrastructure;

ii) A review of national policies, strategies, plans and contexts, relating to WASH, gender and climate change for 15 GCF focal countries: Bangladesh, Botswana, Brazil, Djibouti, Indonesia, Kenya, Malawi, Mozambique, Namibia, Nigeria, Pakistan, Rwanda, Senegal, South Africa and Uganda;

iii) Stakeholder analysis for eight out of the 15 countries;[2]

iv) A problem tree analysis for each of the three areas, which, following GCF guidance, drew on the gender equality evidence and findings to unpack the causes and effects of the core problems identified; and

v) Identified opportunities for promoting gender equality through CFM investments, based on the review evidence.

The GAP presents how CFM will use C2T to take forward the opportunities for promoting gender equality identified in the Gender Assessment and provides a performance measurement framework which will be used to monitor progress. The GAP identifies 15 GCF focal countries[3] and financing investments which have yet to be identified, the actions included in the GAP are, by their very nature, project specific. Project-specific Goals, which will be developed during the project design phase, will allow this high-level framework to be tailored more directly.


[1] CFM Gender Policy 2021

[2] Underpinning the analysis is the systemic process of ensuring that women and men have equal access and control over resources, development benefits and decision-making at all stages of the development process through ensuring that the respective needs, interests and priorities are identified and addressed.

[3] It was not possible to conduct stakeholder analysis for the remaining 5 countries as No Objection Letters were received too late in the process.

# CLIMATE INVESTORS

## EFFECTS

Women and girls are not able to invest in empowerment activities such as going to school, income generating activities or other productive activities.

People, especially women and girls, have a poorer physical and mental health status than men.

Low women's education and skills will be hit the WRM/IKM sector hardest.

### ROOT CAUSES

Gender norms relegated women to the domestic sphere, limiting their ability to work outside, and distance they can stray from the home; women lack access to land and specialized equipment.

Women are mostly excluded from the coastal sector in oceans-based economies, which disproportionately negatively affects informal and unregulated economic activities.

Women are in less profitable values of marine claims.

### CAUSES

Climate change damages natural resources and marine ecosystems.

### EFFECTS

Design of community based management initiatives and lack of access to credit and account restrictions on women's accounts.

Low women participation in decision-making and management positions across relevant sectors.

### PURPOSE STATEMENT

WOMEN ARE MARGINALISED IN THE USE, PROTECTION AND MANAGEMENT OF WETLANDS, MARINE AND COASTAL ECOSYSTEMS.

At the same time, women are disproportionately affected by the different effects of damages to ecosystems. As a result, women face greater risks and additional barriers in adaptation and recovery efforts.

Women working in informal or unregulated economies are vulnerable to exploitation, including uncertainty about their rights and access to resources.

### EFFECTS

Policy- decision-making is gender-blind, overlooking women's needs, interests and potential roles in the sector.

In spite of particularly limited evidence on the intersect between gender, climate and port infrastructure, the Gender Assessment likewise identified widespread exclusion of women from the


---
**Page 232**
---

development and operation of port infrastructure, particularly from employment in operations and management. The Problem Tree Analysis below summarises the causes and effects of this situation (Figure 4).

### Figure 4: Resilient Port Infrastructure Problem Tree Analysis

| CAUSE | EFFECT |
| --- | --- |
| **ROOT CAUSE** | Gender norms dictate that port employment is overwhelmingly dominated by men |
| | Women comprise a fraction of recruits and employees in the port industry |
| | Female employees are particularly underrepresented in decision-making roles, training, and management |
| | There is a lack of support to the integration and empowerment of female employees through mentorship programmes |
| | Working environments are insufficient to accommodate the needs of female employees (e.g. by providing adequate sanitation facilities, family-friendly HR policies) |

### PURPOSE STATEMENT

**WOMEN ARE EXCLUDED FROM THE DEVELOPMENT AND OPERATION OF [CLIMATE-RESILIENT] PORT INFRASTRUCTURE**

There is a lack of evidence on the gender-differentiated effects of ports' regional impacts on ecosystems, as well as the differential benefits that women derive from port infrastructure development and operation. Women do not benefit equally from the employment benefits of port infrastructure development and operation, despite tending to be more willing to engage in sustainable or environmentally friendly practices.

### EFFECTS

- Policy and decision-making processes are dominated by men, with limited opportunities for women's voices and potential roles in the development of climate-resilient port infrastructure.
- Gender norms and inequalities are sustained.

These opportunities directly underpin the action areas presented below and in the action plan in Table 2. With this information as a foundation, the Gender Action Plan will be developed within C2M Project Companies and contractors, to facilitate the Gender Action Plan's full implementation.

Gender Action Areas for CI2

Improving women and girls' access to WASH
As far as possible, CFM and its Project Companies will use its investment projects and community development activities to remove the physical, financial, attitudinal and safety barriers that may limit women's and girls' access to WASH.

Using normal project development processes e.g., the Environment and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA), the Investment Project Company will identify the barriers to WASH. CFM will also explicitly consider the barriers which affect women’s and girls’ access to WASH, and how the investment design can address these. It will incorporate appropriate actions into the design of investment projects, where possible, and will include monitoring and evaluation (M&E) indicators and reporting frameworks to monitor progress. As part of this, CFM will consider the barriers faced by different groups of women and girls, including those with disabilities. The Investment Project Company will also consider the needs of women and girls with disabilities. CFM will also consider the specific needs some of these groups might have in accessing WASH facilities.


---
**Page 234**
---

# CLIMATE INVESTMENT FUND MANAGERS

CFM will seek to use community development projects to increase the direct benefits for women and girls resulting from investments, by addressing identified barriers which cannot be addressed through the main project.

- Working with families and communities to build their understanding of and support for good WASH practices, including during menstruation, so that families are willing to invest scarce resources in sanitation facilities. This will also help to ensure that women and girls continue to observe good hygiene practices.
- Targeting men is critical in such interventions as they often control the household budget.
- Modifying water points and toilet facilities so they can be used by women and girls with disabilities.
- Providing safe lighting in places used as shared water points or sanitation facilities.

It is recognised that improving WASH facilities in schools and health centres often brings significant benefits to women and girls. Opportunities for doing this will be considered as part of the investment project design, where appropriate.

Agreed objectives and actions to improve women's and girls' access to WASH, either through the investment project itself and/or through community development projects, will be set out in the project document. These will be reviewed by the Investment Committee (IC) at the time of the project’s results framework (see Action 4 below). For the investment projects, these objectives will also be reflected within the Project Implementation Document (PID) which will be reviewed as part of normal EPC performance review processes.

## Giving women a voice in the planning, design and management of facilities and initiatives

As a priority, beneficiary group for C2 investments, it is essential that stakeholder engagement and consultation processes service the needs and experiences of women and girls. This is to ensure that investment projects and community development projects can be responsive, as far as possible, to the needs of these groups.

Social norms in C2 focal countries often devalue and silence women's voices, even when public consultation mechanisms are in place. To counter this and to ensure women’s and girls’ needs inform investments, CFM will adopt a gender-responsive approach to stakeholder engagement which provides conducive spaces for women and girls to voice their needs and experiences. This process will:

- **Identifying women and girls as a distinct stakeholder group with whom to engage directly**, whilst recognising that, within this broad category, there are intersecting sub-groups with diverse needs and experiences who may need to be involved, e.g., indigenous women, women with disabilities and chronically poor women.
- At the community level, identifying women’s leaders to work with to convene appropriate fora to engage women and girls in the planning of facilities and initiatives.
- Working with community and male leaders to help them understand the rationale for engaging women in the planning of facilities and initiatives and build their support for that to avoid conflict.
- Holding consultation meetings at times and in venues which are convenient to the women and girls being targeted and considering how the direct and indirect costs of attendance can be offset, particularly for the chronically poor; and for women and girls with disabilities.


---
**Page 235**
---

# CLIMATE FUND MANAGERS

- Using consultation sessions with women and girls as an opportunity to inform the design of the grievance mechanism so that it is accessible and so that women and girls have confidence in its use.
- In some countries, where there are no formal grievance mechanisms in place, cases of gender-based violence and sexual harassment perpetrated by any staff or contractor from CFI, its Project Companies or contractors will be communicated.

In some countries, where women’s voices are not adequately represented on a committee with a role in the management of WASH facilities and ocean ecosystem protection initiatives. Where this is the case, CFM and its Project Companies will ensure that women have an opportunity to participate in these committees and provides space for women's voices to be heard.
The approach is likely to be quite content specific, but might involve:

- Identifying and recruiting to the committee local women who are perceived (by men and other women) as having a strong voice in society or on behalf of their community. These women are likely to have other leadership roles in their communities and may be willing to express publicly expressing their views.
- Building capacity for women’s representatives on the committee so that they can consult with a diverse range of women and girls in the community to get their feedback on the WASH facilities, and to represent these views in committee meetings and decision-making.
- Building mutual understanding between men and women about the need for engagement to be informed by women's and girls' experiences, and to develop skills to ensure women’s voices inform committee decision-making.

Planned activities will include ensuring women’s voices in the planning, design and management of facilities and initiatives will be set out in the investment’s stakeholder engagement plan. Routine monitoring of the plan’s implementation will give particular attention to actions taken to capture women's views on the project and to ensure that these are being responded to as feedback received.

### 3. Providing women with formal employment opportunities

Low levels of secondary education completion amongst girls in several of the OIC focal countries, combined with gender norms around acceptable productive roles for women and men, can make the formal employment of women in the WASH sector, as well as ocean-based industries and operation of port facilities, challenging.

Project Companies will be proactive in hiring a gender-balanced workforce, making adjustments to working practices where necessary. In some countries, there are significant male and female imbalances; it is therefore recognised that in several focal countries there are systemic issues beyond CFM’s control which are likely to make it difficult for women to enter the sector. However, it should be noted that in order to ameliorate this situation, CFM will scope out, in some focal countries, opportunities to encourage more women to enter the WASH sector. Options might include: providing scholarships for women embarking on studies in science, technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM) fields; providing work placements on projects for current female STEM students; or supporting women’s groups that are working with young women to provide them with information about career opportunities within the sector.

It is recognised that there may be limited formal employment opportunities for women in WASH enterprises and put in place appropriate mechanisms to recruit, train and support women in these roles. Opportunities might include: providing training on basic hygiene practices; or providing safe construction or cleaning, amongst others. It is recognised that there is limited experience of such approaches, and work done in this area through C2 will be done on a pilot basis with the intention of
